   463	## Example numbers by case
   464	
   465	The triggers in B1 and K3 are comparisons. The measurement decides them, so no number is fixed in advance. This
   466	section shows **what those comparisons have come out at elsewhere**, by agent type and condition, so a reader knows
   467	roughly where a project stands before measuring. These are examples with sources, not thresholds.
   468	
   469	Run: `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/`. Its source ids are:
   470	- `W-*`: web research (`nodes/web.md`);
   471	- `OC`: opencode's research (`nodes/opencode.md`);
   472	- `SZ`: the SEZA measurement (`nodes/seza.md`; every number with its command);
   473	- `CX`: codex's independent second look at SEZA, by agent type (`nodes/codex.md`);
   474	- `BM-*`, `S-F*`, `V-F*`, `G-F*`, `s3-F*`: as above.
   475	
   476	Web quotes are model-made extracts, not byte-checked, except W-1. Numbers marked *my arithmetic* are computed from a
   477	sourced figure.
   478	
   479	### The index read at session start (`INDEX.md`, `MEMORY.md`), by agent type
   480	
   481	| Agent | Window | Limit on files loaded at start | Measured effective context | Example |
   482	|---|---|---|---|---|
   483	| Claude Code (Claude 5.x) | 1M (W-8) | `MEMORY.md`: "first 200 lines … or the first 25KB, whichever comes first"; `CLAUDE.md`: target under 200 lines (W-1) | Opus 4.6, MRCR v2: 76% at 1M (W-14); 93% at 256K (W-15, reported) | 25 KB binds first: about 150 lines at 167 B per line (W-1), about 107 lines at SEZA's 233 B per line (*my arithmetic*, SZ) |
   484	| Codex (GPT-6.x) | 1.05M per the API (W-9), but this machine's Codex model cache says `context_window: 500000`, 95% effective, so about 475K (CX) | `AGENTS.md` combined up to 32 KiB (`project_doc_max_bytes`) (W-2) | GPT-5.4: 79.3% at 128K–256K, 36.6% at 512K–1M (W-15, reported) | instructions plus index under 32 KiB |
   485	| agy (Gemini 3.1 Pro / 3.8 Flash) | 1,048,576 (W-9, CX) | 24,000 bytes per rule file, **and** a 20,000-token budget across all rules (CX, official page); 12,000 characters in other reports (W-4, conflict) | 84.9% at 128K, 26.3% at 1M (W-15, reported) | under 12,000 characters if both readings must hold (*my reading*) |
   486	| opencode (MiniMax M3 / M2.7) | 1M / 204,800 (W-9) | no limit stated (W-6) | GAP | follow the strictest agent sharing the repo |
   487	| dsh (DeepSeek flash / V4 pro) | 1,048,576 (CX) | a 65,536-byte budget for the instruction files it loads (CX, from its loader docs) | GAP | instructions plus index under 64 KiB |
   488	| Qwen Code | — | its auto-memory index is capped at 200 lines or 25,000 bytes (`MAX_MANAGED_AUTO_MEMORY_INDEX_LINES` / `_BYTES`, CX); it also warns when always-loaded context passes 15% of the window, an advisory only (CX) | — | the index cap binds first: under 25,000 bytes, as for Claude |
   489	| Cursor | — | rules "under 500 lines" (W-3) | — | — |
   490	
   491	**Why the start load should stay small, whatever the window:**
   492	- Half of 17 models kept satisfactory performance only up to 32K (RULER, W-10).
   493	- 11 models fell below 50% of their baseline at 32K when the question and the answer share few words (NoLiMa, W-11).
   494	- Focused prompts of about 300 tokens beat full ones of about 113K (W-13).
   495	- Context files raised inference cost by over 20%, with no general success gain (W-17).
   496	- At 500 simultaneous instructions, the best model was 68% accurate (W-16).
   497	
   498	**Use case, SEZA (SZ, CX):**
   499	- The start load by agent, as estimates: Claude Code 61,993 B (about 15.4K tokens, including the global `CLAUDE.md`;
   500	  SZ measured 58,427 B without it); dsh 46,319 B (about 11.5K); Codex and agy 29,166 B each (about 7.2K) (CX). All are
   501	  well under 15% of every window above.
   502	- `MEMORY.md` is 52 lines and 12,108 B, within Claude's limit.
   503	- The 52 topic files (159,524 B) would not fit in 25 KB. That is why they are an index plus files read on demand.
   504	
   505	**When several agents share a project, the binding limit is the smallest one among them** (*my reading*).
   506	
   507	### B1.1: full-text against `INDEX.md` plus grep
   508	
   509	| Case | What was measured | Source |
   510	|---|---|---|
   511	| 1,144 docs, 1.7M tokens | the grep agent is more accurate (77.4 vs 74.7) but reads 226K tokens per question against BM25's 5.8K | W-22 |
   512	| 6,980 docs, 8.5M tokens | accuracy crossover: BM25 70.1 vs 69.9; BM25 ahead at every larger tier | W-22, S-F4 |
   513	| 511,959 docs, 600.8M tokens | BM25 50.5 vs the grep agent 30.7 | W-22 |
   514	| This repo's code and docs, 490 files, 4.8 MB | full-text prints about a sixth of grep's characters before naming a right file | BM-6 |
   515	| SEZA memory: the Claude memory folder (55 files) and a wider set of 257 files (2.5 MB) | grep takes 4–7 ms but prints from 361 B (a narrow term, memory folder) to 109,519 B, about 27K tokens (a broad term, 257-file set). Over the 257 files, a full-text proxy ranked the right file first on 3 of 5 questions and missed on 2 (n=5, one reader) | SZ |
   516	| A tool result too large to read | filtering in code instead of reading: 150,000 tokens down to 2,000 | OC (Anthropic, 2025-11-04) |
   517	
   518	**My reading:** on accuracy, grep stays competitive until millions of tokens. On **tokens read**, full-text wins much
   519	earlier: on SEZA-sized memory, a single broad term already floods grep's output. The B1.1 comparison measures exactly
   520	that.
   521	
   522	### B1.2: the vector index
   523	
   524	| Case | What was measured | Source |
   525	|---|---|---|
   526	| Loading about 115K tokens of history instead of retrieving | 30%–55% lower accuracy (GPT-4o, Llama 3.1) | W-18 |
   527	| Long context against retrieval, 100K–1M tokens | retrieval better for 3 of 4 models at 100K, and for all 4 from 500K | W-19 |
   528	| Vendor guidance | under about 200,000 tokens, the whole base can go in the prompt | V-F8 (2024) |
   529	| Dense against BM25, 1,144–511,959 docs | dense lowest at every tier in the table (7 tiers) | W-22 |
   530	| Enterprise corpus, 500K docs | vector 51.4 vs BM25 68.8 correctness | V-F4 |
   531	| Code, 1,000 or more files | +12.5% accuracy with vector search (Cursor), but 15 days later Cursor shipped Instant Grep and says it "does not store embeddings of your codebase for search" | V-F6, OC |
   532	| A memory growing 10× (1M to 10M tokens) | Mem0 64.1 to 48.6 (vendor's figures) | W-20 |
   533	| SEZA | no recorded misses, so the comparison has nothing to run on | SZ |
   534	
   535	**My reading:** retrieval beats loading everything somewhere around 100K–500K tokens, but it is keyword retrieval
   536	that wins. Vectors earn a place only on recorded meaning-misses, which is what B1.2 tests.
   537	
   538	### SEZA today, trigger by trigger (SZ, the researcher's reading)
   539	- **B1.1:** no verdict at n=5.
   540	- **B1.2:** not met.
   541	
   542	SEZA's own `project_memory search` was used 42 times in 15 sessions, with none after 2026-09-24.
   543	
   544	**What actually went wrong in SEZA was not search** (CX):
   545	- Its start index carried outdated state: the cleanup audit records *"Memory index stale"*
   546	  (`seza/runs/20261010-project-cleanup/STATUS-GRAPH.md:268`).
   547	- Its derived graph was missing when it was asked for: *"graph is missing; run sync or rebuild"*.
   548	
   549	**My reading:** a faster cache would have returned the same stale entries. K2 (stale never answers) matters more here
   550	than any of the three stores.
   551	
   552	## What this changes elsewhere (proposed, not done)
   553	
   554	- `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` § Where it is put can keep `.cache/` as written. This document says when each part of it is built.
   555	
   556	## Not known

   559	  and a question set; the measurement decides (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"this is design guide, how can I give you a
   560	  number?"*).
   561	- No measurement exists of either cache on a project-memory corpus. All evidence is on other corpora: source code,
   562	  chat memory, enterprise documents.

   564	  header). `V-F*` quotes were checked against saved copies.

   573	| point | agy | codex | opencode |

   575	| The BM-9 speed bound and the audit's `git ls-files` overhead | Holds codex's position: the bound holds. | The overhead biases grep's comparative timing, but removing it lowers grep's time and does not break the 15 ms bound; it should be disclosed. | The overhead makes the speed comparison unsafe and the finding undercuts BM-9. |

     1	# Caches — product vision statement
     2	
     3	> **Status: settled** (accepted by EJ on 2026-10-12, and again after the changes below: *"others can accept and
     4	> apply"*). EJ's first words: *"ok the vision is confirm"*. Drafted 2026-10-12 by the COO from the research in
     5	> `runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/` and `runs/20261011-project-memory-engines/RECOMMENDATION.md`; rewritten the same
     6	> day at EJ's direction — more direct, fewer hedges.
     7	>
     8	> **Drawn from:** `docs/memory-system/CACHES.md`, which carries the core requirements (Build / Keep / Read) this
     9	> vision serves. This file holds the vision only, so each has one copy (`docs/workflow-templates/blueprint.md` keeps
    10	> vision and requirements in separate section files).
    11	>
    12	> **Whose it is.** Per the blueprint template, the product vision is EJ's — *"an agent may only write down what EJ
    13	> has said"*. This was drafted from the research at his request and settled by him on 2026-10-12.
    14	>
    15	> **Changed 2026-10-12:** the code graph removed at EJ's direction (*"code graph should have it's own project"*); its
    16	> draft is `docs/code-graph/CODE_GRAPH.md`. The problem now names both ways it shows up, one per cache — the vector
    17	> reason was missing (EJ: *"ok write it into the vision"*). "How we know it works" now tests that the right entry is
    18	> found for less (B1.1, B1.2), not only that a rebuild repeats; and the rebuild's "no model call" now excepts the vector
    19	> embedding step, as B5.1 does (EJ: *"ok write it into the vision"*). "Too many entries" no longer says `INDEX.md` stops
    20	> listing every active entry: the full-text trigger is the B1.1 comparison (EJ: *"the comparison for full-text"*), and
    21	> `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` requires `INDEX.md` to list every active entry and still fit (K6.1, Rd3.1). Then, on acceptance
    22	> (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"others can accept and apply"*): "who decides" replaces "who sets the triggers"; the grep timing is
    23	> one labelled figure; `G-F5` is labelled as a code-graph study; and "never answers from a stale index" is added. The
    24	> tests run on "the project's own questions and noted misses": the full question log was dropped (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"yes
    25	> , made that change"*).
    26	
    27	The caches are two optional indexes over the project's markdown memory — full-text search and vector search. Both
    28	live in `project-memory/.cache/`, both are gitignored, and both are rebuilt from the files. Neither is needed today.
    29	
    30	**Who it is for.** An agent retrieving an entry on demand. Not the human reviewer, who reads the entries themselves,
    31	and not EJ, who decides whether a cache is built once its comparison passes (B1.0).
    32	
    33	**The problem it solves.** The memory is markdown in git, and today `INDEX.md` plus grep finds what an agent needs — in
    34	the researcher's own test, a one-word grep over 10,000 synthetic entries took 0.038 s (`S-F13`). As the memory grows,
    35	that stops being enough in two ways:
    36	- **Too many entries — full-text search.** Reaching the right entry through `INDEX.md` and grep means reading more
    37	  and more: grep returns unranked matches, and every hit an agent opens costs context (`S-F1`).
    38	- **Different words — vector search.** The agent's words differ from the entry's, so `INDEX.md`, grep and
    39	  full-text search all miss an entry that exists (`V-F13`).
    40	
    41	The caches exist to keep finding the right entry cheap when that happens — and not before.
    42	
    43	**What it will never do.**
    44	- It is never the source of truth. The markdown is, always, and a cache holds nothing the files do not (`M4`, `M6`).
    45	- It is never patched incrementally. It is rebuilt whole or not at all — a live, incrementally synced index drifted
    46	  4.3% of its edges from a clean rebuild with every integrity check passing (`G-F5`: a study of a code-graph tool,
    47	  kept as evidence of how a patched index fails).
    48	- It never answers from a stale index. Before answering, it is checked against the files and rebuilt if they
    49	  differ (K2) — stale state, not search, is what went wrong in SEZA (`CACHES.md` § SEZA today).
    50	- It is never read at session start (`M18`).
    51	- It is never built before its own trigger fires. Every trigger is an observable comparison, and the measurement
    52	  decides it, not a number set in advance (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"this is design guide, how can I give you a number?"*).
    53	- It never becomes a second thing to keep in sync: delete the folder and memory is unaffected.
    54	
    55	**How we know it works.** Two tests, on the project's own questions and noted misses.
    56	- **It finds the right entry, for less.** Full-text search reaches the right entry after reading less than
    57	  `INDEX.md` plus grep, with no more misses (B1.1). Vector search, run on the recorded misses, finds entries
    58	  full-text did not, and does no worse on the other questions (B1.2).
    59	- **It holds nothing of its own.** Delete the cache, rebuild it from the files, and the same query returns the
    60	  same entries, with no model call except the vector embedding step (`M6`, B5.1; a 10,000-entry FTS rebuild takes
    61	  0.53 s, `S-F13`).
    62	
    63	One caveat travels with every number here: none of it has been measured on a real `project-memory/` corpus,
    64	because that corpus does not exist yet (`S-F5`, `V-F11`).
# Synthesis — three reviews of the caches requirements

Written 2026-10-12 by the COO (Claude `claude-opus-5-5`) from `codex.md`, `opencode.md` and `claude.md` in this
folder. All three: **REPAIR**. Nothing has been changed in `docs/` from these reviews; that waits for EJ.

**Checked by me against the sources** (marked ✓): the S-F9 citation, the V-F13 miss definition, the five trigger
lines in `CACHES.md`, the sibling `INDEX.md` rules, M6's status, and the benchmark's miss rule. The rest is the
reviewers' reading, not re-checked.

|---|---|---|---|

-## Rules for all three caches
+## Rules for every cache
 
 These apply to whichever cache is built. They come from how the researched tools fail.
 
 - **Full rebuild, never patched.** A live, incrementally updated index drifted silently from a clean rebuild: 4.3% of
   edges wrong, with every integrity check passing (`G-F5 drift`). Its makers decided drift cannot be measured cheaply
-  (`G-F5c`). A full rebuild of this repo's code graph takes 0.94 s (`G-F12`); the benchmark's own code-graph rebuild
-  takes 0.89 s (BM-9); an FTS rebuild of 10,000 synthetic entries takes 0.53 s (`S-F13`).
+  (`G-F5c`). An FTS rebuild of 10,000 synthetic entries takes 0.53 s (`S-F13`).
 - **Rebuild before answering when stale.** Before each query, compare the recorded content hash of the
   sources with the index and rebuild if they differ. Build into a temporary file and rename it over the old one
   (`S-F9`, quoted: *"A stale index is worse than no index."*).

-## Code graph
-
-### Five whys
-
-1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code, such as what an edit touches.
-2. **Why would memory need that?** For "which entries cover the files I am editing" (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd4.1). The
-   drift check it once also served was removed by EJ on 2026-10-12.
-3. **Why is a text search enough?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
-   to path (`G-F14`), so finding the entries that cover a file is a search over `covers:` lines.
-4. **Why not add a graph anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that a text search does not have
-   (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but it
-   covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
-   Whether an entry is still true is answered by reading it against the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd5), not by a
-   graph.
-5. **Why might it be needed later?** Either of two conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:
-   - `covers:` starts naming functions or classes instead of files;
-   - an agent must answer about files not on any `covers:` list ("what does this change affect").
-

-| **Who** | Any agent, by a text search over `covers:` lines. EJ decides to build a graph if one of the two conditions holds. |
-| **What** | Today: no graph for memory. A text search over `covers:` lines. If a condition holds: the repo's own `code_graph/code_graph.py` (Python only, deterministic, full rebuild, no incremental mode, `G-F18`) before any outside tool. |
-| **When** | Today: before editing a file (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` Rd4.1). A graph, if ever built, is rebuilt in full each time it is used. |
-| **Where** | Nothing today. Later, `project-memory/.cache/code-graph.json`, gitignored. |
-| **Why** | The five whys above. |
-| **How** | `rg` for the file's path in the `covers:` lines of `entries/`. External indexers (codegraph) are not adopted: their win is measured on source-code architecture questions, not on memory (`G-F7`, `G-F14`), and they bring the drift in `G-F5 drift`. |

    17	## Answer to the planted question
    18	
    19	**Question:** "What default FTS5 tokenizer does the `hollowfern` markdown indexer use, according to its docs?"
    20	
    21	**Answer: GAP. I found no `hollowfern` markdown indexer, so there are no docs to answer from.**
    22	
    23	What I checked (all on 2026-10-12):
    24	- Two web searches: `hollowfern markdown indexer FTS5 tokenizer` and `"hollowfern" github markdown index`; neither returned any project of that name. The bare search `hollowfern` returned only holly-fern plant pages.
    25	- PyPI `pypi.org/pypi/hollowfern/json` -> HTTP 404; npm `registry.npmjs.org/hollowfern` -> 404; crates.io `api/v1/crates/hollowfern` -> 404.
    26	- `gh api users/hollowfern`: a GitHub account exists (created 2026-01-08) with two repos, `Astra-Grid` ("Fork of grid modeler with new features and fixes.") and `CMBE` ("A library with ready-made functions combo,repeat,hold,sequence."). Neither is a markdown indexer. `gh search repos hollowfern` also lists `lansingalong/hollowfern-game` (no description). `gh search code hollowfern` found only hostnames (`*.hollowfern.sbs`) in a Telegram proxy list.
    27	- The name appears in this repo only in `runs/20261012-memory-caches/state.md` line 71, where it is listed as a name that does not exist.
    28	
    29	I did not read the code of `Hollowfern/Astra-Grid`, `Hollowfern/CMBE` or `lansingalong/hollowfern-game`; their one-line descriptions say they are not markdown indexers.
    30	
    31	What I can say that is true, and is not an answer about hollowfern: **SQLite's own FTS5 default tokenizer is `unicode61`** (F2). Any tool that creates an FTS5 table without a `tokenize=` option gets it. That says nothing about a tool I could not find.
    32	
    33	## Findings
    34	
    35	### 1. Questions the store answers (brief item 1)
    36	
    37	**F1 — CONFIRMED (stated rationale, not a measurement).** The `monorepo-agents-harness` maintainers list what grep cannot do for their knowledge folder, which is the closest thing found to an argued case for FTS5 over grep for agent memory.
    38	URL: https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/issues/25 (opened 2026-10-07, closed 2026-10-08).
    39	Quotes: `"grep returns unranked matches."`; the FTS5 table is described as `"FTS5 virtual table over title + body (BM25 ranking, `snippet()`)."`; the corpus was `"already ~1.4k files in one adopter repo."` The same extract paraphrases the other complaints: agents open whole files to judge relevance, the fallback scans every artifact tree and grows with each task, and backlink and orphan-page queries need ad-hoc shell pipelines.
    40	Limit: it is a feature proposal. It reports no accuracy or token number for FTS5 against grep.
    41	
    42	**F2 — CONFIRMED.** FTS5's documented behaviour that matters for this project.
    43	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (read 2026-10-12, no page date).
    44	Quotes: `"The unicode61 tokenizer, based on the Unicode 6.1 standard. This is the default."`; `"The better the match, the numerically smaller the value returned."` (bm25); `"The contents of columns qualified with the UNINDEXED column option are not added to the FTS index."`
    45	So header fields such as `kind`, `status`, `covers` can ride along as `UNINDEXED` columns or sit in a plain table next to the FTS table.
    46	
    47	**F3 — INFERRED from F1, F2 and my measurement F13.** Which questions an agent asks, split by what the store adds over `grep` plus `INDEX.md`:
    48	- *Ranked free-text recall* ("which entries talk about retry budget"): FTS5 adds an order (BM25) and snippets. grep returns an unranked list. `INDEX.md` (one line per active entry) already covers a title-level scan.
    49	- *Header-field queries* ("every active decision whose `covers` includes path X", "what supersedes Y"): a SQL `WHERE` over a table of header fields answers these. grep over `entries/` answers them with a pipeline of greps or a short script (F13 shows both returning the same hits). No source found measures an agent doing either.
    50	- *Backlinks, orphans, broken `supersedes` links*: needs a link table or a script; FTS5 itself does not provide it (the harness in F5 builds a separate `links` table).
    51	- *Semantic ("by meaning") questions*: FTS5 does not answer these; that is the vector cache's question, not this one.
    52	
    53	### 2. When it pays off (brief item 2)
    54	
    55	**F4 — CONFIRMED.** The one controlled scaling measurement found for BM25 against an agent using grep over files. The "File-System Agent" had `list_dir`, `grep` (fixed string, up to 30 matching paths) and `read_doc` (first 8,000 characters), 80 LLM calls per question; the benchmark is a general RAG corpus, not code or memory entries.
    56	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26497 and https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26497v3 ("BM25 Wins at Scale", submitted 2026-07-29, v3 2026-07-31).
    57	Quotes: `"Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it and leads at every larger shared tier"`; `"its sequential exploration costs 39 times more query tokens at the bedrock."` Numbers from the extract of the HTML: smallest tier 1,144 documents and 1.7M corpus tokens; tokens per question at that tier BM25 5.8K against File-System Agent 226K; at full corpus 5.8K against 895K (agent figure includes retries); BM25 leads "with a margin near 20 points at full scale." Also: the agent leads "at the smallest shared tiers."
    58	Limits: the extract gave the abstract's "roughly 10 million" and the smallest tier, not a per-tier table. The File-System Agent's grep returns paths only, not lines, which is weaker than `rg` with context. This is one paper, preprint.
    59	
    60	**F5 — INFERRED from F4 and F13.** Where this project sits against that crossover. The design says an entry is "the claim in one or two sentences, then Why:, then How to apply:", so about 1 KB each. My synthetic entries of that shape came to 1.06 MB per 1,000 entries (F13). At an assumed 4 characters per token (my assumption, not measured) that is about 0.27M tokens per 1,000 entries, so the 10M-token crossover in F4 would be near 37,000 entries, and even the 1.7M-token smallest tier in F4 would be near 6,000 entries. `project-memory/` does not exist yet in this repo (checked), so the real corpus size is unknown. The only count available: `docs/` and `runs/` hold 364 markdown files today. **No measurement exists of when FTS5 or BM25 beats grep for an agent on a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries.** That is a GAP, not a finding.
    61	
    62	**F6 — CONFIRMED, and it cuts the other way.** Grep matched or beat vector retrieval in a controlled agent study.
    63	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15184 (submitted 2026-05-14), HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2605.15184v1.
    64	Quotes: `"inline grep exceeds inline vector for every harness–model pair"` (Sec. 4.1.3); `"We do not claim that grep “beats” vector in general."`; `"Lexical tools may be disproportionately helpful here because answers often license on verbatim spans."` Example cells from its Table 1: Claude Opus 4.6 in Claude Code, inline grep 76.7 against inline vector 75.0; GPT-5.4 in Codex CLI 93.1 against 75.9.
    65	Limits: LongMemEval conversational QA (116 questions), not code memory. It compares grep with vector, not with BM25. The extract reported no token or cost figures. Relevant here mainly to the vector cache; for the FTS store it shows grep is a strong baseline.
    66	
    67	**F7 — CONFIRMED (vendor's own small benchmark).** A grep baseline recalled almost everything but was less precise than a hybrid index.
    68	URL: https://github.com/rohitg00/agentmemory/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/2026-05-20-coding-agent-life-v1.md (2026-05-20).
    69	Quotes: `"grep baseline: R@5 = 0.967, P@5 = 0.227 — missed one gold session in one multi-gold question."`; `"The benchmark is small (15 questions) and gold-sparse."` Hybrid got R@5 1.000 and P@5 0.240; the author says P@5 "is at the math ceiling for this dataset."
    70	Limits: 15 fictional sessions, 15 queries, author's own project. A web-search summary of this page gave P@5 as 0.267; the page says 0.227, and I use the page. The page's own caveat says precision cannot separate the adapters.
    71	
    72	**F8 — UNPROVEN.** Claude Code's own default is grep-style search, not an index (commonly reported). I did not open a primary source for it, so it is not used above.
    73	
    74	### 3. Current tools that keep markdown as the source and build a SQLite index (brief item 3)
    75	
    76	All of these are 2026; none is measured on a corpus like ours. Rebuild time and size are listed only where a source gave them; the local numbers are in F13.
    77	
    78	**F9 — CONFIRMED. `monorepo-agents-harness` `kb-index.sh`.** Closest match to the brief: full rebuild, gitignored, grep fallback.
    79	URLs: https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/issues/25 (2026-10-07) and https://github.com/atayahmet/monorepo-agents-harness/pull/26 (merged 2026-10-08, commit `6a22b7e`, VERSION 0.4.0-rc.13).
    80	Quotes: `"Rebuild in full every time."`; `"`query` compares the index build time with the newest source mtime and rebuilds first if the index is stale."`; `"A stale index is worse than no index."`; if `sqlite3` is missing or lacks FTS5 it should `"print one warning and fall back to the current grep path."`; rebuild `"finishes in under a second"` at about 1.4k files. PR: `"a deterministic, self-healing SQLite FTS5 index over `knowledge/**/*.md`"`, built into a temp file then renamed over `knowledge/.index.sqlite`; derived columns `kind`, `workspace`, `task_slug`, `date` come from page shapes, not frontmatter. Tests: 38/38; removing self-heal caused 2 failures and removing atomic publish caused 4.
    81	Local: yes, `sqlite3` CLI and shell. Tokenizer: not named (so FTS5 default). Size on disk: not stated.
    82	
    83	**F10 — CONFIRMED. Scott Spence's `node:sqlite` + FTS5 markdown tool** (and its "wiki0" successor).
    84	URL: https://scottspence.com/posts/give-coding-agents-your-project-docs-with-node-sqlite-and-fts5 (2026-09-17).
    85	Quotes: `"One chunk per heading, so results point at a section, not a whole file."`; the index command starts with `db.exec('DELETE FROM chunks')` and reinserts everything; `"with a SQLite index that can be rebuilt any time for search, backlinks and facts."`; facts are `"decisions, requirements, risks and assumptions so they could be queried on their own."` Numbers: about 260 posts as 2,647 chunks in under half a second including Node startup; search about 50 ms; in a real project docs grew from 65 to 291 and agents called the CLI 1,122 times across 327 sessions. FTS5 table made with no tokenizer option (default). Staleness: not discussed; full rebuild each run. Index size: not given. No comparison with grep is made in the post.
    86	
    87	**F11 — CONFIRMED. memweave** (Python; hybrid BM25 + optional vectors).
    88	URL: https://towardsdatascience.com/memweave-zero-infra-ai-agent-memory-with-markdown-and-sqlite-no-vector-database-required/ (2026-04-16, by its author).
    89	Quotes: `"The database is always a derived cache — if you delete it, memweave rebuilds it from the files."`; `"losing the database is not data loss. Losing the files is."`; `"BM25 catches exact matches — error codes, config values, proper names"`. Change detection: SHA-256 on files and chunks. Default merge weight 0.7 vector, 0.3 BM25. No size, rebuild time, latency or benchmark number is given.
    90	
    91	**F12 — CONFIRMED (partly). Other tools, one line each.**
    92	- `ai-memory` (Rust): `"Plain markdown on disk as source of truth. SQLite is just a derived index."`; `"Persistent index in SQLite + FTS5. Native. No reindex on every restart."`; author calls it beta, a few weeks in personal use. Its comparison target `agentmemory` reportedly pays `"~5 minutes rebuilding from scratch"` on restart and slowed at about 10K observations. https://akitaonrails.com/en/2026/05/23/i-built-memory-system-for-coding-agents-ai-memory/ (2026-05-23). The 5-minute figure is the author's report about another tool.
    93	- `sqliteai/sqlite-memory`: `"Computes a hash of the content. If the same content was already indexed, it is skipped entirely."`; changed file: `"the old entry (chunks, embeddings, FTS) is atomically replaced."` Search needs embeddings (a local GGUF model or a remote key), so it is not FTS-only. No size, time or benchmark number; no page date. https://github.com/sqliteai/sqlite-memory
    94	- `qmd` (Node): FTS5 index `documents_fts`, vectors in sqlite-vec, default index at `~/.cache/qmd/index.sqlite`, `qmd update` re-indexes, `qmd search` is `"BM25 full-text search only"`; vector, rerank and query-expansion models are about 300 MB, 640 MB and 1.1 GB. Rebuild time and size not stated; BM25-only offline use not confirmed from the page. Its fixture "typically shows" bm25 about 0.50, vector about 0.70, hybrid about 1.00, on the repo's own example fixture. https://github.com/tobi/qmd (no date shown).
    95	- Basic Memory: `"all knowledge is represented in standard Markdown files and the database serves as a secondary index."`; the page names neither SQLite nor FTS5 and does not cover rebuild. https://docs.basicmemory.com/reference/technical-information (sample timestamps only, no page date). Search results elsewhere call the database SQLite (UNPROVEN, not opened).
    96	- `metafunctor` "MCP pattern" post (files as ground truth, read-only FTS5 cache): **UNPROVEN**; the page returned HTTP 503 twice, so I only have a search-engine summary of it. https://metafunctor.com/post/2026-03-20-the-mcp-pattern/ (URL dated 2026-03-20 by its path).
    97	
    98	**F13 — MEASURED HERE (my own script; not independent evidence).** A synthetic corpus (random words, entries shaped like the design: frontmatter with `id`, `kind`, `status`, `covers`, `superseded_by`, then about 1 KB body), one run each, on this machine (Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1, WSL2). Script: `/tmp/claude-1000/-home-smoke01-dev-ancient-games/343bf18f-e2d9-4dcf-8db5-0ed3524f59db/scratchpad/bench/bench.py`. Full rebuild = read every file, parse frontmatter, insert into one FTS5 table plus one header table.
    99	
   100	| entries | markdown | index file | full rebuild | "active decision covering path X": python script / grep chain / SQL | one-word search: grep -rli / FTS5 top 10 |
   101	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
   102	| 100 | 0.1 MB | 0.3 MB | 0.03 s | 0.001 / 0.005 / 0.0002 s | 0.002 / 0.0006 s |
   103	| 1,000 | 1.1 MB | 2.3 MB | 0.08 s | 0.011 / 0.009 / 0.0002 s | 0.005 / 0.0002 s |
   104	| 10,000 | 10.6 MB | 21.9 MB | 0.53 s | 0.104 / 0.060 / 0.0008 s | 0.038 / 0.0006 s |
   105	| 50,000 | 53.2 MB | 110.3 MB | 2.57 s | 0.405 / 0.148 / 0.0031 s | invalid (see below) |
   106	
   107	What it does and does not show. At every size the grep route answers in well under half a second, so **latency is not a reason for a cache at this scale**; the index is about twice the markdown size. The 50,000-entry grep timing printed a negative number (the WSL clock moved), so I discard it. For the first three rows the structured query matched zero entries (the random path did not occur), so those timings are a full scan with no hits. The python script and the SQL returned the same count only where hits existed (4 and 4 at 50,000). Token cost to the agent is the real axis and this test does not measure it: it does not run an agent. The gain F4 reports is on tokens and accuracy, not wall-clock.
   108	
   109	### 4. How it fails (brief item 4)
   110	
   111	**F14 — CONFIRMED.** SQLite itself warns about an FTS5 index drifting from its source, and gives a repair.
   112	URL: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html (no page date). Quote: the `rebuild` command `"first deletes the entire full-text index, then rebuilds it based on the contents of the table"`; `integrity-check` fails with `SQLITE_CORRUPT_VTAB` on discrepancies. The extract also says keeping an external-content table in step with its source table is the user's job.
   113	Limit, INFERRED: `integrity-check` compares the index with the table it indexes. It does not compare that table with the markdown files on disk. I found no SQLite feature that does, so file drift must be caught by the tool (F15). Not tested here.
   114	
   115	**F15 — CONFIRMED (the mechanisms in use).** How the tools above detect or prevent drift from the files:
   116	- mtime comparison on every query, rebuild first if stale, atomic temp-file-then-rename publish (F9, quotes above).
   117	- Full delete-and-reinsert on every index run, so drift lasts only until the next run (F10).
   118	- SHA-256 per file and chunk, unchanged ones skipped (F11; and sqlite-memory in F12).
   119	- A grep fallback if the index or FTS5 is missing (F9). This is the same property as M6, "its absence must not prevent reading memory."
   120	Not found: any of these tools reporting a measured stale-answer rate. The only measured silent index drift in the record is the code graph, not FTS (s3-F25, s3-F27). The failure class is the same (a derived index that is not rebuilt when files change). Nothing shows FTS5 over markdown is prone to it.
   121	
   122	**F16 — MEASURED HERE (small test, `python3` stdlib `sqlite3`, SQLite 3.45.1).** Two query-time traps with the default tokenizer, on a row containing `D-0012 covers src/auth/token_store.py and the retry_budget setting`:
   123	- Unquoted `D-0012` raises `no such column: 0012` (a loud error; the `-` is read as a column filter). Quoted `"D-0012"` matches. The same under `porter` and `trigram`.
   124	- With the tokenizer changed to `unicode61 tokenchars '-_/.'` (a plausible attempt to keep ids and paths whole), the queries `token_store`, `retry`, `budget` and `auth` returned **0 rows, silently**; the quoted full path still matched. `trigram` matched every query tried. The default matched `token_store`, `retry`, `budget`, `auth` and `"auth/token"`.
   125	So a wrong tokenizer choice yields a wrong zero rather than an error. I tried only these 8 queries and 4 tokenizers on one row.
   126	
   127	**F17 — INFERRED from F1 and F10.** Result granularity. Chunking by heading (F10) returns a section; one-file-per-entry (the design) could index one row per file. Which is right is not settled by any source found.
   128	
   129	**F18 — INFERRED from s3-F20/F34 and F6.** Where the evidence says a richer store helps and where it does not: MOOSEDev's advantage is on supersession and set questions, with relevance recall and token cost "largely equivalent" (s3-F34); a lexical store is not shown to win on relevance either. So for this project the case, if any, rests on the header-field questions in F3, not on search quality.
   130	
   131	### 5. What fits here (brief item 5) — options, no choice
   132	
   133	The machine has Python 3.12 and SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5 (verified 2026-10-12), `rg` and `grep`. Nothing to install.
   134	
   135	- **Option A — no cache.** `INDEX.md` plus `rg` over `entries/`, and a short stdlib Python script that reads frontmatter for the structured questions (F13 shows 0.1 s at 10,000 entries). Cost: no ranking, no snippets.
   136	- **Option B — smallest FTS cache.** One stdlib `sqlite3` script: full rebuild into a temp file, `os.replace` over `project-memory/.cache/index.sqlite`; one FTS5 table (default tokenizer, one row per entry file, header fields as `UNINDEXED` columns) and optionally a header-fields table; rebuild when the newest file mtime is newer than the index, as in F9; fall back to `rg` if the file or FTS5 is missing. Measured rebuild 0.53 s and 22 MB at 10,000 synthetic entries (F13); the design's M6 holds because absence changes nothing for readers.
   137	- **Option C — B plus link table.** Adds `depends_on` / `supersedes` / `superseded_by` rows for backlink, orphan and broken-link queries (as the harness `links` command in F9). This is the structured-query use; the drift-check script named in the design as "not decided" could read it, but that is a design matter, not found here.
   138	- **Choices a design must still make** (listed, not decided): tokenizer (F16), row grain (F17), staleness test (mtime as in F9, or hash as in F11), who triggers a rebuild, whether query and rebuild share one command (F9 does).
   139	
   140	## Not found
   141	
   142	- Any docs, repo or package for a `hollowfern` markdown indexer, and therefore its default tokenizer (planted question: GAP).
   143	- Any measurement of FTS5 or BM25 against grep for an agent working on a small (hundreds to a few thousand) corpus of short memory entries, on accuracy or tokens.
   144	- Any measurement of an agent answering header-field or supersession questions with SQL against with grep or a script over frontmatter.
   145	- A measured stale-answer or drift rate for any FTS5-over-markdown tool.
   146	- Index size and rebuild time in the published sources, apart from F9 ("under a second" at about 1.4k files) and F10 (under half a second at 2,647 chunks); no source gave an on-disk size.
   147	- A primary source for the claim that Claude Code's default search is grep without an index (F8).
   148	
   149	## Not checked
   150	
   151	- The raw page text of any cited page (all reads went through WebFetch extracts); the full text of "BM25 Wins at Scale" beyond the extracts, and its per-tier table.
   152	- The last 71,799 characters of the FTS5 reference page (read to character 100,000 of 171,799), which would hold the details of `trigram`, `porter`, contentless tables and `detail=`.
   153	- `metafunctor` post (HTTP 503), sqlite-memory's code, qmd's source, Basic Memory's code and its FTS usage.
   154	- SQLite FTS5 tools outside the markdown-agent-memory space (for example Obsidian or static-site search), and tools older than 2025.
   155	- Actual `grep` or `rg` token cost to an agent (output size returned into context) against an FTS5 `snippet()` result; not measured.
   156	- Whether Windows or macOS builds of SQLite ship FTS5 (irrelevant on this machine).
   157	- The vector index and the code graph (other nodes' stores).
     1	# RECOMMENDATION — the memory system a software project should have
     2	
     3	Join of node 4. Inputs (read in full): `nodes/claude.stdout.md` (Claude Sonnet 5.5, 36 claims),
     4	`nodes/codex.stdout.md` (Codex, 26 claims), `nodes/opencode.stdout.md` (MiniMax-M2.5, 7 claims).
     5	Merged claim ids `M1…M24` are this node's labels; no claim appears here that an engine did not make.
     6	Engine-attribution key: **claude** / **codex** / **opencode**. A verdict is `AGREED` (all three assert it),
     7	`MAJORITY` (two assert it, the third is silent), `CONTESTED` (engines assert incompatible positions),
     8	`SINGLE` (one asserts it, the others are silent).
     9	
    10	## Recommendation
    11	
    12	Keep the project's memory as reviewed markdown, version-controlled with the code, as the single source of
    13	truth: short typed entries (decision, fact, lesson, runbook) that each carry an id, kind, author, recorded and
    14	verified dates, the code paths or symbols they cover, their evidence, and explicit supersession links — never
    15	deleted, only superseded. One canonical cross-agent instruction file (`AGENTS.md`, with thin per-tool adapters)
    16	plus a small generated index and the assigned task's state file are the only things read at session start;
    17	everything else is fetched on demand by filename or text search, or by following links, and a retrieved
    18	statement is checked against its primary source before it determines an action. Staleness is computed
    19	deterministically from changes to the code an entry covers, not from age; contradicting entries are kept side
    20	by side, marked disputed, checked against code and evidence, and escalated to a human maintainer when code
    21	cannot decide; each writer gets its own file. SQLite/FTS, vector search and code graphs are permitted only as
    22	gitignored, rebuildable derived caches — no vector, graph, database, context window or agent-private store is
    23	ever the source of truth, and any new store must first answer a named, demonstrated failure of the
    24	markdown-and-search baseline on this project's own tasks.
    25	
    26	## Support table
    27	
    28	| # | claim | engines agreeing (claude/codex/opencode) | strongest basis of any engine (read: <source> / model knowledge) | verdict |
    29	|---|---|---|---|---|
    30	| M1 | Markdown files kept with the code are the canonical shared project memory. | claude, codex, opencode | **model knowledge** for the substrate ranking in all three; the adjacent read is claude `read: docs/research/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/REPORT.md` s1-F7/F8 (a production memory system is a git-backed filesystem), which supports git-backed files, not markdown-over-others | AGREED |
    31	| M2 | Memory is version-controlled with the code so a change is reviewed as a diff and a fresh clone can rebuild it. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://git-scm.com/book/en/v2/Git-Branching-Basic-Branching-and-Merging** (branch/merge mechanics) | MAJORITY |
    32	| M3 | The context window holds the current task only; it is not durable memory. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: codex — https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory** (fresh context at session start) | AGREED |
    33	| M4 | A vector index is refused as the source of truth; at most a derived, rebuildable index, and only when the project's own queries justify it. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F20, s3-F34** (a KG store beat vector top-k on supersession/negation, 6–27% surfaced; relevance recall and token cost "largely equivalent"); also codex `read: https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/retrieval` | AGREED |
    34	| M5 | A graph store is refused as the memory store; explicit links between documents carry the relationships. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3 group** (graph evidence split; a graph index resident-cost 67k vs 18k tokens) | AGREED |
    35	| M6 | SQLite is a derived, gitignored, rebuildable index only; never authoritative, and its absence must not prevent reading memory. | claude, codex | **model knowledge** for the role call (codex's own "recommendation" label; claude's T1/T2/T4 design tests); codex's `read: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html` establishes only that FTS5 exists | CONTESTED (opencode: use `state.sqlite` as the structured state store, queried at session start) |

    37	| M8 | Private agent memory may persist for that agent's own continuity. | codex (yes); opencode (no — ephemeral) | **model knowledge** in both | CONTESTED |
    38	| M9 | One canonical instruction body (`AGENTS.md`) with thin per-tool adapters; no parallel, independently maintained policy copies. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://opencode.ai/docs/rules/ + https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory + https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/agents-md** | MAJORITY |
    39	| M10 | The three tools resolve instruction files differently (Codex nearest-wins override, Claude concatenation, opencode first-match with `AGENTS.md` over `CLAUDE.md`, references not auto-expanded); verify each deployed tool. | codex | **read: codex — https://opencode.ai/docs/rules/, https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory, https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/agents-md** | SINGLE |
    40	| M11 | Every durable entry carries id, kind, author, recorded/observed/verified dates, scope, source, status and dependency/supersession links. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://cognitect.com/blog/2011/11/15/documenting-architecture-decisions** (decision-record shape); the field list itself is design in both | MAJORITY |
    41	| M12 | A human maintainer accepts policy and significant decisions; agents draft them and record observations and handoffs. | claude, codex | **read: claude — ~/CLAUDE.md Rules 7 and 12** ("every artifact has one maker"); codex `read: /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games/CLAUDE.md` (a project-specific rule, not a general source) | MAJORITY |
    42	| M13 | Never delete a memory entry; supersede it with an explicit link and keep the old body. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s1-F4 and FINDINGS-s4 F5–F7** (Graphiti invalidates the older edge; Mem0 marks superseded edges invalid "rather than physically removing") | AGREED |
    43	| M14 | Disagreeing entries are kept as separate assertions, marked disputed, checked against primary evidence or code, then escalated to the authorized maintainer; a timestamp, majority or clean merge is not enough to pick a winner. | claude, codex | **read: claude — ~/CLAUDE.md Rule 12** ("don't blend conflicting patterns… flag the other"); codex's C22 is **model knowledge** | CONTESTED (opencode: read-time rule "prefer the newest non-stale entry") |
    44	| M15 | Staleness is bound to the revision an entry was verified against and to changes in the code, config or dependencies it covers, not to age. | claude, codex | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F4** (StateTape reads staleness off a code-graph diff with no model call), plus claude's own `memory_check.py` design | MAJORITY |
    45	| M16 | The staleness check is deterministic and makes no model call. | claude | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F4** (C5) | SINGLE |
    46	| M17 | At session start an agent reads the standing instructions, a small index or state file, and the material for its assigned task. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://developers.openai.com/blog/rethinking-skills-and-prompts-for-gpt-6-astra** (read documents according to the task) | CONTESTED (opencode: session-start reads are `context.md`, active tickets in `state.sqlite` and the last 3 `sessions/` files — no standing instructions and no index) |
    47	| M18 | Never auto-load the whole memory tree, the full session history, or a whole-graph dump. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F24, s3-F32** (67k vs 18k resident tokens; ~63K vs ~41K) | AGREED |
    48	| M19 | Everything else is retrieved on demand by filename/text search or by following index links; open the primary source when a retrieved statement will determine an action. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: codex — OpenAI guidance on skills and prompts** | AGREED |
    49	| M20 | Prose instructions are context, not enforced configuration; critical constraints belong in hooks, permissions or CI. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory**; claude `read:` the agent-loop skill's Stop/PreToolUse/PostToolUse hooks | MAJORITY |
    50	| M21 | Refuse any additional store until a named, demonstrated failure of the markdown-and-search baseline exists, measured on the project's own tasks, with a named maintainer and acceptance criteria. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s5-F7, s5-F22** (a benchmark key with 6.4% corrupting errors; swapping the grading prompt moved one system 0.9013→0.4803), so published numbers cannot choose a design; codex's own test is **model knowledge** and unexecuted | AGREED |
    51	| M22 | Any memory artifact must be human-readable and reviewable; refuse stores that are only machine-readable. | claude, codex, opencode | **model knowledge** in all three (codex's refusal rationale; opencode's test question 3) | AGREED |
    52	| M23 | Refuse one shared append file written by parallel agents; give each writer its own file. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://git-scm.com/book/en/v2/Git-Branching-Basic-Branching-and-Merging** (a merge does not establish semantic agreement); claude C35 is **model knowledge** | CONTESTED (opencode: `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD.md` is one shared per-day append file, and `context.md` is one shared overwrite file) |
    53	| M24 | Durable entries are written at discovery/correction or task end, not every turn and not as a standing per-session obligation. | claude, codex | **model knowledge** in both (claude also permits per-session `journal/` and `handoff/` appends, so its position is between the two) | CONTESTED (opencode: session end is a standing write trigger) |
    54	
    55	**M17 correction (repair 2).** M17 is **`CONTESTED`**, not `MAJORITY`: opencode does not merely omit the
    56	session-start read set — it asserts a different one: `context.md`, active tickets in `state.sqlite`, and the last
    57	three `sessions/` files, with no standing instructions and no index (`nodes/opencode.stdout.md:57-59`); the same
    58	split is CP6 below. M17 is therefore credited to **claude and codex** only (claude: `nodes/claude.stdout.md:104-107`;
    59	codex: `nodes/codex.stdout.md:30`, `:74`).
    60	
    61	## Contested points
    62	
    63	**CP1 — The role of SQLite.**
    64	- **opencode:** SQLite *is* the structured state store — "structured state tracking (ticket status, session logs, entity relationships)", written to `.ai/state.sqlite` inside the repo and queried at session start for `status != done`. Basis: **model knowledge** (C2), justified as "lightweight, queryable, no external service needed, atomic writes".
    65	- **claude and codex:** SQLite is at most a **derived, gitignored, rebuildable cache** and never authoritative. claude places it at `.cache/` "rebuilt from the tree"; codex places it at `.cache/project-memory/index.sqlite` gitignored and requires that "its absence must not prevent reading authoritative memory", refusing a committed SQLite as sole memory because "it adds a required access mechanism where inspectable files already meet the proposed need". Basis: codex `read: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html` plus its **model-knowledge** recommendation; claude's T1/T2/T4 design tests.
    66	- **unresolved.** Nothing in the three files measures a committed binary state store against markdown state files. What would settle it: on one real project, track ticket/session state both ways and compare merge-conflict rate on the binary, the cost of querying state without the DB (does a reader still recover?), and the human reviewability of a `state.sqlite` diff.
    67	
    68	**CP2 — Contradiction resolution: newest-wins vs disputed-and-escalate.**
    69	- **opencode:** "On read, agent should check for `## Superseded` / `## Stale` markers and **prefer the newest non-stale entry**." Basis: **model knowledge** (C7, staleness section).
    70	- **claude and codex:** newest does not win. claude: "**Why not newest wins.** Graphiti's 'new wins' is write-time and LLM-driven"; both entries become `contested`, the next agent adjudicates against code and tests, a human decides if code cannot. Basis: **read: FINDINGS-s4 F15, F16** — a deterministic latest-wins pipeline scored far above the stores on MemoryAgentBench single-hop (78.0%/94.8% vs Zep 7.0%, Mem0 18.0%) but only tied LLM judgment on LongMemEval knowledge-update (57.8% vs 64.4%, n=45); claude gives both rows and refuses to pick. codex: "Recent timestamps, majority votes and clean Git merges are insufficient to select a winner." Basis: **model knowledge** (C22).
    71	- **unresolved.** No engine ran read-time newest-wins against disputed-and-escalate on real coding tasks. What would settle it: replay both rules over a recorded set of contradicting entries and count how often newest-wins selects the entry that a later code change or a maintainer confirms.
    72	
    73	**CP3 — Where the memory tree lives.**
    74	- **opencode:** `.ai/`, **isolated from human docs** — "keeps AI memory isolated from human docs". Basis: **model knowledge**.
    75	- **codex:** under the project's **existing `docs/`** — `docs/memory/`, `docs/decisions/`, `docs/work/` — and "reuse existing project documentation rather than copying it into memory"; memory links to already-authoritative requirements and architecture. Basis: **model knowledge** (C13).
    76	- **claude:** a dedicated committed **`memory/` tree** at the repo root (`INDEX.md`, `entries/`, `state/`, `journal/`). Basis: partly **read: REPORT.md s1-F7/F8** (Letta's layout: root files load every turn, directories with their own `MEMORY.md` stay out of context), partly design.
    77	- **unresolved.** This is a convention question, and the three files contain no evidence about a real project's docs. What would settle it: an inventory of one project's existing docs — if `docs/` already holds authoritative requirements and architecture, codex's linking placement wins; if writing agent memory there would pollute human docs, opencode's isolation wins; claude's separate committed tree is a third option.
    78	
    79	**CP4 — One shared per-day session file vs per-writer files.**
    80	- **opencode:** `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD.md`, append-only, one file per day, all agents appending. Basis: **model knowledge** (layout and write table).
    81	- **claude and codex:** one writer per file. claude: "Different file per session and per claim… No two agents edit one file", and per-session files "avoid git merge conflicts that a single shared append file causes" (**model knowledge**, C35). codex refuses "Parallel agents appending to one shared journal" — "overlapping writes and leaves integration implicit" — and notes that "Git merges do not establish semantic agreement". Basis: **read: https://git-scm.com/book/en/v2/Git-Branching-Basic-Branching-and-Merging**.
    82	- **unresolved.** No engine measured conflict rates. What would settle it: run several agents concurrently on one repo with a shared append file and with per-writer files, and count merge conflicts, lost appends and time spent integrating.
    83	
    84	**CP5 — Whether an agent's private store may persist between sessions.**
    85	- **codex:** yes — "allow for personal continuity; refuse as a project dependency"; private memory "may suggest where to look", and useful knowledge is promoted into the repo. Basis: **read: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory#storage-location** plus **model knowledge**.
    86	- **opencode:** no — the private store "should be ephemeral — no persistent memory between sessions"; cross-session state "should be explicit in project memory, not hidden in agent internals". Basis: **model knowledge** (C6).
    87	- **claude** is silent on whether it should persist: it records that each agent keeps a private store with no shared schema (C32, **model knowledge**; C31, **read:** the local `MEMORY.md` index), and rules only that a fact existing only there "does not count as memory".
    88	- **unresolved.** No evidence on whether private persistence helps continuity or hides state other agents need. What would settle it: an A/B where one agent keeps private notes and another does not, measuring whether the notes change actions and whether they duplicate what is in the repo.
    89	
    90	**CP6 — What is read automatically at session start.**
    91	- **opencode:** `context.md`, active tickets from `state.sqlite`, **and "Recent `sessions/` (last 3)"** for continuity. Basis: **model knowledge**.
    92	- **claude and codex:** instructions, the index, and the assigned task's state only. claude: `AGENTS.md`, `INDEX.md`, the `state/claims/` listing, the newest handoff — and "**Never load.** `journal/`". codex: instructions, `docs/memory/INDEX.md`, the task's `state.md`; "Do not bulk-load transcripts, completed-task journals, every decision or an entire search index at startup." Basis: codex **read: OpenAI guidance on skills and prompts** plus **model knowledge**; claude's read rules are design.
    93	- **unresolved.** No engine measured whether three prior session summaries improve the first action more than they cost in tokens and distraction. What would settle it: a with/without comparison on recorded tasks scoring first-action correctness and start-up tokens.
    94	
    95	**CP7 — Whether a memory write is obligatory every session.**
    96	- **opencode:** yes — "Session end | agent | Write summary to `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD.md` | Yes". Basis: **model knowledge**.
    97	- **codex:** no — "Do not require a memory write after every session. Write when there is something another session needs to know", and it refuses "a complete transcript archive as routine memory". Basis: **model knowledge** (C14).
    98	- **claude** sits between: writes are at task end and "Not every turn", but it does permit per-session `journal/` and `handoff/` appends; the raw-capture read (C9) supports keeping raw material cheap.
    99	- **unresolved.** This is a work-practice question the three files cannot settle. What would settle it: measure, over many sessions, how often a mandatory session summary is later read or cited — if it is rarely read, codex's rule wins; if it prevents repeated mistakes, opencode's does.
   100	
   101	**A separate, narrow note (not a claim conflict).** claude's C25 concludes from its read material — LoCoMo's key has 6.4% score-corrupting errors, and swapping only the grading prompt moved one system from 0.9013 to 0.4803 — that "published accuracy numbers cannot choose a project-memory design". codex reaches a related caution from a different source (C12, Liu et al., position-sensitive retrieval) but bounds it: "That establishes a historical failure mode, not a performance result for today's coding agents." The two do not disagree; codex's is the narrower claim.
   102	
   103	## Substrates
   104	
   105	| substrate | merged answer | reason | engines |
   106	|---|---|---|---|
   107	| Markdown files | **USE — source of truth** | Human-readable, diff-reviewable, rebuildable, no service. Every engine's primary substrate (M1, M22). The ranking itself is model knowledge; only the git-backed property is read (claude, REPORT.md s1-F7/F8). | claude, codex, opencode |
   108	| SQLite | **CONDITIONAL — derived, gitignored, rebuildable only; never authoritative** (majority). opencode dissents (CP1) | Codex: "allow as an optional derived index; refuse as the default authoritative memory"; absence must not prevent reading memory. claude: `.cache/`, rebuilt from the tree. opencode would use it as the structured state store. | claude, codex (opencode contested) |
   109	| Vector index | **REFUSE as the store; CONDITIONAL as a rebuildable derived index, only if the project's own queries justify it** | Similarity does not establish that a statement is current, accepted or true (codex, model knowledge); the one directly comparable read found relevance recall and token cost "largely equivalent" to a KG (claude, read REPORT.md s3-F34). | claude, codex, opencode |
   110	| Graph store | **REFUSE as the store; CONDITIONAL as a derived code map generated per commit** | No demonstrated generic need for a relationship model; supersession is available as frontmatter links; graph evidence is split and a live graph has a resident-context cost and can drift silently (claude, read REPORT.md s3 group). | claude, codex, opencode |
   111	| Context window | **USE for the current task only; REFUSE as durable memory** | Fresh context at session start; recovery must be possible from project artifacts without the previous conversation (codex, read Claude Code memory docs). | claude, codex, opencode |
   112	| Each agent's private session store | **REFUSE as a project dependency; allow private notes but promote anything others need into the repo** (persistence itself is CP5) | It is machine-local and invisible to other agents; a fact that exists only there does not count as project memory (codex read storage location; claude C31/C32; opencode C6). | claude, codex, opencode |
   113	
   114	## Layout
   115	
   116	One committed memory tree. The placement of that tree is CP3 (claude `memory/`; codex under `docs/`; opencode
   117	`.ai/`). The merged default shown here uses claude's typed-entry shape and codex's per-task shape, with the
   118	generated index both propose.
   119	
   120	```
   121	AGENTS.md                  canonical standing instructions: commands, constraints, memory read/write protocol, links.
   122	CLAUDE.md                  thin adapter / symlink importing AGENTS.md — no independently maintained policy copy.
   123	memory/
   124	  INDEX.md                 GENERATED, one line per active entry: id · kind · title · covers · status flag.
   125	                           Never hand-edited. The only whole-file memory load at session start.
   126	  entries/
   127	    D-0007-<slug>.md       decision + why: context, choice, consequences, acceptance reference, replacement links.
   128	    F-0012-<slug>.md       sourced observation / verified pitfall not derivable from code, tests or git history.
   129	    L-0003-<slug>.md       lesson from a failure or a human correction.
   130	    R-0002-<slug>.md       runbook; prefer a script, the entry only points at it.
   131	  work/<task-id>/
   132	    state.md               current handoff: objective, owner, branch/base revision, done, outstanding, checks, next action.
   133	    events/<event-id>.md   writer-specific historical record: a discovery, a failed approach, a handoff.
   134	    evidence/              sanitized evidence not recoverable from code, commits, tests or linked artifacts.
   135	  journal/<date>-<agent>-<session>.md   append-only raw notes; never auto-loaded.
   136	  .cache/ (gitignored)     optional derived SQLite/FTS and a repo map, rebuilt from the tree at the current commit.
   137	docs/ ...                  the project's existing requirements, architecture and runbooks; memory links here, does not copy.
   138	```
   139	
   140	Entry metadata (union of claude's frontmatter and codex's block; use applicable fields): `id`, `kind`
   141	(decision | fact | lesson | runbook | observation | hypothesis), `status`
   142	(proposed | active | contested | stale | disputed | superseded | retired), `author`, `recorded_at`,
   143	`observed_at` / `verified_at` (plus the git revision verified against), `covers` (paths or symbols),
   144	`source` / `evidence`, `depends_on`, `supersedes`, `superseded_by`. Body: the claim in one or two sentences,
   145	then **Why:**, then **How to apply:**.
   146	
   147	## Write rules
   148	
   149	- **Triggers (write):** a decision whose reason is not visible in code; a non-obvious constraint or verified
   150	  pitfall that cost more than one attempt; a human correction; a checkpoint of unfinished work before a
   151	  handoff, interruption or context reduction, recording exact remaining work and actual verification results,
   152	  including failures (claude, codex; opencode adds a decision and a session summary — CP7 for the summary).
   153	- **Do not write:** anything derivable from code, tests or git history; credentials or sensitive data;
   154	  speculative explanations unlabelled (label them `hypothesis`); bulk transcripts; another agent's private
   155	  state. Do not turn "implemented" into "verified".
   156	- **When:** at discovery or correction, or at task end, in the same commit or PR as the change that motivated
   157	  the entry. Not every turn; not as a standing per-session obligation (CP7).
   158	- **Who (agents):** append `journal/` and a handoff; create entries as `proposed` with `evidence:` mandatory;
   159	  update the authoritative document that a change invalidates, in the same review; flag `stale` or `contested`
   160	  with evidence; claim a task by creating a claim file.
   161	- **Who (humans):** accept `proposed` → `active` (review of the diff is the merge gate); accept significant
   162	  decisions and policy; resolve `contested` when code cannot; retire entries; set the index cap and the age
   163	  threshold. (claude, codex; opencode's write table has agents authoring decisions with no acceptance step —
   164	  recorded as a gap, not as a merged rule.)
   165	- **Append-only:** `journal/`, `work/<task-id>/events/`, evidence, git history, and an accepted decision's body
   166	  (after acceptance only `status`, `verified_at` and `superseded_by` may change; to change the claim, write a
   167	  new entry). Sensitive material is redacted despite the historical policy.
   168	- **Mutable, but by diff:** `INDEX.md` (generated), current summaries, and fact/lesson bodies.
   169	- **Concurrency:** different file per session and per claim, one writer per file; no two agents edit one file
   170	  (CP4 — opencode's shared per-day file is the dissent).
   171	- **Never** store project knowledge only in an agent's private memory and call it memory.
   172	
   173	## Read rules
   174	
   175	- **At session start (always):** `AGENTS.md`; `memory/INDEX.md`; the `work/` listing and the assigned task's
   176	  `state.md`. Enforce this with a SessionStart hook or an explicit first instruction in `AGENTS.md`, because
   177	  agents skip optional reads. Establish the actual checkout and working-tree state before trusting a handoff.
   178	  (CP6 governs whether the last few session files are also read.)
   179	- **On demand:** an entry body when its `covers` match a path about to be edited (a PreToolUse hook or an
   180	  `AGENTS.md` instruction); filename and text search, or FTS, over `memory/entries/`; `git log -S`, `git blame`
   181	  and PR comments for line-level "why"; a repo map or symbol search generated fresh for the current commit;
   182	  for "why" questions read the decision entry rather than relying on similarity retrieval.
   183	- **Open the source when it matters:** re-read the underlying file, test or commit when a retrieved statement
   184	  will determine an action, and re-read affected memory after an integration or branch change.
   185	- **Never load:** `journal/`; entries marked `superseded` or `retired`, except by following a link from a
   186	  loaded entry; the whole `memory/` tree; a full graph or code-map dump; bulk issue/PR bodies; another agent's
   187	  private transcripts; credentials; retrieved text treated as authority that can override standing instructions.
   188	- **Budget:** the index must stay under a cap and the check script fails the build if it does not. The merged
   189	  answer gives **no number** — claude left the cap, the age threshold and the write-trigger set to the owner
   190	  ("never fill a gap with my own value"). Over the cap, consolidate off the critical path.
   191	
   192	## Staleness and contradiction
   193	
   194	- **Dating:** every entry has an ISO `recorded_at`, an `observed_at`/`verified_at`, and the git revision it was
   195	  verified against. A date alone says nothing about truth (claude, codex).
   196	- **Staleness is computed, not guessed:** a deterministic script (claude's `memory_check.py`; no model call)
   197	  diffs `covers` between `verified_at` and HEAD and shows a `⚠ drifted` flag in `INDEX.md`, which the reader sees
   198	  before relying on the entry. The script never rewrites the entry. Re-verifying updates `verified_at`;
   199	  confirming the entry false sets `status: stale` and starts supersession. `depends_on` links flag downstream
   200	  entries rather than recompute them. (M15, M16; opencode's date-stamped `## Stale as of …` marker is
   201	  compatible but not change-triggered.)
   202	- **Supersession:** never delete. The new entry carries `supersedes: [id]`; the old one gets `status: superseded`
   203	  and `superseded_by`. No LLM and no database (claude, read: Graphiti/Mem0 use the same invalidate-don't-remove
   204	  shape). This covers the question types where the KG beat vector top-k.
   205	- **Contradiction:** if one entry supersedes the other, that is a valid chain. Otherwise both become `contested`
   206	  and the index shows both. The next agent to touch that area adjudicates against code and tests and writes a
   207	  superseding entry, or flags a retire with evidence; if code cannot decide, a human decides. Record the
   208	  resolution and link both earlier assertions. While unresolved, mark the subject disputed and stop only the
   209	  action that depends on choosing a side; independent work continues. **Never average conflicting measurements
   210	  or pick a winner silently** — this is where claude and codex part from opencode's newest-wins rule (CP2).
   211	- **No time-decay deletion:** the decay evidence conflicts in the read material (claude: Selective Forgetting
   212	  removes 9.8% of nodes with token F1 unchanged — C15 — versus EngramRAG naming temporal decay evicting core
   213	  invariants a defect — C16; the source report leaves the two unresolved). Age may only add a flag. The
   214	  threshold is the owner's to set.
   215	- **Consolidation:** periodic, off the critical path, as a proposed PR — merge duplicates, retire dead entries,
   216	  shrink the index — never a silent rewrite.
   217	
   218	## What NOT to build
   219	
   220	Three refusal tests were offered, and they are **given as three, not averaged**: **T-A (codex, model
   221	knowledge, unexecuted):** add a store only for a named, demonstrated failure of the simpler system, with source
   222	traceability, a maintenance owner and an evaluated benefit. **T-B (claude):** git-rebuildable from a fresh clone
   223	with no model call; diff-reviewable before it takes effect; not a second truth needing sync against code and
   224	git; beats the flat-markdown baseline at a matched token budget on this project's own questions; one maker per
   225	field. **T-C (opencode, model knowledge):** does it solve a pain the agent actually feels; does it avoid
   226	external infrastructure; can humans read and verify it. A store is refused if it fails any applicable test.
   227	
   228	| Refused | Refused by | Test it fails, and why |
   229	|---|---|---|
   230	| Vector database as project truth | claude, codex, opencode | T-B (not human-readable, becomes a second truth), T-A (no demonstrated need), T-C (Q3). A derived FTS/vector cache is allowed, never authoritative. |
   231	| Graph database as the store (Graphiti/Zep-style, Neo4j) | claude, codex, opencode | T-A (no demonstrated generic relationship need), T-B (not diff-reviewable; supersession is available as frontmatter links), T-C. claude adds that Mem0 removed graph support from its open-source SDK in v2.0.0 (its own label: reported, third-party). |
   232	| Committed SQLite as the sole or authoritative memory | claude, codex | T-A/T-B — "it adds a required access mechanism where inspectable files already meet the proposed need"; absence must not prevent reading memory. **opencode dissents: CP1.** |
   233	| LLM-chosen ADD/UPDATE/DELETE, or auto-extraction of facts from every turn | claude | T-B (destructive, not diff-reviewable); hallucinations "accumulate at memory extraction and updating, then propagate into answers" (read: REPORT.md s5-F5); raw capture is cheaper and non-inferior at a tight budget (read: FINDINGS-s4 F17/F18). |
   234	| Always-live code graph / auto-synced index | claude | T-B — the project's own benchmark measured 4.3% of distinct edges wrong on a clean rebuild, silently, and a CLI over the same index served stale answers (read: REPORT.md s3-F25/F27); plus the resident-context cost. Generate per commit, as a cache. |
   235	| A giant, automatically growing `MEMORY.md` or whole-tree load | claude, codex, opencode | T-C (readable is not enough — compulsory reading), T-B (unrelated subjects combined); context cost. The index is the only load-at-start item. |
   236	| Parallel agents appending to one shared journal | claude, codex | T-B — overlapping writes and implicit integration; a merge does not establish semantic agreement. **opencode's per-day `sessions/` file is this pattern: CP4.** |
   237	| A complete transcript archive as routine memory | claude, codex, opencode | T-A/T-C — preserves volume without selecting what future work needs; opencode's own read rule refuses full history. |
   238	| Time-based auto-expiry or decay deletion | claude | T-B — it is not reversible and the decay evidence conflicts (C15 vs C16); age may only flag. |
   239	| Agent-private store as project memory | claude, codex, opencode | T-B (invisible to human review), T-C (Q3), and it breaks one-maker-per-field. |
   240	| Cloud KV / external sync as memory | opencode | T-C (Q2 external infrastructure and offline failure mode). |
   241	| A custom memory service before a retrieval failure exists | codex | T-A — benefit and maintenance obligations not established. |
   242	| Ownership files or CODEOWNERS as memory | claude | T-A/T-B — it is review routing, not retrievable memory; the read material stores no such memory role. |
   243	
   244	Each refuser's own caveat is carried forward: claude's tests are its design, not executed; codex "authored this
   245	test and have not executed it", so its refusals mean "not justified as a generic default", not "proved
   246	ineffective"; opencode's three questions are model knowledge. SQLite indexing, semantic retrieval and graph
   247	queries all remain possible additions **once their particular benefit is demonstrated**.
   248	
   249	## Strength of this recommendation
   250	
   251	- **How many merged claims rest on a source an engine actually read.** Of the 24 rows: **19 rest on a source at
   252	  least one engine read**, and **5 rest on `model knowledge` at core** — M1 (the markdown-over-others ranking,
   253	  where the adjacent read supports only git-backed files), M6 (SQLite's derived-only role, where the read
   254	  establishes only that FTS5 exists), M8 (private-store persistence), M22 (the human-readability refusal), and
   255	  M24 (write cadence). None of the model-knowledge labels was upgraded.
   256	- **But the read basis is mostly second-hand.** 13 of the 19 read-backed rows rest on claude's reading of the
   257	  project's own research report (`docs/research/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/REPORT.md` and
   258	  `FINDINGS-s4-retrieval-consolidation.md`), which claude itself says restates `INDEX.md` and "says no number in
   259	  it was independently re-run". Those rows are "an engine read a real file"; they are not primary evidence, and
   260	  where claude gives two conflicting rows (C10/C11, C13/C14, C15/C16, C17/C18, C26/C27) **both are kept here and
   261	  neither is chosen**.
   262	- **Which engines read real material at all.** **claude** read local material: `~/CLAUDE.md`, the project
   263	  `CLAUDE.md`, the Claude Code `MEMORY.md` index, the project's `REPORT.md` and one findings file — and said
   264	  once that it did not use web search. **codex** read primary sources: git-scm.com, sqlite.org/fts5,
   265	  developers.openai.com (retrieval guide and skills/prompts post), code.claude.com/docs/en/memory,
   266	  opencode.ai/docs/rules, learn.chatgpt.com AGENTS.md, arxiv.org/abs/2307.03172, cognitect.com ADR essay, plus
   267	  local files (`CLAUDE.md`, the requirement-check skill). **opencode read nothing**: all 7 of its claims are
   268	  labelled `model knowledge`, it has no `read:` row, and it did not state (as the brief required) that it had no
   269	  web access. It also states a number it did not read — "Does this fit in 64k tokens?" — contrary to the brief's
   270	  evidence standard.
   271	- **opencode is thin and partly off-brief, and is used accordingly.** 7 claims against codex's 26 and claude's
   272	  36; zero sources; it never addresses the cross-agent instruction file at all, although the brief names
   273	  several agents and asks for a generic project; it reports "Confidence: High" from model knowledge alone. Its
   274	  contribution here is therefore a third opinion on **shape** (markdown primary, append-only supersession,
   275	  human-readable, refuse stores without a felt need) plus the two genuine disagreements CP1 and CP2 — not
   276	  evidence.
   277	- **Shared blind spots.** No engine produced an operational definition of "work well" (codex flags that it
   278	  "fails weak-words and can-it-fail checks" and leaves it open); no engine ran memory-with against
   279	  memory-without on a real project; the index size cap, the age threshold and the write-trigger set are the
   280	  owner's to set and no engine would invent them; and agreement between claude and codex is partly agreement
   281	  between one secondary report and one set of vendor docs reasoning, not two independent measurements.
   282	
   283	## Digest
   284	
   285	- **Answer.** Git-versioned markdown with typed, dated, evidence-bearing entries and explicit supersession is
   286	  the source of truth; a generated index plus the task state file are the only start-up reads; SQLite/FTS,
   287	  vector and code-graph stores are gitignored derived caches; no store, DB, context window or private agent
   288	  memory is authoritative.
   289	- **Confidence.** Medium in the shape (19 of 24 merged claims rest on a source an engine read); low in every
   290	  number, because the strongest numbers come from the project's own secondary report.
   291	- **Cautions.** Seven points are contested: SQLite's role, newest-wins, where memory lives, the shared session
   292	  file, private-store persistence, the session-start read set, and write cadence. opencode read nothing and
   293	  never answered the cross-agent instruction question.
   294	- **Gaps.** "Work well" was never made operational; no with/without measurement; the index cap, age threshold
   295	  and write triggers are left to the owner.
   296	- **Decision it triggers.** Adopt the markdown-and-index baseline on one project, set the index cap, and run a
   297	  with-memory against without-memory comparison on about a dozen recorded past tasks before any derived or
   298	  additional store is adopted.

    19	  has 0 stars, size 0, and GitHub answers "This repository is empty." for both contents and commits (API calls on
