    28	## F-list
    29	
    30	### Q1. Which questions does a vector index answer better than grep over `entries/` plus `INDEX.md`?
    31	
    32	**F1 (CONFIRMED) The structured-memory paper that s3-F20/F34 cite: the baseline is `mem0`, and the large gaps are about
    33	enumerating answers, not about vectors versus keywords.** MOOSEDev (arXiv 2608.13662, submitted 2026-08-13, one author,
    34	a vendor paper: the author's company sells the system) compared five conditions on 835 typed records (553 mem0
    35	memories). Its table, with the baseline mem0 (vector memory) and `B1-notes` (documentation search over flat files):
    36	
    37	| Task class | typed graph | mem0 (vector) | notes search |
    38	|---|---|---|---|
    39	| Set completeness | 1.00 | 0.18 | 0.08 |
    40	| Negation (absence) | 0.98 | 0.06 | 0.00 |
    41	| Supersession traversal | 0.98 | 0.27 | 0.12 |
    42	
    43	Quote: "It is not caused by incomplete ingestion: B1-mem0 contains the relevant facts, but its retrieval returns a limited
    44	ranked slice, so it cannot enumerate a complete set, establish an absence, or traverse relationships." And on plain
    45	relevance: "On simple relevance the graph reached coverage 0.82 at roughly 35k agent tokens, whereas mem0 scored between
    46	0.67 and 0.90 at roughly 40k." URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13662 (full text https://arxiv.org/html/2608.13662).
    47	Two points that change how s3-F20 reads:
    48	- the plain notes search (0.08 / 0.00 / 0.12) did worse than the vector tool, so the failure is "a top-k slice cannot
    49	  enumerate", which a vector index and a keyword search share (INFERRED from the table);
    50	- mem0 is not a bare embedding index (it extracts "memories" from the documentation with "its own native capture
    51	  method"), so "vector top-k" in s3-F20 stands for mem0, not for an embedding index over unchanged files.
    52	The paper reports no vector index over the unchanged markdown and no hybrid condition.
    53	
    54	**F2 (CONFIRMED) Same paper, on supersession specifically.** "On the reversal tested, B1-mem0 tied the graph at 100%
    55	where a cold agent scored 0.00; the free-text B1-rag condition, on the one pair where the superseded record outranks the
    56	current one and with that content pushed into the prompt, was current in only 1 of 13 trials (8%)." `B1-rag` is BM25.
    57	So superseded-record failure is shown for BM25, not for vectors, on one reversal pair; the vector tool was fine on the
    58	reversal tested. Four reversal pairs in all, so n is tiny. Same URL as F1.
    59	
    60	**F3 (CONFIRMED) On conversational agent memory (LongMemEval), grep beat vector retrieval when hits were returned
    61	inline.** "Is Grep All You Need? How Agent Harnesses Reshape Agentic Search", arXiv 2605.15184 (2026-05-14, PwC
    62	authors), 116-question LongMemEval-S sample, four harnesses including Claude Code and Codex. Quote: "With inline
    63	delivery, lexical search is uniformly stronger than dense retrieval: inline grep exceeds inline vector for every
    64	harness-model pair." Examples: Chronos + Claude Opus 4.6 93.1 vs 83.6; Codex + GPT-5.4 93.1 vs 75.9; narrowest Claude
    65	Code + Opus 4.6 76.7 vs 75.0. Not uniform: with file-based ("programmatic") delivery "programmatic vector exceeds
    66	programmatic grep on five of ten harness-model pairs". The authors' own limit: "in domains where evidence is rarely
    67	literal (e.g., scientific synthesis over paraphrased abstracts, visual-heavy documents, or code semantics), dense
    68	retrieval and hybrid routing may look different." This is chat history, not decisions/facts files, and not code.
    69	URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15184 (HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2605.15184).
    70	
    71	**F4 (CONFIRMED) On a 500,000-document enterprise corpus, BM25 beat dense vector search; an agent with shell tools was in
    72	between.** EnterpriseRAG-Bench, arXiv 2605.05253 (2026), 500 questions, top-10 retrieval, vector = OpenAI
    73	text-embedding-3-large over Qdrant (an API model, not local). Table 6: correctness BM25 68.8 / vector 51.4 / bash agent
    74	60.6; document recall 68.4 / 46.0 / 55.8. Per type: "Semantic (125)" correctness 44.8 / 32.8 / 42.4; "Conflicting Info
    75	(20)" 90.0 / 60.0 / 80.0; "Completeness (20)" recall 46.5 / 33.4 / 59.0. Quote: "Vector search underperforms
    76	expectations even on semantic questions (32.8% correctness, 24.8% recall), the category explicitly designed to favor
    77	embedding-based retrieval by limiting keyword overlap." No hybrid condition. URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05253
    78	(HTML https://arxiv.org/html/2605.05253).
    79	
    80	**F5 (CONFIRMED) Code: dense retrieval beats BM25 for issue localization, and a lexical-only agent is near graph/semantic
    81	baselines for code completion.**
    82	- SpIDER (arXiv 2512.16956, 2025-12-18): "While dense embedding approaches can outperform BM25 by large margins, both
    83	  ignore the graph-structured characteristics of the codebase." Task: locating the functions an issue touches in SWE-style
    84	  repositories (Python, Java, JavaScript, TypeScript). I did not extract a BM25-vs-dense number from its tables.
    85	- "Better Call Grep" (arXiv 2601.23254, 2026-01-30): "Despite its simplicity, Naive GrepRAG achieves performance
    86	  comparable to sophisticated graph-based baselines." Task: repository-level code completion.
    87	URLs: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.16956, https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23254. Neither tests entries-style project memory.
    88	
    89	**F6 (CONFIRMED, vendor) Cursor: semantic search helps agents, more so in large codebases; the best result is grep plus
    90	semantic search.** Cursor blog "Improving agent with semantic search", 2025-11-06: "semantic search significantly
    91	improves agent performance, especially over large codebases: Achieving on average 12.5% higher accuracy in answering
    92	questions (6.5%-23.5% depending on the model)"; "agent code retention increases by 0.3% when semantic search is
    93	available. This effect increases to 2.6% on large codebases with 1,000 files or more"; "Our agent makes heavy use of
    94	grep as well as semantic search, and the combination of these two leads to the best outcomes." Caveats: Cursor's own
    95	trained embedding model, their private Context Bench, no absolute scores published, code (not decisions/lessons).
    96	URL: https://cursor.com/blog/semsearch.
    97	
    98	**F7 (CONFIRMED) Anthropic: hybrid beats embeddings alone, because embeddings miss exact matches.** "Introducing
    99	Contextual Retrieval", 2024-09-19: "While embedding models excel at capturing semantic relationships, they can miss
   100	crucial exact matches"; BM25 "is particularly effective for queries that include unique identifiers or technical terms";
   101	top-20 failure rate 5.7% with embeddings alone, 2.9% with contextual embeddings plus contextual BM25, 1.9% with reranking.
   102	Datasets include codebases. Note this is a 2024 chunk-RAG test with API embeddings, and the baseline 5.7% is a
   103	chunk-level failure rate, not an agent-task result. URL: https://www.anthropic.com/news/contextual-retrieval.
   104	
   105	**F8 (CONFIRMED) Anthropic on when not to retrieve at all.** Same page: "If your knowledge base is smaller than 200,000
   106	tokens (about 500 pages of material), you can just include the entire knowledge base in the prompt that you give the
   107	model, with no need for RAG or similar methods." Written in 2024; the context-size figure has since moved, but I opened
   108	no newer Anthropic statement.
   109	
   110	**F9 (CONFIRMED) General-purpose evidence that BM25 is a hard baseline.** BEIR (arXiv 2104.08663, 2021): "BM25 is a robust
   111	baseline and re-ranking and late-interaction-based models on average achieve the best zero-shot performances ... dense
   112	and sparse-retrieval models are computationally more efficient but often underperform other approaches". URL:
   113	https://arxiv.org/abs/2104.08663. Old (2021) models; included because newer entries (F4) point the same way.
   114	
   115	**F10 (UNPROVEN) A homelab report that adding a sparse (keyword) retriever and a reranker to a vector-only agent-memory
   116	stack moved LoCoMo from 63% to 80%.** Dev.to post, 2026-08-13, one practitioner, self-run: "Pure vector search got my
   117	agent memory stack to 63% on LoCoMo. Adding a sparse retriever and a reranker that runs on a card I already owned pushed
   118	it to 80%." Also: swapping to a higher-ranked embedding model moved 63% to 65%. The failures were "lexically specific":
   119	"proper nouns get averaged into oblivion by dense embeddings". Secondary, not replicated; the code/benchmark were not
   120	opened. URL: https://dev.to/futhgar/i-benchmarked-my-homelab-memory-stack-hybrid-search-local-reranker-took-locomo-from-63-to-80-4pbp.
   121	
   122	**What the confirmed evidence says about the question (INFERRED from F1-F9):** the evidence that a vector index beats
   123	grep/keyword search is for (a) code at scale with a trained embedding model (F5, F6, vendor) and (b) paraphrase-style
   124	relevance. For decision/fact/lesson files the only direct head-to-head (F1) found "largely equivalent" relevance and
   125	found the big differences on enumeration, absence and supersession, where plain keyword search was no better. The
   126	question class a vector index is claimed to add is "find an entry by meaning when the words differ"; I found no measured
   127	instance of that on a corpus shaped like `entries/`. That is a GAP, listed below.
   128	
   129	### Q2. When does it start paying off?
   130	
   131	**F11 (GAP) No measurement of a crossover for a markdown project-memory corpus.** I looked for corpus size, query type,
   132	token cost and accuracy at which vector/hybrid starts beating grep/FTS over decision-style notes and found none. What
   133	exists are bounds from other corpora:
   134	- Below about 200k tokens retrieval is not needed at all (F8, Anthropic, 2024).
   135	- In a 116-question conversational test grep led inline, and the order flipped with more distractor sessions on some
   136	  harness/model pairs and not others (F3 Experiment 2: e.g. Gemini CLI + Pro vector ahead at every size, 89.7 vs 78.5
   137	  at full; Claude Code + Opus grep ahead at every size): "the grep-vector ordering is not preserved under scaling ...
   138	  but the shape of that non-preservation still depends on which stack is observed." So the crossover depends on the
   139	  harness, not just on size.
   140	- MOOSEDev's own scale study (50 to 634 records): "We had expected the structured advantage to compound as the store
   141	  grew, but it did not ... the slope's confidence interval spans zero" (F1 URL). It tested a graph against mem0, not
   142	  vector against grep.
   143	- Cursor ties the benefit to repository size ("1,000 files or more", F6) for code.
   144	
   145	**F12 (INFERRED from F8 and the design) Scale arithmetic for this project.** The design keeps `INDEX.md` to "one line per
   146	active entry" under a size limit EJ has not set (SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md line 126). If entries average ~300-500 tokens (my
   147	assumption, not measured; no entries exist), 200k tokens is on the order of 400-650 entries. Below that, F8 says reading
   148	everything is an option; the session-start read of `INDEX.md` plus grep already covers it. The design reads whole files
   149	only on demand, so F8 is a bound on the cost of "read it all", not a rule.
   150	
   151	**F13 (CONFIRMED) How others measured their own need before adopting a retrieval component (these are the methods a
   152	project could copy to "tell"; the adaptation is INFERRED).**
   153	- Zep (vendor blog, 2026-07-16): a gold set of 5,954 "production-faithful recall queries", Recall@10 and nDCG@10, "paired
   154	  bootstrap 95% confidence intervals plus Wilcoxon significance tests", exact cosine search so no approximate-index
   155	  effect. Their own caveat: "relevant items derive from the query's own conversation, so absolute recall is optimistic
   156	  and the relative ordering between models is the signal." Zep itself uses "semantic similarity and BM25 full-text search
   157	  ... fused by reciprocal rank fusion". URL: https://www.getzep.com/blog/evaluating-nemotron-3-embed-for-agent-memory/.
   158	  Result worth noting: Recall@10 for four embedding models spanned 0.617-0.663 on three tasks, i.e. choice of model moved
   159	  recall by 1-4 points.
   160	- MOOSEDev pre-registered question classes and graded with a strict LLM judge (F1).
   161	- Cursor ran an online A/B with and without the tool (F6).
   162	- qmd ships `qmd bench <fixture.json>` to score BM25, vector and hybrid backends on your own fixture. Its README: the
   163	  example fixture "typically shows bm25 ~0.50, vector ~0.70, and hybrid/full ~1.00" (a sample fixture, not evidence).
   164	  URL: https://github.com/tobi/qmd (pushed 2026-10-06).
   165	INFERRED from these: the sort of queries that would justify a vector index are ones where the entry exists, the asker's
   166	words share no term with it, and grep, FTS and `INDEX.md` titles all miss. A project could count those from its own
   167	sessions (misses that were later answered by a different route) or from a labelled query set of its own. No source I
   168	opened defines a threshold for how many such misses justify an index. That threshold would be EJ's, not a finding.
   169	
   170	### Q3. Current tools that build such an index from files that stay the source of truth
   171	
   172	**F14 (CONFIRMED) qmd (tobi/qmd), MIT, 30,340 stars, pushed 2026-10-06.** "An on-device search engine for everything you
   173	need to remember." BM25 plus vector plus LLM reranking, "all running locally via node-llama-cpp with GGUF models"; stores
   174	a single SQLite file (`~/.cache/qmd/index.sqlite`) with an FTS5 table and a sqlite-vec table; chunks ~900 tokens with 15%
   175	overlap; models: `embeddinggemma-300M-Q8_0` ~300MB (default embeddings), `qwen3-reranker-0.6b-q8_0` ~640MB,
   176	`qmd-query-expansion-1.7B-q4_k_m` ~1.1GB. Rebuild: `qmd update` rescans files and reports "indexed, updated, unchanged,
   177	removed, needsEmbedding"; `qmd embed` makes vectors as a separate step; changing model needs `qmd embed -f` because
   178	"vectors are not cross-compatible between models"; `QMD_EMBED_MODEL` can point at Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B GGUF. Node 22.22.1
   179	is installed here; bun is not (README gives `npm install -g @tobilu/qmd`). Rebuild time and index size on disk: GAP, the
   180	README gives none. URL: https://github.com/tobi/qmd.
   181	
   182	**F15 (CONFIRMED) Basic Memory (basicmachines-co/basic-memory), AGPL-3.0, 4,147 stars, pushed 2026-10-12.** Markdown files
   183	stay the source: "Just files plus a local SQLite index. No servers required." Semantic search uses FastEmbed
   184	(`BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5`, 384 dimensions, "no API key, no network calls, no cost") with `sqlite-vec` on SQLite; "Hybrid
   185	full-text + vector ranking"; rebuild by `bm reindex` ("`--embeddings` rebuilds derived vectors from database entities; it
   186	does not discover new files on disk"), so the vector rebuild and the file scan are separate steps. Switching model needs
   187	`bm reindex --embeddings`. Rebuild time and size: GAP. URL: https://github.com/basicmachines-co/basic-memory
   188	(docs/semantic-search.md). License AGPL is a constraint if code were reused (INFERRED; not a legal reading).
   189	
   190	**F16 (CONFIRMED, vendor claim) Claude Context (zilliztech/claude-context), MIT, 12,602 stars, pushed 2026-07-14.** Code

   225	download GBs of PyTorch dependencies, and instead use the ONNX Runtime"; GPU via `pip install fastembed-gpu`; PyPI
   226	0.9.0 (2026-10-07). Hugging Face text-embeddings-inference README lists Qwen3 and Gemma3 as supported, with "Token based
   227	dynamic batching" and Flash Attention (a server, not a library). sentence-transformers 6.1.0 (2026-09-18) needs PyTorch.

   229	llama.cpp/ollama on PATH).
   230	
   231	### Q4. How it fails
   232	
   233	**F20 (CONFIRMED) Negation is a documented weakness of bi-encoders.** NevIR (arXiv 2305.07614, 2023, EACL 2024): "cross-
   234	encoders perform best, followed by late-interaction models, and in last place are bi-encoder and sparse neural
   235	architectures. We find that most information retrieval models (including SOTA ones) do not consider negation, performing
   236	the same or worse than a random ranking." URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.07614. It tests ranking two documents that
   237	differ only by a negation, with 2023 models; I found no newer measurement for current embedding models (listed below).
   238	For project memory this matters for entries such as "do not use X" next to "use X" (INFERRED).
   239	
   240	**F21 (CONFIRMED + INFERRED) Exact identifiers and rare terms.** Anthropic (F7): "they can miss crucial exact matches".
   241	EnterpriseRAG-Bench authors give a reason for vector's weak showing: "embedding models are trained on public corpora
   242	(web pages, Wikipedia, forums) and have limited exposure to enterprise-specific vocabulary such as project codenames"
   243	(quoted from the paper's Section 6.1, sentence continues "and CRM records, and conversational styles typical of internal messaging"; see F4 URL). The homelab report (F10, UNPROVEN)
   244	gives the concrete case: "find me the exact string TICKET-4471". For this project, entry ids (`D-`, `F-`, `L-`, `R-`) and
   245	`covers:` code paths are exact strings (INFERRED), which is the case where F7 and F10 say keyword search is the better tool.
   246	I found no measurement on code-symbol lookup specifically (GAP).
   247	
   248	**F22 (CONFIRMED + INFERRED) Superseded entries returned as if current.** Evidence is thin and points two ways:
   249	- F2: the BM25 free-text condition returned the superseded record "on the one pair where the superseded record outranks the
   250	  current one", current in 8% of trials; the vector tool was current on the reversal it was tested on.
   251	- F4: "Conflicting Info (20)": vector 60.0 correctness against BM25 90.0 and bash agent 80.0 (conflict handling over a
   252	  500,000-document corpus).
   253	Whether a superseded entry ranks above its replacement is a property of the text, not of the retriever (INFERRED from F2).
   254	In this design an index over `entries/` would hold whatever files are in the directory unless it filters on the `status`
   255	field; `INDEX.md` lists "active" entries only (SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md line 100). A vector index built over `entries/`
   256	without that filter would return superseded entries; one built over the same active set as `INDEX.md` would not
   257	(INFERRED; no source tested this).
   258	
   259	**F23 (CONFIRMED) Top-k cannot enumerate or prove absence.** F1 quote: it "cannot enumerate a complete set, establish an
   260	absence, or traverse relationships". Applies to any ranked top-k, vector or keyword (F1's notes-search column).
   261	
   262	**F24 (CONFIRMED) Drift between files and vectors; what the tools do about it.**
   263	- qmd: `update` reports "unchanged" and "removed" counts and "needsEmbedding" (new text not yet embedded); `embed` is a
   264	  second step, so between the two steps changed files have no current vectors (INFERRED from F14's API). A model change
   265	  invalidates every vector and needs `embed -f`.
   266	- Basic Memory: `bm reindex --embeddings` "does not discover new files on disk" (F15), i.e. two rebuild paths that can
   267	  disagree if one is skipped.
   268	- Claude Context: Merkle-tree change detection (F16, vendor claim).
   269	- No tool README I opened states a "never stale" guarantee for the vector side, and none exposes a "stale" flag on a
   270	  returned hit (INFERRED: I did not find one).
   271	- The code-graph analogue is a measured silent drift (s3-F25: "4.3% of distinct edges were wrong"; s3-F27: a CLI "returned
   272	  'No results found'" for a newly added symbol). I found no equivalent measurement for a vector index from files (GAP).
   273	  Practitioner advice (hash content not mtime; delete orphan chunks; canary edits; full re-embed on model change) is
   274	  UNPROVEN, from blogs such as https://tianpan.co/blog/2026-06-02-the-vector-index-whose-source-updates-never-reached-the-embeddings
   275	  and https://dev.to/futhgar/silent-drift-why-re-embedding-only-on-count-changes-rots-your-semantic-index-3k6h
   276	  (search-result descriptions only; I did not open them).
   277	
   278	**F25 (UNPROVEN) Claude Code dropping its vector index.** Many secondary write-ups repeat that a Claude Code lead said
   279	"Early versions of Claude Code used RAG + a local vector db, but we found pretty quickly that agentic search generally
   280	works better", citing staleness and security of an index. I could not open the primary post. Not relied on.
   281	
   282	### Q5. What fits here: smallest working versions (options, no choice)
   283	
   284	Machine: Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5, numpy 2.4.3, RTX 4070 Ti SUPER 16 GB, Node 22.22.1, ~700 GB free; no
   285	embedding runtime installed. The question for the corpus is that a project-memory `entries/` directory of hundreds of
   286	files is a few thousand vectors at most (INFERRED from F12 and 300-1,000 token chunks); brute-force search over that is
   287	trivial (author's sqlite-vec figure, F17, is 33ms for 1,000,000 x 128-d).
   288	
   289	- **Option 0: none.** `INDEX.md` + grep (+ the SQLite FTS cache from the sibling research). Supported by F1 (relevance
   290	  parity, no vector gain on enumeration/supersession), F3, F4, F8, F12. Cost: zero new tools.
   291	- **Option A: static embeddings, no GPU.** `pip install model2vec` + numpy; `potion-base-8M` (7.6M params, ~8-30 MB, F18);
   292	  store a `.npy` matrix plus a JSON of per-file content hashes under `.cache/`; cosine by numpy. Rebuild = re-embed the
   293	  files whose hash changed (INFERRED design detail). Quality of static models on decision-style text: GAP; the model
   294	  card reports MTEB averages that I did not extract.
   295	- **Option B: small ONNX model, CPU or GPU.** `pip install fastembed` (or `fastembed-gpu`) with `bge-small-en-v1.5`
   296	  (33M params, 384 dims, F18/F19), vectors in a numpy file or in `sqlite-vec` (F17, pre-v1, brute force). This is the
   297	  stack Basic Memory ships by default (F15).
   298	- **Option C: larger model on the GPU.** `sentence-transformers` (needs PyTorch + CUDA wheels) or llama.cpp/TEI serving
   299	  `Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B` (1.2 GB f16, 639 MB Q8_0 GGUF, code retrieval stated in its card) or `embeddinggemma-300m`
   300	  (gated, Gemma terms). Gives the bigger models; adds a heavy dependency for a few thousand vectors (INFERRED). The 1-4
   301	  point recall spread among models in Zep's test (F13) is the only comparison I have between model sizes, and it is not on
   302	  this kind of text.
   303	- **Option D: adopt an existing tool over the markdown.** `qmd` (Node, `npm install -g @tobilu/qmd`, FTS5 + sqlite-vec +
   304	  reranker, ~2 GB of models, F14; it would also supply the FTS side) or Basic Memory (AGPL, F15, which wants its own note
   305	  format). Both would be a new dependency whose index format, chunking and file layout are theirs. Note qmd's embed step
   306	  is separate from its update step (F24).
   307	- **Hybrid in any option.** Every option above that adds vectors is only evidenced to help in combination with keyword
   308	  search (F7, F10, F13, qmd's own pipeline); vector-only was the worst arm in F3, F4. A hybrid needs a fusion step
   309	  (qmd and Zep use reciprocal rank fusion, `score = sum(1/(k+rank+1))`, k=60 in qmd's README).
   310	- **Filters any option would need to honour (INFERRED from the design):** index only active entries (the same set as
   311	  `INDEX.md`), carry `covers:` and `status` as metadata, store the content hash and the embedding-model id with every
   312	  vector, and treat a missing or mismatched cache as "absent" so memory still reads without it (M4/M6).
   313	
   314	## Not found

   318	- A corpus-size or query-type crossover at which a vector index beats grep for such notes (F11).

   323	  index size for qmd, Basic Memory and sqlite-vec on a corpus of a few hundred notes.

   329	
   330	- Claude Context's own evaluation directory (the "~40% token reduction" method) and its reranking/chunking details.
   331	- SpIDER's per-language BM25-vs-dense tables and whether BM25 wins anywhere; the index-cost tables of "Better Call Grep"; READ (arXiv 2608.06305), FluctlightDB (2608.12365) and RARG (2607.24223) beyond their abstracts. READ reports that
   332	  BM25 was "statistically indistinguishable" from its agent on 51 financial-report questions (abstract only; not
   333	  project memory).
   334	- LongMemEval and LoCoMo original papers, and the Appendix A per-category (e.g. "knowledge-update") results of F3.
   335	- Other tools: Chroma, LanceDB, txtai, LEANN, mcp-server-qdrant, Mem0 itself, Zep/Graphiti as a service, sqlite-vss.
   336	- Other local embedding models (e.g. Nemotron 3 Embed 1B/8B, pplx-embed, multilingual-e5, snowflake-arctic) and their
   337	  MTEB scores; MTEB/CoIR code-retrieval leaderboards.
   338	- Re-rankers as a separate stage (cross-encoders) and their cost on this machine.
   339	- Licensing of the Gemma terms for use in this project; AGPL implications for Basic Memory (not a legal reading).
   340	- Whether sqlite-vec 0.1.9 loads on this machine's Python/SQLite build (the loader attribute exists; nothing was
   341	  installed or run). No code or embedding job was run for this note.
   342	- The s3-F20 entry in `docs/research/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/` was compared with the paper only through the paper
   343	  text above; the r2 file's other claims were not re-verified.
   127	**F17 — INFERRED from F1 and F10.** Result granularity. Chunking by heading (F10) returns a section; one-file-per-entry (the design) could index one row per file. Which is right is not settled by any source found.
   128	
   129	**F18 — INFERRED from s3-F20/F34 and F6.** Where the evidence says a richer store helps and where it does not: MOOSEDev's advantage is on supersession and set questions, with relevance recall and token cost "largely equivalent" (s3-F34); a lexical store is not shown to win on relevance either. So for this project the case, if any, rests on the header-field questions in F3, not on search quality.
   130	
   131	### 5. What fits here (brief item 5) — options, no choice
   132	
   133	The machine has Python 3.12 and SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5 (verified 2026-10-12), `rg` and `grep`. Nothing to install.
   134	
   135	- **Option A — no cache.** `INDEX.md` plus `rg` over `entries/`, and a short stdlib Python script that reads frontmatter for the structured questions (F13 shows 0.1 s at 10,000 entries). Cost: no ranking, no snippets.
   136	- **Option B — smallest FTS cache.** One stdlib `sqlite3` script: full rebuild into a temp file, `os.replace` over `project-memory/.cache/index.sqlite`; one FTS5 table (default tokenizer, one row per entry file, header fields as `UNINDEXED` columns) and optionally a header-fields table; rebuild when the newest file mtime is newer than the index, as in F9; fall back to `rg` if the file or FTS5 is missing. Measured rebuild 0.53 s and 22 MB at 10,000 synthetic entries (F13); the design's M6 holds because absence changes nothing for readers.
   137	- **Option C — B plus link table.** Adds `depends_on` / `supersedes` / `superseded_by` rows for backlink, orphan and broken-link queries (as the harness `links` command in F9). This is the structured-query use; the drift-check script named in the design as "not decided" could read it, but that is a design matter, not found here.
   138	- **Choices a design must still make** (listed, not decided): tokenizer (F16), row grain (F17), staleness test (mtime as in F9, or hash as in F11), who triggers a rebuild, whether query and rebuild share one command (F9 does).
   139	
   140	## Not found
   141	
   142	- Any docs, repo or package for a `hollowfern` markdown indexer, and therefore its default tokenizer (planted question: GAP).
   143	- Any measurement of FTS5 or BM25 against grep for an agent working on a small (hundreds to a few thousand) corpus of short memory entries, on accuracy or tokens.
   144	- Any measurement of an agent answering header-field or supersession questions with SQL against with grep or a script over frontmatter.
   145	- A measured stale-answer or drift rate for any FTS5-over-markdown tool.
   146	- Index size and rebuild time in the published sources, apart from F9 ("under a second" at about 1.4k files) and F10 (under half a second at 2,647 chunks); no source gave an on-disk size.
   147	- A primary source for the claim that Claude Code's default search is grep without an index (F8).

   149	## Not checked
   150	
   151	- The raw page text of any cited page (all reads went through WebFetch extracts); the full text of "BM25 Wins at Scale" beyond the extracts, and its per-tier table.
   152	- The last 71,799 characters of the FTS5 reference page (read to character 100,000 of 171,799), which would hold the details of `trigram`, `porter`, contentless tables and `detail=`.
   153	- `metafunctor` post (HTTP 503), sqlite-memory's code, qmd's source, Basic Memory's code and its FTS usage.
   154	- SQLite FTS5 tools outside the markdown-agent-memory space (for example Obsidian or static-site search), and tools older than 2025.
   155	- Actual `grep` or `rg` token cost to an agent (output size returned into context) against an FTS5 `snippet()` result; not measured.
   156	- Whether Windows or macOS builds of SQLite ship FTS5 (irrelevant on this machine).
   157	- The vector index and the code graph (other nodes' stores).
     1	# RECOMMENDATION — the memory system a software project should have
     2	
     3	Join of node 4. Inputs (read in full): `nodes/claude.stdout.md` (Claude Sonnet 5.5, 36 claims),
     4	`nodes/codex.stdout.md` (Codex, 26 claims), `nodes/opencode.stdout.md` (MiniMax-M2.5, 7 claims).
     5	Merged claim ids `M1…M24` are this node's labels; no claim appears here that an engine did not make.
     6	Engine-attribution key: **claude** / **codex** / **opencode**. A verdict is `AGREED` (all three assert it),
     7	`MAJORITY` (two assert it, the third is silent), `CONTESTED` (engines assert incompatible positions),
     8	`SINGLE` (one asserts it, the others are silent).
     9	
    10	## Recommendation
    11	
    12	Keep the project's memory as reviewed markdown, version-controlled with the code, as the single source of
    13	truth: short typed entries (decision, fact, lesson, runbook) that each carry an id, kind, author, recorded and
    14	verified dates, the code paths or symbols they cover, their evidence, and explicit supersession links — never
    15	deleted, only superseded. One canonical cross-agent instruction file (`AGENTS.md`, with thin per-tool adapters)
    16	plus a small generated index and the assigned task's state file are the only things read at session start;
    17	everything else is fetched on demand by filename or text search, or by following links, and a retrieved
    18	statement is checked against its primary source before it determines an action. Staleness is computed
    19	deterministically from changes to the code an entry covers, not from age; contradicting entries are kept side
    20	by side, marked disputed, checked against code and evidence, and escalated to a human maintainer when code
    21	cannot decide; each writer gets its own file. SQLite/FTS, vector search and code graphs are permitted only as
    22	gitignored, rebuildable derived caches — no vector, graph, database, context window or agent-private store is
    23	ever the source of truth, and any new store must first answer a named, demonstrated failure of the
    24	markdown-and-search baseline on this project's own tasks.
    25	
    26	## Support table
    27	
    28	| # | claim | engines agreeing (claude/codex/opencode) | strongest basis of any engine (read: <source> / model knowledge) | verdict |
    29	|---|---|---|---|---|
    30	| M1 | Markdown files kept with the code are the canonical shared project memory. | claude, codex, opencode | **model knowledge** for the substrate ranking in all three; the adjacent read is claude `read: docs/research/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/REPORT.md` s1-F7/F8 (a production memory system is a git-backed filesystem), which supports git-backed files, not markdown-over-others | AGREED |
    31	| M2 | Memory is version-controlled with the code so a change is reviewed as a diff and a fresh clone can rebuild it. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://git-scm.com/book/en/v2/Git-Branching-Basic-Branching-and-Merging** (branch/merge mechanics) | MAJORITY |

    33	| M4 | A vector index is refused as the source of truth; at most a derived, rebuildable index, and only when the project's own queries justify it. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F20, s3-F34** (a KG store beat vector top-k on supersession/negation, 6–27% surfaced; relevance recall and token cost "largely equivalent"); also codex `read: https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/retrieval` | AGREED |

    40	| M11 | Every durable entry carries id, kind, author, recorded/observed/verified dates, scope, source, status and dependency/supersession links. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://cognitect.com/blog/2011/11/15/documenting-architecture-decisions** (decision-record shape); the field list itself is design in both | MAJORITY |
    41	| M12 | A human maintainer accepts policy and significant decisions; agents draft them and record observations and handoffs. | claude, codex | **read: claude — ~/CLAUDE.md Rules 7 and 12** ("every artifact has one maker"); codex `read: /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games/CLAUDE.md` (a project-specific rule, not a general source) | MAJORITY |
    42	| M13 | Never delete a memory entry; supersede it with an explicit link and keep the old body. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s1-F4 and FINDINGS-s4 F5–F7** (Graphiti invalidates the older edge; Mem0 marks superseded edges invalid "rather than physically removing") | AGREED |
    43	| M14 | Disagreeing entries are kept as separate assertions, marked disputed, checked against primary evidence or code, then escalated to the authorized maintainer; a timestamp, majority or clean merge is not enough to pick a winner. | claude, codex | **read: claude — ~/CLAUDE.md Rule 12** ("don't blend conflicting patterns… flag the other"); codex's C22 is **model knowledge** | CONTESTED (opencode: read-time rule "prefer the newest non-stale entry") |

    45	| M16 | The staleness check is deterministic and makes no model call. | claude | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F4** (C5) | SINGLE |
    46	| M17 | At session start an agent reads the standing instructions, a small index or state file, and the material for its assigned task. | claude, codex | **read: codex — https://developers.openai.com/blog/rethinking-skills-and-prompts-for-gpt-6-astra** (read documents according to the task) | CONTESTED (opencode: session-start reads are `context.md`, active tickets in `state.sqlite` and the last 3 `sessions/` files — no standing instructions and no index) |
    47	| M18 | Never auto-load the whole memory tree, the full session history, or a whole-graph dump. | claude, codex, opencode | **read: claude — REPORT.md s3-F24, s3-F32** (67k vs 18k resident tokens; ~63K vs ~41K) | AGREED |

    55	**M17 correction (repair 2).** M17 is **`CONTESTED`**, not `MAJORITY`: opencode does not merely omit the
    56	session-start read set — it asserts a different one: `context.md`, active tickets in `state.sqlite`, and the last
    57	three `sessions/` files, with no standing instructions and no index (`nodes/opencode.stdout.md:57-59`); the same
    58	split is CP6 below. M17 is therefore credited to **claude and codex** only (claude: `nodes/claude.stdout.md:104-107`;
    59	codex: `nodes/codex.stdout.md:30`, `:74`).
    60	
    61	## Contested points
    62	
    63	**CP1 — The role of SQLite.**
    64	- **opencode:** SQLite *is* the structured state store — "structured state tracking (ticket status, session logs, entity relationships)", written to `.ai/state.sqlite` inside the repo and queried at session start for `status != done`. Basis: **model knowledge** (C2), justified as "lightweight, queryable, no external service needed, atomic writes".
    65	- **claude and codex:** SQLite is at most a **derived, gitignored, rebuildable cache** and never authoritative. claude places it at `.cache/` "rebuilt from the tree"; codex places it at `.cache/project-memory/index.sqlite` gitignored and requires that "its absence must not prevent reading authoritative memory", refusing a committed SQLite as sole memory because "it adds a required access mechanism where inspectable files already meet the proposed need". Basis: codex `read: https://sqlite.org/fts5.html` plus its **model-knowledge** recommendation; claude's T1/T2/T4 design tests.
    66	- **unresolved.** Nothing in the three files measures a committed binary state store against markdown state files. What would settle it: on one real project, track ticket/session state both ways and compare merge-conflict rate on the binary, the cost of querying state without the DB (does a reader still recover?), and the human reviewability of a `state.sqlite` diff.
    67	
    68	**CP2 — Contradiction resolution: newest-wins vs disputed-and-escalate.**
    69	- **opencode:** "On read, agent should check for `## Superseded` / `## Stale` markers and **prefer the newest non-stale entry**." Basis: **model knowledge** (C7, staleness section).
    70	- **claude and codex:** newest does not win. claude: "**Why not newest wins.** Graphiti's 'new wins' is write-time and LLM-driven"; both entries become `contested`, the next agent adjudicates against code and tests, a human decides if code cannot. Basis: **read: FINDINGS-s4 F15, F16** — a deterministic latest-wins pipeline scored far above the stores on MemoryAgentBench single-hop (78.0%/94.8% vs Zep 7.0%, Mem0 18.0%) but only tied LLM judgment on LongMemEval knowledge-update (57.8% vs 64.4%, n=45); claude gives both rows and refuses to pick. codex: "Recent timestamps, majority votes and clean Git merges are insufficient to select a winner." Basis: **model knowledge** (C22).
    71	- **unresolved.** No engine ran read-time newest-wins against disputed-and-escalate on real coding tasks. What would settle it: replay both rules over a recorded set of contradicting entries and count how often newest-wins selects the entry that a later code change or a maintainer confirms.
    72	
    73	**CP3 — Where the memory tree lives.**
    74	- **opencode:** `.ai/`, **isolated from human docs** — "keeps AI memory isolated from human docs". Basis: **model knowledge**.
    75	- **codex:** under the project's **existing `docs/`** — `docs/memory/`, `docs/decisions/`, `docs/work/` — and "reuse existing project documentation rather than copying it into memory"; memory links to already-authoritative requirements and architecture. Basis: **model knowledge** (C13).
    76	- **claude:** a dedicated committed **`memory/` tree** at the repo root (`INDEX.md`, `entries/`, `state/`, `journal/`). Basis: partly **read: REPORT.md s1-F7/F8** (Letta's layout: root files load every turn, directories with their own `MEMORY.md` stay out of context), partly design.
    77	- **unresolved.** This is a convention question, and the three files contain no evidence about a real project's docs. What would settle it: an inventory of one project's existing docs — if `docs/` already holds authoritative requirements and architecture, codex's linking placement wins; if writing agent memory there would pollute human docs, opencode's isolation wins; claude's separate committed tree is a third option.
    78	
    79	**CP4 — One shared per-day session file vs per-writer files.**
    80	- **opencode:** `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD.md`, append-only, one file per day, all agents appending. Basis: **model knowledge** (layout and write table).
    81	- **claude and codex:** one writer per file. claude: "Different file per session and per claim… No two agents edit one file", and per-session files "avoid git merge conflicts that a single shared append file causes" (**model knowledge**, C35). codex refuses "Parallel agents appending to one shared journal" — "overlapping writes and leaves integration implicit" — and notes that "Git merges do not establish semantic agreement". Basis: **read: https://git-scm.com/book/en/v2/Git-Branching-Basic-Branching-and-Merging**.
    82	- **unresolved.** No engine measured conflict rates. What would settle it: run several agents concurrently on one repo with a shared append file and with per-writer files, and count merge conflicts, lost appends and time spent integrating.
    83	
    84	**CP5 — Whether an agent's private store may persist between sessions.**
    85	- **codex:** yes — "allow for personal continuity; refuse as a project dependency"; private memory "may suggest where to look", and useful knowledge is promoted into the repo. Basis: **read: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory#storage-location** plus **model knowledge**.
    86	- **opencode:** no — the private store "should be ephemeral — no persistent memory between sessions"; cross-session state "should be explicit in project memory, not hidden in agent internals". Basis: **model knowledge** (C6).
    87	- **claude** is silent on whether it should persist: it records that each agent keeps a private store with no shared schema (C32, **model knowledge**; C31, **read:** the local `MEMORY.md` index), and rules only that a fact existing only there "does not count as memory".
    88	- **unresolved.** No evidence on whether private persistence helps continuity or hides state other agents need. What would settle it: an A/B where one agent keeps private notes and another does not, measuring whether the notes change actions and whether they duplicate what is in the repo.
    89	
    90	**CP6 — What is read automatically at session start.**
    91	- **opencode:** `context.md`, active tickets from `state.sqlite`, **and "Recent `sessions/` (last 3)"** for continuity. Basis: **model knowledge**.
    92	- **claude and codex:** instructions, the index, and the assigned task's state only. claude: `AGENTS.md`, `INDEX.md`, the `state/claims/` listing, the newest handoff — and "**Never load.** `journal/`". codex: instructions, `docs/memory/INDEX.md`, the task's `state.md`; "Do not bulk-load transcripts, completed-task journals, every decision or an entire search index at startup." Basis: codex **read: OpenAI guidance on skills and prompts** plus **model knowledge**; claude's read rules are design.
    93	- **unresolved.** No engine measured whether three prior session summaries improve the first action more than they cost in tokens and distraction. What would settle it: a with/without comparison on recorded tasks scoring first-action correctness and start-up tokens.
    94	
    95	**CP7 — Whether a memory write is obligatory every session.**
    96	- **opencode:** yes — "Session end | agent | Write summary to `sessions/YYYY-MM-DD.md` | Yes". Basis: **model knowledge**.
    97	- **codex:** no — "Do not require a memory write after every session. Write when there is something another session needs to know", and it refuses "a complete transcript archive as routine memory". Basis: **model knowledge** (C14).
    98	- **claude** sits between: writes are at task end and "Not every turn", but it does permit per-session `journal/` and `handoff/` appends; the raw-capture read (C9) supports keeping raw material cheap.
    99	- **unresolved.** This is a work-practice question the three files cannot settle. What would settle it: measure, over many sessions, how often a mandatory session summary is later read or cited — if it is rarely read, codex's rule wins; if it prevents repeated mistakes, opencode's does.
   100	
   101	**A separate, narrow note (not a claim conflict).** claude's C25 concludes from its read material — LoCoMo's key has 6.4% score-corrupting errors, and swapping only the grading prompt moved one system from 0.9013 to 0.4803 — that "published accuracy numbers cannot choose a project-memory design". codex reaches a related caution from a different source (C12, Liu et al., position-sensitive retrieval) but bounds it: "That establishes a historical failure mode, not a performance result for today's coding agents." The two do not disagree; codex's is the narrower claim.
   102

    24	## By category
    25	
    26	`file hit@5` counts a gold `path::name` as hit when the route returns its file. `symbol hit@5` needs the symbol itself: an entry naming it, the top-level def/class holding it or a method of it, a module-code chunk for a module-level name, and for `rg` a printed match line inside the symbol's lines. A gold given as a plain file scores the same on both. **The best route is chosen on symbol hit@5.**
    27	
    28	`chars` is the mean number of characters of output read up to and including the first line where the route names a gold file as its answer (`# <path> (`, or a line starting `<path>:`), over the answers that named one; `n` is how many did. A miss is left out, never scored 0. What each route prints differs -- rg: a `# path (N matching lines)` header per file plus at most 40 match lines of up to 300 characters; fts: one 24-token snippet per path; vec: the first 300 characters per path; hybrid: the vec or fts block per path; graph: one id per line -- so `chars` measures the print format as much as the store.
