    30	| GitHub REST | `api.github.com/repos/colbymchenry/tarnline` | HTTP 404 |
    31	| Web (DuckDuckGo) | `tarnline code graph cli`, `"tarnline"` (exact phrase) | 10 results returned, **zero** naming `tarnline`; the space is occupied by `colbymchenry/codegraph`, `al1-nasir/codegraph-cli`, `lib.rs/crates/code-graph-cli` |
    32	| Local disk | `grep -rin tarnline` over the repo and `~/.claude`, `~/.agents` | only `runs/20261012-memory-caches/` itself (`launch-opencode.sh:6`, the brief) |
    33	
    34	**Answer to "Which languages does the `tarnline` code-graph CLI support, according to its README?":** it has no README, because there is no such CLI at any registry or index I can reach. Any language list would be invented. The nearest real tools, in case the name was garbled, are **codegraph** (F2) and **Serena** (F11).
    35	
    36	Corroboration *about the brief, not about the world*: `runs/20261012-memory-caches/state.md:71` states that each brief carries "one planted question about a tool that does not exist (`hollowfern`, `quillvec`, `tarnline`); the right answer is not-found."
    37	
    38	---
    39	
    40	## 1. The questions it answers — better than grep over `entries/` + `INDEX.md`
    41	
    42	The honest answer: **almost none, for the memory corpus.** The code-graph evidence base is about *source code*, and `project-memory/` is *markdown*.
    43	
    44	**F2 — CONFIRMED — codegraph indexes 24 language rows by file extension; Markdown is not one of them.**
    45	<https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/reference/languages/> (retrieved 2026-10-12; the page carries no visible date — a GAP on its revision date, see §Not found).
    46	
    47	> "Language support is automatic from the file extension — there's nothing to configure."
    48	
    49	Rows: TypeScript, JavaScript, Python, Go, Rust, Java, C#, PHP, Ruby, C, C++, Objective-C (*Partial support*), Swift, Kotlin, Scala, Dart, Svelte, Vue, Astro, Liquid, Pascal/Delphi, Lua, R, Luau. The README's language assets additionally include ArkTS, so the true count is ≥24 and the two pages disagree.
    50	
    51	**F3 — CONFIRMED — what gets into the index is code, by definition.**
    52	<https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/guides/indexing/> (retrieved 2026-10-12)
    53	
    54	> "Every file whose extension maps to a supported language, minus dependency/build directories excluded by default (node_modules, vendor, dist, …), anything your .gitignore excludes, and files over 1 MB."
    55	
    56	**F14 — INFERRED — the benchmark evidence does not transfer to this store.** Derived from F2, F3, F7, s3-F29, s3-F31. Every measured graph-vs-grep win below is on source code. `project-memory/entries/*.md`, `INDEX.md` and `covers:` lists are markdown. So for the questions an agent actually asks this cache — *"what do we already know about `journal.py`?"*, *"which decision covers the paths I am about to edit?"*, *"what did we decide about X and why?"* — a code graph either does not apply or adds nothing over `grep -rl` plus `INDEX.md`, because `covers:` is **already an explicit, hand-maintained index** of entry→path (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:50`). You built the graph by hand, in the entries.
    57	
    58	**F15 — CONFIRMED (cited) — where the graph demonstrably loses to plain reading.** s3-F29 (arXiv 2603.27277), already verified in r2:
    59	
    60	> "The Explorer retains an advantage for full source context (16/31 languages) and exhaustive call-site grep (10/31)—queries requiring line-level code that the graph intentionally does not store. The weakest MCP result is macro-heavy C (0.58 vs. 1.00), where macros are not represented in the AST."
    61	
    62	s3-F31 (reported) gives the small-repo threshold and the churn hazard:
    63	
    64	> "codebases that generate classes or functions at runtime … produce AST symbols that do not reflect runtime structure; PageRank over those symbols misleads rather than orients."
    65	> "In a monorepo with thousands of commits per day, the parsed AST can be stale within minutes; Claude Code's on-demand agentic search is a better fit because it queries the live filesystem."
    66	
    67	**F17 — INFERRED — the one graph-shaped question that memory actually asks is a *change* question, not a *lookup* question.** "What does this edit touch?", "what else did we decide about these paths?", "is this entry's code still what the entry says?" That is the code graph's home turf — and it is exactly the question the design already assigns to the background subagent (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:70-73`), not to the model-free script on line 75. §6 works this through.
    68	
    69	**Verdict on Q1.** Grep + `INDEX.md` + `covers:` already answers the lookup questions; the graph's unique capability sits on the model-assigned side of the line, not on the cache's side. See §6.
    70	
    71	---
    72	
    73	## 2. When it pays off — measured evidence only
    74	
    75	**F7 — CONFIRMED — codegraph's own benchmark.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph> README (last commit to `README.md` 2026-09-30; measured 2026-08-05).
    76	
    77	> "**The universal win — every repo, every size: 88% fewer tool calls · 53% faster · 62% fewer tokens · 44% cheaper · file reads cut to zero on all seven repos.**"
    78	
    79	> "Tested across **7 real-world open-source codebases** spanning 7 languages, comparing an agent (Claude Code, headless) answering **one architecture question** **with** and **without** CodeGraph, at the **median of 4 runs per arm**."
    80	
    81	| Codebase | Language | Tool calls | Time | File reads | Tokens | Cost |
    82	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    83	| VS Code | TypeScript · ~11k files | 2 vs 28 | 2.2× faster (58s vs 2m 10s) | 0 vs 12 | 77% fewer | 71% cheaper |
    84	| Excalidraw | TypeScript · ~640 | 2 vs 43 | 3.6× faster (45s vs 2m 42s) | 0 vs 18 | 84% fewer | 78% cheaper |
    85	| Django | Python · ~3k | 3 vs 14 | 35% faster (54s vs 1m 23s) | 0 vs 8.5 | 41% fewer | **13% cheaper** |
    86	| Tokio | Rust · ~790 | 3 vs 29 | 2.6× faster (1m 3s vs 2m 43s) | 0 vs 19 | 65% fewer | 64% cheaper |
    87	| OkHttp | Java · ~645 | 1 vs 6 | 43% faster (33s vs 58s) | 0 vs 2 | 54% fewer | 21% cheaper |
    88	| Gin | Go · ~110 | 1 vs 7 | 39% faster (28s vs 46s) | 0 vs 4 | 52% fewer | **~even** |
    89	| Alamofire | Swift · ~110 | 4 vs 33 | 2.6× faster (54s vs 2m 22s) | 0 vs 16.5 | 59% fewer | 57% cheaper |
    90	
    91	**F7a — CONFIRMED — the vendor's own table refutes a corpus-size threshold, in the vendor's own words.** This is the single most useful row for Q2:
    92	
    93	> "Cost tracks how much *discovery* a question demands more than raw repo size: 57–78% on questions the file-reading agent needed 28–43 tool calls to answer, near-even where it got there in 7."
    94	
    95	Read off the table: Django at ~3k files bought **13%**, while Excalidraw at ~640 files bought **78%** and Gin at ~110 files bought **~even**. Repo size does not order the effect; *how much structure the question requires you to re-derive* does.
    96	
    97	**F7b — CONFIRMED — the vendor's own context-cost caveat (this is s3-F24, re-verified at source).**
    98	
    99	> "the numbers above measure *throughput* — tokens processed, tools called, dollars spent to reach one answer. They don't measure what is still sitting in your context window afterward, and on that axis CodeGraph costs **more**, not less. Across the same seven repos in multi-turn sessions, CodeGraph's responses leave about **80% more retrieval context resident** at the end of a session than a file-reading agent's do — on VS Code, 67k tokens against 18k."
   100	
   101	**F8 — CONFIRMED — a task-shaped, not size-shaped, win.** s3-F16, <https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20048> (2026-02-23), abstract read 2026-10-12:
   102	
   103	> "Through258 automated trials across 30 benchmark tasks on a production FastAPI repository, we demonstrate that graph-based structural navigation via CodeCompass … achieves 99.4% task completion on **hidden-dependency tasks**, a 23.2 percentage-point improvement over vanilla agents (76.2%) and 21.2 points over BM25 retrieval (78.2%)."
   104	
   105	So the class where the graph beats grep is **dependencies with no lexical overlap**. And the same paper's cost:
   106	
   107	> "we uncover a critical adoption gap: **58% of trials with graph access made zero tool calls**, and agents required explicit prompt engineering to adopt the tool consistently."
   108	
   109	**F9 — CONFIRMED — the graph must be *adopted*, not merely present.** Same as F8, and reinforced by s3-F32 (Blueprint: ~63K provider-billed tokens with the structural graph against ~41K without, "because the structural map enabled more confident and thorough exploration").
   110	
   111	**F10 — CONFIRMED — no measurement exists for the case that matters here.** A code graph indexed over a *markdown decisions-and-facts corpus*, queried for "which entry covers this path", has no published measurement anywhere I looked. **GAP, and the most load-bearing gap in this store's case.**
   112	
   113	**F12 — MEASURED (first-party, this machine, 2026-10-12)** — the in-repo graph and the git alternative, on this repo:
   114	
   115	| Measurement | Value | How |
   116	|---|---|---|
   117	| `code_graph.py .` full rebuild | **0.94 s** wall, 0 errors | `python3 code_graph/code_graph.py .` |
   118	| Graph content | 126 files, 126 modules, 1240 functions, 433 tests, 13 fixtures | `stats` block |
   119	| Edges | **3700** (call 2000, contains 1240, import 345, fixture 115) | `stats` block |
   120	| Unresolved calls | attribute 2863, dynamic 927, name 84 | `stats` block |
   121	| Graph size on stdout | **2,607,872 bytes** (~2.6 MB) | `ls -l` |
   122	| Prior recorded build | 110 files, 1211 nodes, 3371 edges, 0 errors, **0.6 s** | `runs/20261005-code-graph-tool/nodes/n3-build-3.result.md:33` |
   123	| Repo commits | 246 | `git rev-list --count HEAD` |
   124	| `git log -1 --format=%at -- <path>` | **0.00 s** | timed |
   125	| Full-history `git log --name-only -- ancient_games/` | **0.01 s**, 40 distinct paths ever touched | timed |
   126	| `.git` size | **19 MB** | `du -sh .git` |
   127	
   128	Note the 0.6 s → 0.94 s growth tracks110 → 126 files: linear-ish, and still under a second at this size.
   129	
   130	**Verdict on Q2.** There is no measured corpus size at which a code graph starts beating grep. The one measured axis that orders the effect is **question type** (hidden-dependency / structural questions — F8), and the second is **how much discovery the question demands** (F7a), which is a property of the question, not the repo. For the memory system, entry→path lookup is neither: `covers:` is already the index, and it is exact.
   131	
   132	---
   133	
   134	## 3. Current tools (2025–2026) that index files that stay the source of truth
   135	
   136	**F4 — CONFIRMED — codegraph.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph> (MIT, 73,760★, pushed 2026-10-11; README last commit 2026-09-30).
   137	
   138	*How built:* tree-sitter AST extraction, native parse workers, a multi-worker resolver pool; resolution binds a reference to one of the same-named definitions **project-wide**.
   139	*Storage:* `.codegraph/codegraph.db`, SQLite with FTS5.
   140	
   141	> "2. **Storage** — Everything goes into a local SQLite database (`.codegraph/codegraph.db`) with FTS5 full-text search."
   142	> "| **100% Local** | No data leaves your machine. No API keys. No external services. SQLite database only |"
   143	
   144	*How rebuilt:* incremental, by default and by design.
   145	
   146	> "Auto-sync is enabled by default. CodeGraph watches the project and updates the graph on every file change — while your agent edits code, or you add, modify, or delete files. **The index is never stale, and there is nothing to re-run.**"
   147	
   148	Rebuild time and size:
   149	
   150	> "The Swift compiler repository (27k files of Swift and C++) fresh-indexes in about100 seconds; a one-file edit re-syncs in ~4."
   151	> "the Linux kernel (70k files, 2M symbols, 6.4M relationships) indexes to completion in under 12 minutes"
   152	> "saving a file updates the graph in well under a second — the watcher fires 300ms after a lone save and syncs exactly what changed (**~0.3s of work on a 4,400-file project, ~0.4s on the 27,000-file Swift compiler repo**), never re-scanning the tree."
   153	
   154	From the drift benchmark doc (last commit 2026-10-07): **"392-file repo: index 1.88–2.02s in both arms, single-file sync 0.183s in both."**
   155	Local: **yes**,100% local, single binary, "No Node.js required" (bundles its own runtime and grammars, so tree-sitter need not be installed).
   156	Size on disk: **GAP** — see §Not found.
   157	
   158	**F5 — CONFIRMED — Serena: the no-index alternative.** <https://github.com/oraios/serena> (30,193★, pushed 2026-10-09).
   159	
   160	> "**Language servers** implementing the language server protocol (LSP) — the free/open-source alternative"
   161	> "When using Serena's language server backend, we provide **support for over 40 programming languages**, including Ada / SPARK, … **Markdown**, … TOML, TypeScript, Vue, … YAML, and Zig."
   162	
   163	*F11 — INFERRED (from F5 + absence of any index/refresh command in its README):* Serena builds no persistent index to go stale; it asks the live language server. That is the drift-free-by-construction shape. It is also the only one of these tools whose language list **includes Markdown**, which matters for a memory corpus — but it is symbol-level over language constructs, which markdown largely does not have. Local: yes. Cost: language servers must be installed per language (README: "some additional dependencies may need to be installed to support certain languages"). Build/rebuild time: not applicable — no index.
   164	
   165	**F13 — CONFIRMED — SCIP: the interchange format, not a tool.** <https://github.com/sourcegraph/scip> (Apache-2.0, 827★, pushed 2026-10-10).
   166	
   167	> "SCIP (pronunciation: \"skip\") is a language-agnostic protocol for indexing source code, which can be used to power code navigation functionality such as Go to definition, Find references, and Find implementations."
   168	
   169	Files stay the truth; the index is a per-commit build artifact produced by a per-language indexer. Rebuild time and size: **GAP** (not found; the repo is a protobuf schema + bindings + a CLI).
   170	
   171	**F16 — CONFIRMED — codegraph's own CI recipe uses git as the change oracle.** <https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/reference/cli/> (retrieved 2026-10-12)
   172	
   173	> `codegraph index [path]  # Full re-index from scratch (--force, --quiet, --verbose)`
   174	> `codegraph sync [path]  # Incremental update (--quiet)`
   175	> `codegraph affected [files...]`
   176	> `codegraph affected --stdin # pipe from git diff`
   177	> `AFFECTED = $( git diff --name-only HEAD …`
   178	
   179	Relevant to §6: **the vendor's graph consumes git's answer to "what changed"; it does not try to out-perform git at it.**
   180	
   181	**F18 — the in-repo tool.** `code_graph/code_graph.py`, 981 lines, stdlib only (`argparse, ast, builtins, fnmatch, json, sys, collections, pathlib`). Emits JSON to **stdout only** — no DB, no file, no cache, no lockfile, no incremental path; `GraphBuilder.run()` re-walks, re-reads and re-`ast.parse`s everything every invocation. `*.py` only (`self.root.rglob("*.py")`, `code_graph.py:262`). Node kinds `module`/`function`; edge kinds `contains`/`import`/`call`/`fixture` (`code_graph.py:55`). Its own stated design rule — "Files are walked in sorted path order. Two runs on the same tree give byte-identical output" — means it **cannot drift** (confirmed independently by F5's determinism control, below).
   182	
   183	---
   184	
   185	## 4. How it fails
   186	
   187	**F5 — CONFIRMED — this is the best-documented drift study I could find, and it is a vendor measuring itself against its own tool.** <https://github.com/colbymchenry/codegraph/blob/main/docs/benchmarks/index-drift-cg33.md> (last commit 2026-10-07; "Measured 2026-08-06"). Confirms and extends s3-F25/F26/F28.
   188	
   189	*Silent, and the README says the opposite.*
   190	
   191	> "A live, auto-sync-maintained index **does not converge** to a clean full rebuild of the identical working tree. On codegraph's own repo, **4.3% of distinct edges were wrong**, in both directions, overwhelmingly `calls` edges."
   192	> "This matters because it is silent: nothing warns, nothing surfaces it, and the README tells users the index is never stale and there is nothing to re-run."
   193	
   194	| | count |
   195	|---|---|
   196	| distinct edge triples (rebuild) | 28,809 |
   197	| in rebuild but **missing** from live | **751** |
   198	| in live but **absent** from rebuild (stale) | **476** |
   199	| **total divergent** | **1,227 — 4.3%** |
   200	

exec
/bin/bash -lc "nl -ba runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/code-graph.opencode.stdout.md | sed -n '200,370p'; nl -ba runs/20261012-caches-routing/bench/results.md | sed -n '180,310p'; cat runs/20261012-caches-routing/nodes/audit-0.json; cat runs/20261012-caches-routing/nodes/build.opencode.stdout.md" in /home/smoke01/dev/ancient-games
 succeeded in 0ms:
   200	
   201	*Failure mode 1 — a count-based check lies.* (New; not in r2.)
   202	
   203	> "Raw edge **rows** were 39,845 live vs 40,122 rebuilt — a benign-looking +0.7%. The divergence is bidirectional, so a net-count check nets it out and reports almost nothing wrong. **Any drift detector must compare edge sets, not totals.**"
   204	
   205	*Failure mode 2 — every conventional integrity check passes.* (New.)
   206	
   207	> "Node sets are identical — `files` 501 = 501, `nodes` 10,110 = 10,110, heuristic edges 36 = 36 — and every integrity check is 0 on *both* indexes: no duplicate nodes, no orphan edges, no nodes referencing a missing file row. **Nothing accumulates. Cross-file resolution goes stale.**"
   208	
   209	*Failure mode 3 — it is not noise.*
   210	
   211	> "Control, rebuild vs rebuild on the same tree and build: **0 differing edges** (28,809 both runs). So the live-vs-rebuild delta is not run-to-run noise."
   212	
   213	*Failure mode 4 — the fix is partial, by design.*
   214	
   215	> "| 80 commits | 1,634 (963 / 671) | 890 | **361 (359 / 2)** |"
   216	> "**The full rebuild is the wrong one here** — converging would mean teaching sync to manufacture thousands of wrong edges. Left as is, deliberately."
   217	
   218	*Failure mode 5 — a mistyped cache path is indistinguishable from staleness.* (New; directly relevant to M6.)
   219	
   220	> "`sqlite3` against a mistyped path **creates an empty database** rather than failing, and every subsequent query then answers from an empty schema — which reads exactly like a stale pre-migration index."
   221	
   222	*Failure mode 6 — retrieval quality decays invisibly (s3-F26, re-verified).*
   223	
   224	> "a generated ambient-types file carried graph mass **0.24750** on the drifted index vs **0.13119** on a clean rebuild (~1.9×), and score **49.0** vs **27.0**. On the drifted index it took **60.7%** of an explore envelope and starved the file the agent had actually named by symbol, which rendered **251 chars of a 10,970 reservation**."
   225	
   226	**F5b — CONFIRMED — the detection mechanisms exist, and none of them can see this failure class.** <https://colbymchenry.github.io/codegraph/guides/indexing/>
   227	
   228	The three layers are named, on the page, as covering exactly two windows: the debounce window and the server-not-running gap.
   229	
   230	> "1. **File watcher with debounced auto-sync (always on)**" — "A debounce timer collapses bursts of edits into a single sync." (default 2000 ms, `CODEGRAPH_WATCH_DEBOUNCE_MS`, clamped `[100ms, 60s]`)
   231	> "2. **Per-file staleness banner** — covers the debounce window"
   232	> "3. **Connect-time catch-up** — covers gaps when the MCP server wasn't running" — "a (size, mtime) stat pre-filter, then a content hash on the rest"
   233	
   234	The banner:
   235	
   236	> "⚠️ Some files referenced below were edited since the last index sync —their codegraph entries may be stale: - src/Widget.ts (edited 800ms ago, pending sync)For accurate content of those specific files, Read them directly.The rest of this response is fresh."
   237	
   238	**F6 — INFERRED — these layers cannot detect the F5 class.** Derived from F5 + F5b. All three are keyed to *mtime/content* of files on disk. The F5 failure is not a file that changed and was missed; it is a file that was correctly re-extracted, after which **cross-file resolution elsewhere in the repo became wrong** and nothing was left behind to revisit. A per-file "is this file pending sync" signal is structurally incapable of seeing it.
   239	
   240	**F5c — CONFIRMED — and the vendor says so, explicitly.**
   241	
   242	> "### `codegraph status` — decided: no drift metric
   243	> The issue asked whether `status` should surface divergence. Decision: **no**.
   244	> A drift number cannot be computed without the full rebuild it would be recommending, so anything cheap enough to run on `status` would be an estimate — and an honest estimate is not available."
   245	
   246	This sharpens the s3 gap ("`status` deliberately declines to report a drift metric") into the load-bearing sentence: **you cannot cheaply detect drift, and detecting it costs exactly the thing you were avoiding.**
   247	
   248	**F19 — CONFIRMED (cited) — the CLI-vs-daemon variant.** s3-F27: after adding a symbol, `codegraph query "metodoNuevo"` returned "No results found" and the database had 0 rows for it; issue created 2026-09-20, closed 2026-10-07. Because only the MCP daemon auto-syncs, a user-facing CLI over the same index is stale by construction — i.e. **"the cache" and "the index the reader sees" can be different objects**, which is the sharpest possible violation of "its absence must not stop anyone reading memory" (M6).
   249	
   250	**F20 — CONFIRMED (cited) — adoption failure is a failure mode too.** s3-F33: "58% of trials with graph access made zero tool calls" (arXiv 2602.20048). A correct graph that agents route around produces the same outcome as no graph, plus the maintenance and drift cost.
   251	
   252	**F21 — INFERRED — the repo has already paid this bill once.** `docs/KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:31-34`: *"Non-goals, stated up front because this repo has a track record of building graphs nobody queries (`loop/graph_memory.py`, 631 lines, no query code, `~/dev/seza/.ctxgraph/graph.db`, wired once, never touched again…)"*, and `:489-499`: *"Building it now would mean testing it exclusively against synthetic data … which is exactly the failure mode wave2 flags … : correct and unused."* That is F20 and F19 in this repo's own history.
   253	
   254	---
   255	
   256	## 5. What fits here — options, not a choice
   257	
   258	Machine as given: Python 3.12, SQLite 3.45.1 + FTS5, numpy, RTX 4070 Ti SUPER 16 GB. **Not installed: sqlite-vec, sentence-transformers, fastembed, tree-sitter.** The repo is 246 commits, 1446 tracked files, 123 `.py` files, `.git` 19 MB. `project-memory/` **does not exist yet**, so there are no real `covers:` values to size this against.
   259	
   260	The GPT is bad and most of these numbers are small enough that I would not build anything before the corpus exists. Options as required:
   261	
   262	**Option A — no code graph. Git on `covers:`.**
   263	*Needs installed:* nothing. *Rebuild:* n/a. *Size:* 0 (uses `.git`, already 19 MB). *Drift class:* none — `git log` reads the truth, not a projection of it.
   264	*Answers:* the line-75 check exactly, and "which entries cover the files I am about to edit". *Does not answer:* "what does this change transitively affect", "is this entry still true".
   265	*Measured here:* 0.00 s per path, 0.01 s for a full-history path scan of one directory (F12).
   266	
   267	**Option B — reuse `code_graph/code_graph.py` as-is, full rebuild, never incremental.**
   268	*Needs installed:* nothing (stdlib). *Rebuild:* **0.94 s** measured now (was 0.6 s at 110 files). *Size:* **2.6 MB** to stdout; no file written. *Drift class:* none by construction — deterministic, byte-identical, no incremental path, no DB.
   269	*Answers:* symbol-level and call-level change within `*.py`. *Does not answer:* anything about markdown; nothing about non-Python paths in `covers:`.
   270	*The trap:* `covers:` names *paths*, not symbols, so a rebuild is mostly wasted — and partial language coverage is a **silent partial answer**, which is F19's failure mode wearing a different hat. A graph that answers for Python entries and silently not for the rest is worse than no graph, because the miss is invisible.
   271	
   272	**Option C — adopt an external indexer (codegraph).**
   273	*Needs installed:* the codegraph binary (self-contained; bundles its own runtime and grammars, so the absent tree-sitter is not a blocker). *Rebuild:* ~100 s for 27k files; ~0.3–0.4 s incremental sync; 1.88–2.02 s full index on a 392-file repo. *Size:* GAP. *Runs locally:* yes, 100% local.
   274	*Answers:* F7's measured win — but on **source-code architecture questions**, not on markdown memory (F2, F14). *Costs:* the entire drift apparatus of §4, plus a background daemon and a writer lock, plus `--stdin | git diff` plumbing (F16).
   275	
   276	**Option D — adopt Serena (LSP, no index).**
   277	*Needs installed:* a language server per language. *Rebuild:* n/a. *Size:* no index. *Drift class:* none — queries the live filesystem.
   278	*Answers:* live symbol navigation. *Does not answer:* markdown semantics (F5 lists Markdown as supported, but a markdown file has few language-level symbols), call-graph edges it never promised, or anything offline. *Also the only option above that can read `.md` at all.*
   279	
   280	**Option E — git for the check, stdlib `ast` for the refinement.**
   281	*Needs installed:* nothing. *Rebuild:* bounded by the diff, not the repo — parse only the changed file(s) to map each diff hunk to its enclosing symbol. *Size:* n/a. *Drift class:* none.
   282	*Answers:* the line-75 check **and** symbol-level precision on `*.py`, because the work is proportional to what changed. *Does not answer:* transitive call impact, non-Python.
   283	
   284	Trade-off axis worth naming: **A and E cost nothing and answer the check; C buys a measured win on a question this store has not been shown to ask; D is drift-free but changes tool surface and dependency footprint; B is free and correct but answers a narrower question than the design's entries are phrased in.** I am not choosing, per the brief.
   285	
   286	---
   287	
   288	## 6. The specific question: does the line-75 drift check need a code graph, or is git on `covers:` enough?
   289	
   290	> `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:75` — "A script with no model call also marks a decision or fact in `INDEX.md` when its code changed with no earlier change to the entry."
   291	
   292	> `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:79` — "**Not decided yet (EJ decides):** … how the script tells that the entry changed earlier than the code …"
   293	
   294	### The check, decomposed
   295	
   296	Two primitives, both pure git, given what the design says `covers:` is:
   297	
   298	`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:50` — the entry header holds "`covers` (the code paths it is about)". **Paths, not symbols.** That single word decides most of this.
   299	
   300	1. *When did the entry last change?* → `git log -1 --format=%at -- project-memory/entries/<id>.md`. **0.00 s here** (F12).
   301	2. *Has a covered path changed since?* → `git log --since=@<t> --name-only -- <each covers path>`. **0.01 s** for a full-history scan of `ancient_games/` (F12).
   302	
   303	Compare the two timestamps. No parse, no resolver, no index, no model. Ordering a commit against an entry commit is a field comparison on data git already stores as the record of what happened — the one artifact in this system with no drift class at all, because it is not a projection.
   304	
   305	### The case **for** a code graph
   306	
   307	1. **Precision.** A one-line comment edit in a 400-line covered file is a change to that file; a symbol-level check would not flag the decision the file encodes. This is real, and it is the strongest argument. *But:* git gives the diff hunk; mapping a hunk to its enclosing symbol is a ~30-line stdlib `ast` walk over the **changed file only**. That is Option E — it buys the precision without a repo-wide graph.
   308	2. **Call edges — transitive meaning change.** If `journal.py` calls into `stages.py` and `stages.py` changes, an entry covering only `journal.py` is not flagged. A graph would flag it. **This is genuinely more capability than git on paths has.** *But it lands on the wrong side of the design's own line:* "does this entry still match the code" is the background subagent's job (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:70-73`, "it reports to EJ which entry, what the entry says, and what the code does instead"), not the model-free script's. A transitive check is the more expensive question, and the design already pays a model to answer it. Line 75 is the cheap ordering check.
   309	3. **s3-F4 — StateTape reads staleness off a code-graph diff with no model call.** Verified at source (F8, <https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36319>, 2026-09-28):
   310	
   311	> "The key idea of StateTape is to model the repository as a symbol-level code graph, whose dependencies and language rules expose which symbols a write can affect. Upon this graph, a tape marks the symbols each write changed, which turns staleness from an inference about text into an observation of the agent's writes."
   312	
   313	Two caveats that survive the citation. First, the abstract concedes a model in the loop for the residue: *"a small manager model settles what the write log cannot."* The model-free claim covers **nomination**, not adjudication — the design's line 75 is nomination-shaped, but so is the model half of StateTape. Second, and decisive for our case: **a write is the agent's own edit, in the current session, on files it just touched.** StateTape marks what the write log logs. The line-75 check is "the code changed at some point in the last four days and nobody updated the entry" — a historical scan over commits the agent did not make. Different problem. And StateTape's records cover **symbols by construction** ("Its unit is the symbol (a function, method or class), the smallest unit whose change alters meaning, not position", s3-F3) — which means s3-F4's applicability is a consequence of *how StateTape chose to scope its records*, not a general property of drift checks. If `covers:` named symbols, s3-F4 would apply. It names paths.
   314	4. **s3-F26's production incident** — a drifted index degraded retrieval. True, and it is evidence *against* the graph for this check, not for it: it is what you get when you introduce a stale projection to answer a question git answers exactly.
   315	
   316	### The case **against** — for git on `covers:`
   317	
   318	1. **The graph introduces a staleness class into a check that had none.** F5 is the best available measurement of exactly that class: 4.3% of distinct edges wrong, bidirectionally, silently, with a README promising the opposite — and, per F5c, **no cheap way to detect it, because detection costs the full rebuild you were avoiding.** For a check whose entire value is "is this entry still trustworthy", a 4.3%-and-silent oracle is a downgrade from git's exactness, not an upgrade.
   319	2. **The same vendor already ruled on this.** F5: *"The full rebuild is the wrong one here — converging would mean teaching sync to manufacture thousands of wrong edges. Left as is, deliberately."* So the incremental graph — the only form that makes the check cheap — is the form its own maintainers declined to converge, and the full-rebuild form is the form you do not need because git is already the rebuild.
   320	3. **The repo's own rule forbids the edge.** `docs/KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:45-50`: *"No node or edge type without a listed query."* The design lists exactly one query for this store, and it is an ordering check over covered **paths**. No listed query needs a symbol or call edge. `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:524-528` names the follow-on and its trigger in the same breath: *"The code import/call graph is deliberately not built. If Guard is ever asked 'does this edit reach a hub transitively through imports,' the follow-on is a `kind='imports'` addition to `edge` … This is not built now because no listed query needs it."*
   321	4. **The repo's own doctrine points the other way.** `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:355-366`, citing the evidence-KG skill's rule 9: *"Build a new graph from the whole corpus. Never incrementally patch a production graph unless the importer proves exact update and provenance-merge semantics … a full rebuild from an append-only log at K3's scale … is cheap enough that incrementality buys nothing."* The in-repo graph honours this and cannot drift (F18: deterministic, byte-identical, 0.94 s). So the repo already has the drift-free shape — and it still does not need it, because git is cheaper and exact.
   322	5. **The git-bound memory literature reaches the same ledger from the other direction.** s3-F13, <https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14390> (2026-07-15), abstract read 2026-10-12 — *"Why Git Is the Memory Solution for the Agentic Development Lifecycle"*:
   323	
   324	> "We argue memory should instead be git-bound -- built into the repository's version control, inheriting the guarantees the machinery struggles to construct: **ground truth from commits, freshness from rebuild**, verification from the merge, containment from review."
   325	
   326	Note two things. It is a *memory* paper, and it lands on git — and when it does want structure, it wants a **git-anchored** one: *"A router dispatches breadth to a git-anchored structural map, pointed lookups to confidence-gated episodes, and rationale to decision synthesis."* Same split as `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:70-75`: git decides the ordering, something more expensive decides the meaning.
   327	6. **When it did use structure anyway, it still fell back to git plus a parser.** s3-F22 (relore, Hugging Face): the tool *"had to finish with grep plus a Python AST scan."* The empirical shape of "git, then parse" is what real systems converged on.
   328	
   329	### Conclusion, and the flip condition
   330	
   331	**Git on the `covers:` paths is enough for the line-75 check as the design states it** — because `covers:` names paths, because git is the authoritative record rather than a projection of it, and because a code graph's two unique capabilities (symbol precision, call edges) either reduce to a parse of the changed file or belong to the model-assigned job on lines 70-73. The check's own open question at line 79 ("how the script tells that the entry changed earlier than the code") is answerable with two `git log` calls and 0.00 s, and does not need to become an index question.
   332	
   333	**Three named conditions would flip this** — stated as tests, in the idiom of `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md` §5:
   334	
   335	1. `covers:` starts naming **symbols** rather than paths (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md:50`). Then s3-F4's mechanism applies and a parser is needed. Test: does any entry's `covers:` field stop being a path?
   336	2. The check changes from *"did the covered code change"* to *"what does this change affect"* — the `codegraph impact` / transitive question. Test: does the script need an answer about a file not on any `covers:` list? That is the `kind='imports'` trigger already written down at `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md:524-528`.
   337	3. Call sites drift at a rate that makes path-level flags useless noise. Test: if >X% of flags are false positives from unrelated edits in large covered files, precision has become the binding constraint — at which point Option E buys it for free, from the diff.
   338	
   339	Per the brief: options reported, not chosen.
   340	
   341	---
   342	
   343	## Not found- **`tarnline`** — in GitHub repo search (4 query variants, `total_count: 0`), PyPI (404), npm (404), the GitHub REST API (404), and the open web (exact-phrase search: zero hits). No README, no language list, no error page quoting a project of that name.
   344	- **Index size on disk for any external code-graph tool.** codegraph publishes WAL thresholds ("soft threshold = the larger of 256 MB and a quarter of the index size, up to 2 GB"; "at rest it is trimmed to 64 MB") and states "the index itself has no size limit", but **never states the index size for any repo**. Checked: README, `guides/indexing/`, `reference/cli/`, `reference/languages/`.
   345	- **A measured corpus-size threshold at which a code graph beats grep.** None exists. The closest is s3-F31's qualitative "~20 files" claim, and the vendor's own table contradicts size as the ordering variable (F7a).
   346	- **Any measurement of a code graph over a markdown decisions-and-facts corpus.** The whole benchmark base (F7, s3-F16, s3-F29, s3-F38) is source code. This is the load-bearing gap for this store.
   347	- **A drift *detection* metric in codegraph.** Deliberately declined, with reasons (F5c). Matches the r2 gap, now with the reason quoted.
   348	- **A published rebuild-time or index-size figure for Serena or SCIP.** Serena has no index; SCIP is a format (F13). Neither publishes one.
   349	- **Any tool found that detects doc-vs-code staleness from git commit timestamps** — searched for stalint/"checkdocs"-class tooling and for `git diff`-based staleness CI. The search endpoint returned nothing on repeated attempts. **Unproven/GAP** — I am not claiming none exists, only that I did not find one. This would have been the strongest third-party precedent for §6.
   350	- **The unit of "~4" in codegraph's re-sync claim** ("a one-file edit re-syncs in ~4"). The README fragment does not state the unit. Quoted verbatim, not interpreted.
   351	- **The date of codegraph's languages doc page.** No visible date; README last-commit 2026-09-30 recorded instead.
   352	- **`store-research/wave2-kg/MERGED.md`** — already known absent from disk before this run; `KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md` numbers citing it are second-hand.

   358	- The README's "2–7× faster than the fastest competing indexer's re-index-on-change … across a 31-repo, 30-language benchmark" — the competitor was not identified or benchmarked.

## What I built

Everything is under `runs/20261012-caches-routing/bench/`; nothing else in the repo changed (verified against `baseline-git-status.txt` — identical).

| file | lines | what |
|---|---|---|
| `chunk.py` | 228 | corpus = `git ls-files '*.py' '*.md'` minus the two run dirs (490 files: 123 `.py`, 367 `.md`); markdown by heading section, Python by top-level `def`/`class` + module-level code → 4017 chunks |

Graph indexes as asked: functions, call edges, fixture edges, test→function reachability (direct table + transitive with `hops`), module→file. My BFS over call+fixture edges agrees with `code_graph.py`'s own coverage count (241 = 241), which the build asserts and reports.

**No leakage is enforced by construction:** `pick_route_input()` is the only code that touches a question; it copies four keys into a frozen `RouteInput`, and an `assert` fails the module if a fifth field appears. `run_bench.py` reads `gold` in a separate function after the route answered. Probed with a poisoned gold (`CANARY/...`) — no route's answer contains it.

## What did not work, and what I chose

- **S03 is a real miss, and the graph is right.** "Which tests should I run after changing `code_graph/code_graph.py`" → the graph answers *none*. `code_graph/tests/test_code_graph.py` runs the tool as a **subprocess** and never imports it, so no call edge exists. A static graph cannot see process-exercised code.
- **Crashes I found and fixed while testing:** `vec` raised `TypeError` on an empty question (model2vec returns no embedding, not a zero one). FTS term quoting survives `D-0012`, `c++`, `a AND b`, `"`, `*` — no raises.
- **Choices the brief left open**, all in `results.json` under `config` so a review can flip one: `rg` uses `-F` (fixed strings) so it searches the same literal text FTS quotes; printed lines cut at 300 chars (1495 corpus lines are longer); `rg` output capped at 40 lines; scoring compares files, so a gold `path::name` is hit by a bare path; route seconds exclude the one-time model load (0.31 s, reported separately).
- **`newest_source_mtime` moved mid-run** (4016 → 4017 chunks): `docs/memory-system/CACHES.md` was edited at 13:59:43 by the parallel session `20261012-caches-enhance`. The stores picked it up — which is rule 1 working, but it means these numbers are a snapshot of a corpus in motion. Re-run before the tables are used.
- `../queries.json` did not exist (node 1a still running), so the run used `bench/sample_queries.json`; `--queries ../queries.json` will work as soon as it lands.

One caveat on evidence: these numbers come from a harness I wrote, and the determinism check is my own check of my own code — node 2's audit is what should make them evidence.

   125	  kind of session; MEMORY.md is 52 lines, under the 200-line limit I know Claude Code applies, so none truncated).
   126	- `~/CLAUDE.md` (3,566 B) is an ancestor-directory file, also auto-loaded; it is the user's file, not SEZA's.

   128	  "bookend": project_memory verify/status line, plus `git status --short` of two repos, each truncated at 200 chars. I did

   192	
   193	Commands:

   196	rg -n -i ... 'ctxgraph' -g '*.py' -g '*.sh' -g '*.js' -g 'Makefile' -g '*.toml' -g '*.yaml' -g '*.rs' -g '*.json' .

   199	
   200	`loop/graph_memory.py` (630 lines, 30,085 B):
   201	- Only code reference in the live tree: `backtest/loop/program_db.py:551,552,563,564`, inside the handler for a
   202	  `memory_snapshot` event, as a validation call (`from loop import graph_memory`, `MemorySnapshot.from_payload`,
   203	  `restore_snapshot`). That is validation of event payloads, not a query of any database.

   205	  `loop/GRAPH_MEMORY_DESIGN.md` are **kept, dormant, and now untested**."; `LOOP_CLEANUP_PLAN.md:53-54`: "`loop/evaluate_candidate.py` never creates any of those three event types, so this is
   206	  genuinely dead code today"; `CLAUDE.md:183-184`:
   207	  "`loop/graph_memory.py` is also retained, dormant and untested".
   208	- Its own docstring (line 1-7): "intentionally a pure projection layer ... Its inputs are a frozen prefix of canonical
   209	  `program_db` events" - it is not about `.ctxgraph`.

   215	  `backtest/loop/worktree.py:291` (it is masked off with an empty tmpfs from candidate sandboxes).

   217	  Makefile target references it.

   219	  Do not merge, overwrite, or use them as live project-progress state." `backtest/AGENTS.md`: "Keep the research

   222	  atime on this filesystem is unreliable evidence of who read it (relatime), and I do not know who read it on Oct 11.

    31	
    32	The discovery evidence is:
    33	
    34	- **Claude Code:** its official memory documentation specifies “first 200 lines or 25KB” and says “The main conversation’s auto memory isn’t loaded into subagents.” Topic files are read on demand. Thus the whole Claude memory directory is **not** the main conversation’s startup load, and fresh subagents do not inherit that index unless forked. [Claude Code memory documentation](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory), accessed 2026-10-12.
    35	- **Codex:** project discovery begins at the project root; it “includes at most one file per directory.” The documented instruction-chain cap is “32 KiB by default.” Therefore `/home/smoke01/AGENTS.md` is not automatically added to this Git-root session merely because it exists. [Official OpenAI documentation](https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/agents-md), accessed 2026-10-12.
    36	- **agy:** current official documentation lists workspace `AGENTS.md` and `GEMINI.md`. Applicability to SEZA’s historically recorded CLI version is not an observed startup trace. [Antigravity rules](https://www.antigravity.google/docs/rules/), accessed 2026-10-12.
    37	- **opencode:** its documented precedence says: “if you have both `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md`, only `AGENTS.md` is used.” [OpenCode rules](https://docs.opencode.ai/docs/rules/), accessed 2026-10-12.
    38	- **Qwen:** installed source defines the default candidates as `"QWEN.md"` and `"AGENTS.md"` at [chunk-3PY4LPJF.js:7910](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-3PY4LPJF.js:7910). Its memory loader reads both project and user indexes at [chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:147678](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:147678), when managed memory is enabled and the session is not in bare mode.
    39	- **DeepSeek Harness:** its installed loader documentation says the first request includes “every existing candidate file from the project root down to the session working directory”; the default candidates are `['AGENTS.md', 'CLAUDE.md']`. Its base configuration has a `65,536`-byte instruction budget. [Installed loader README:28](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@deepseek-ai/dsh/node_modules/@deepseek-ai/dsh-agent-instructions/README.md:28). Active-profile overrides remain unverified.
    40	
    41	For a task under `backtest/`, every agent is additionally instructed to read `backtest/AGENTS.md`. Its startup procedure is:
    42	
    43	> “Run `python3 -m project_memory verify` … Read `python3 -m project_memory status` and `python3 -m project_memory pending` … Search for the task's concepts and inspect relevant neighbors.”  
    44	> — [backtest/AGENTS.md:5](/home/smoke01/dev/seza/backtest/AGENTS.md:5)
    45	
    46	That adds **1,869 bytes / 467.25 estimated tokens**, plus variable command output and relevant entries. Scoring-harness and factstore instructions are scoped reads, not universal root-session loads.
    47	
    48	Claude also has a SessionStart hook. Its source says it:
    49	
    50	> “Runs `python3 -m project_memory verify` and `status`, summarizes the working-tree state of both repos, and injects the result as context”  
    51	> — [session_start.py:4](/home/smoke01/dev/seza/.claude/hooks/session_start.py:4)
    52	
    53	**GAP:** the injected text’s current size. I did not execute that hook because its `main()` writes a session stamp.

    55	**Measurement M1 — instruction files, role files, and private memory folders**
    56	
    57	Command:
    58	
    59	```bash
    60	python3 - <<'PY'
    61	from pathlib import Path
    62	r=Path('/home/smoke01/dev/seza');h=Path('/home/smoke01')
    63	paths=[r/'AGENTS.md',r/'CLAUDE.md',r/'orchestration.mdc',
    64	       r/'backtest/AGENTS.md',r/'backtest/eval/CLAUDE.md',
    65	       r/'factstore/CLAUDE.md',h/'CLAUDE.md',h/'AGENTS.md',
    66	       h/'.claude/projects/-home-smoke01-dev-seza/memory/MEMORY.md',
    67	       h/'.claude/projects/-home-smoke01-dev-seza-v2-wave9/memory/MEMORY.md',
    68	       h/'.qwen/projects/-home-smoke01-dev-seza/memory/MEMORY.md',
    69	       h/'.qwen/memories/MEMORY.md']
    70	print('path | bytes | characters | EST tokens (characters/4)')
    71	for p in paths:
    72	 b=p.read_bytes();s=b.decode('utf-8')
    73	 print(p,len(b),len(s),f'{len(s)/4:.2f}',sep=' | ')
    74	print('Claude role files: full bytes | full chars | EST full tokens | body bytes | body chars | EST body tokens')
    75	for p in sorted((r/'.claude/agents').glob('*.md')):
    76	 s=p.read_text()
    77	 body=s.split('---',2)[2].lstrip('\n') if s.startswith('---') else s
    78	 print(p.name,len(s.encode()),len(s),len(s)/4,
    79	       len(body.encode()),len(body),len(body)/4,sep=' | ')
    80	print('memory folders: files | bytes | characters | EST tokens')
    81	for d in list(sorted((h/'.claude/projects').glob('-home-smoke01-dev-seza*/memory')))+[
    82	 h/'.qwen/projects/-home-smoke01-dev-seza/memory',h/'.qwen/memories']:
    83	 fs=[p for p in d.rglob('*') if p.is_file()]
    84	 chars=sum(len(p.read_text()) for p in fs)
    85	 print(d,len(fs),sum(p.stat().st_size for p in fs),chars,chars/4,sep=' | ')
    86	PY
    87	```

   130	**SEZA’s memory stores**
   131	
   132	“Entry” needs a grain here: these are different stores, not five folders of equivalent markdown entries.
   133	
   134	| Store | Meaningful entry grain | Confirmed count | Confirmed serialized/payload bytes | Confirmed median entry bytes |
   135	|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
   136	| `backtest/project_memory/` | Canonical journal event, including its newline | 9,633 | 9,092,203 | 851 |
   137	| Same, current discovery projection | Non-tombstoned entity | 514 | 497,092 | 706 |
   138	| Same, current discovery projection | Non-tombstoned relation | 467 | 83,757 | 175 |
   139	| `factstore/` | Accepted fact | 0 | 0 | Undefined: empty |
   140	| Same | Candidate fact | 0 | 0 | Undefined: empty |
   141	| Same | Document-search chunk | 24 | 37,699 | 1,306.5 |
   142	| `seza-factstore/` | File: design/research proposal, not a populated fact database | 5 | 143,572 | 31,864 |
   143	| `knowledge/` | Structured JSONL record, including its newline | 313 | 540,958 | 1,503 |
   144	| `.ctxgraph/graph.db` | Episode | 164 | 207,145 | 1,203.5 |
   145	| Same | Entity | 367 | 122,238 | 298 |
   146	| Same | Edge | 376 | 132,487 | 352 |
   147	
   148	SQLite payload sizes above sum each row’s UTF-8 text and raw BLOB bytes. They exclude numeric fields, page allocation, indexes, and other SQLite overhead. They are **not database-file sizes**. Project-memory payloads also contain projection fields such as search text, so they are not minimal unique-content sizes.
   149	
   150	The whole-directory measurements follow. They include code, tests, databases, backups, and generated files; they should not be interpreted as memory-entry counts.
   151	
   152	**Measurement M2 — directory footprints and logical records**
   153	
   154	Command:
   155	
   156	```bash
   157	python3 - <<'PY'
   158	from pathlib import Path
   159	import statistics,sqlite3
   160	r=Path('/home/smoke01/dev/seza')
   161	def report(label,s):
   162	 print(label,len(s),sum(s),statistics.median(s) if s else None,sep=' | ')
   163	print('FILES: scope | count | bytes | median bytes')
   164	for rel in ['backtest/project_memory','factstore','seza-factstore','knowledge','.ctxgraph']:
   165	 fs=[p for p in (r/rel).rglob('*') if p.is_file() and not p.is_symlink()]
   166	 report(rel+' all regular files',[p.stat().st_size for p in fs])
   167	 report(rel+' excluding .git,__pycache__,.pytest_cache,.benchmarks,target',
   168	        [p.stat().st_size for p in fs if not any(
   169	         x in {'.git','__pycache__','.pytest_cache','.benchmarks','target'}
   170	         for x in p.relative_to(r/rel).parts)])
   171	for rel in ['knowledge/raw','knowledge/reconciled','knowledge/structured','seza-factstore/research']:
   172	 report(rel,[p.stat().st_size for p in (r/rel).rglob('*')
   173	             if p.is_file() and not p.is_symlink()])
   174	print('RECORDS: scope | count | bytes | median bytes')
   175	for label,fs in [
   176	 ('project_memory events',[r/'backtest/project_memory/state/events.jsonl']),
   177	 ('knowledge structured JSONL',list((r/'knowledge/structured').rglob('*.jsonl')))]:
   178	 report(label+' nonempty raw lines including LF',
   179	        [len(l) for p in fs for l in p.read_bytes().splitlines(keepends=True)
   180	         if l.strip()])
   181	for rel,tables in [
   182	 ('backtest/project_memory/state/graph.db',['entities','relations']),
   183	 ('factstore/factstore.db',['fact','candidate_fact','doc_index']),
   184	 ('.ctxgraph/graph.db',['episodes','entities','edges']),
   185	 ('.ctxgraph/staging-B.db',['episodes','entities','edges'])]:
   186	 c=sqlite3.connect((r/rel).as_uri()+'?mode=ro',uri=True)
   187	 print('database file bytes',rel,(r/rel).stat().st_size)
   188	 all_sizes=[]
   189	 for t in tables:
   190	  where=' WHERE tombstoned=0' if rel.startswith('backtest') else ''
   191	  sizes=[sum(len(v.encode('utf-8')) if isinstance(v,str)
   192	             else len(v) if isinstance(v,bytes) else 0 for v in row)
   193	         for row in c.execute('SELECT * FROM '+t+where)]
   194	  report(rel+' '+t+where+' TEXT+BLOB payload',sizes)
   195	  all_sizes+=sizes
   196	 if '.ctxgraph/' in rel:
   197	  report(rel+' episodes+entities+edges combined payload',all_sizes)
   198	 c.close()
   199	PY
   200	```

   243	## 5. Grep cost today (my own test)
   244	
   245	Setup: five questions taken from SEZA memory entries or docs. Two corpora:
   246	- **M** = the 55-file Claude memory folder `$CM`.
   247	- **D** = M + `backtest/project_memory` (md/json, `state/` excluded) + `docs/decisions` + `knowledge` (md/json/jsonl/txt,
   248	  `target/` excluded) + `factstore/CLAUDE.md` + `seza-factstore`. 257 files, 2,527,718 B (`rg --files` with the same globs, then `stat`).
   249	
   250	Commands (pattern `$P`, `$RGD` = `rg -i -N --no-messages -g '*.md' -g '*.json' -g '*.jsonl' -g '*.txt' --glob '!**/target/**' --glob '!**/state/**' --glob '!**/__pycache__/**'`):
   251	```
   252	rg -il "$P" "$CM"            | wc -l      # files hit in M
   253	rg -i -n "$P" "$CM"          | wc -c      # bytes printed (path:line:text) in M;  wc -l for lines
   254	rg -ic "$P" "$CM/MEMORY.md"               # does the index line itself contain it
   255	$RGD -l "$P" $CM backtest/project_memory docs/decisions knowledge factstore/CLAUDE.md seza-factstore | wc -l   # D

   373	## Side effect (Rule 8)
   374	
   375	My very first `sqlite3 "file:<db>?mode=ro" ".tables"` call opened the four databases without `immutable=1`. For
   376	`.ctxgraph/graph.db` (WAL mode) this touched the SHM scratch file: `.ctxgraph/graph.db-shm` mtime moved from
   377	2026-10-11 19:37 to 2026-10-12 18:31 (checked with `stat`). Its size (32,768 B) is unchanged. `graph.db` mtime is
   378	still 2026-07-28 14:50 and `-wal` is still 0 bytes dated 2026-09-25, so no database content changed. `git status --short` of the
   379	SEZA root lists 41 entries; I did not diff that list against its state before my session, so I cannot show none of them is mine,
   380	but I created no file inside SEZA. All later reads used `mode=ro&immutable=1`.

runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:7:**How sources were read.** WebFetch returns a model-written extract of the page, not raw HTML — the same caveat the earlier nodes recorded for their own quotes. So **CONFIRMED** here means "I opened the source and the extract quoted the page's wording", not byte-checked. **REPORTED** = a secondary source or a vendor's own self-evaluation. **GAP** = looked, not found. Arithmetic I did on a measured figure is marked *arithmetic* and is never presented as a source's claim.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:19:and at the smallest tier (1,144 documents / 1.7M corpus tokens) BM25 used **5.8K tokens per question against 226K** for the grep-and-read agent — but the agent **led at the smallest shared tiers**. So the crossover is a *floor below which grep wins*, not a threshold we are near. `S-F5` already converts this to ≈37,000 entries; I add nothing to that arithmetic.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:26:Read together: the single published corpus-size number favouring semantic search over grep (1,000 files) came from a feature the same vendor has since replaced with an **indexed lexical search**, with no correctness number published for the replacement ("outperforms ripgrep on large codebases" is unquantified). This is an example value for R5.2, and it is a *vendor's reversal*, not a study.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:30:> "Claude Code is an agent that employs this hybrid model: [CLAUDE.md] files are naively dropped into context up front, while primitives like glob and grep allow it to navigate its environment and retrieve files just-in-time, **effectively bypassing the issues of stale indexing and complex syntax trees**."
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:32:> "Of course, there's a trade-off: **runtime exploration is slower than retrieving pre-computed data.**"
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:34:No number. It is a design statement from the vendor of the agent the record's studies measure (Claude Code appears in `V-F3` and `s3-F16`), and it is the closest published statement of the R5.1/R5.2 ordering rule: a small always-loaded index, grep for the rest.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:36:### N4 — CONFIRMED, NEW. The axis BM-6 actually measures ("characters printed") has a vendor number for what printing everything costs.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:50:> "Claude Code measures the file against the 200-line and 25KB read limits. If the file is near a limit, Claude Code reminds Claude to shorten it: **keep one line per entry, move detail into topic files**, and merge or drop stale entries."
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:54:Why this is the most useful number I found for `INDEX.md`: it is a shipping agent whose memory index has the same shape as ours (one line per entry, detail in separate files), it caps that index at **200 lines / 25KB**, and its documented overflow path is *grep and read*, not semantic retrieval. At one line per active entry, 200 lines is about 200 entries — roughly half of the400–650 entries `V-F12` derives from Anthropic's 200,000-token "just put it in the prompt" bound (`V-F8`).
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:57:`V-F3` (arXiv 2605.15184, LongMemEval-S, 116 questions, four harnesses incl. Claude Code): *"With inline delivery, lexical search is uniformly stronger than dense retrieval: inline grep exceeds inline vector for every harness-model pair."*, with the exception *"programmatic vector exceeds programmatic grep on five of ten harness-model pairs."* `V-F4` (500,000 documents, EnterpriseRAG-Bench): BM25 correctness 68.8 / vector 51.4 / bash agent 60.6. `V-F5`: dense beats BM25 for issue localization in SWE-style repos, and *"Naive GrepRAG achieves performance comparable to sophisticated graph-based baselines."*
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:59:**Reading (mine, not a source's):** the record now holds three independent vendor/harness positions pointing the same way — grep or index-and-grep at these sizes, hybrid only as an addition, vectors never alone (`V-F3`, `V-F4`, `N2`) — and one outlier favouring semantic search only above ~1,000 files (`V-F6`), which its own vendor then stopped shipping. The gap that matters for R5.1 is unchanged: **no measurement on short markdown entries** (`S-F5`, `V-F11`).
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:69:`G-F7b` is the offset on the same repos: *"CodeGraph's responses leave about **80% more retrieval context resident** at the end of a session than a file-reading agent's do — on VS Code, 67k tokens against 18k."*
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:72:`s3-F16`, arXiv 2602.20048: *"achieves 99.4% task completion on hidden-dependency tasks, a 23.2 percentage-point improvement over vanilla agents (76.2%) and 21.2 points over BM25 retrieval (78.2%)"*; and *"58% of trials with graph access made zero tool calls."*
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:88:### N10 — CONFIRMED, NEW, and thin: Aider's repo map publishes a *budget*, not a saving.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:90:> "The token budget is influenced by the `--map-tokens` switch, which defaults to 1k tokens."
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:92:and the mechanism (aider.chat/2023/10/22/repomap.html, Oct 22 2023): *"analyzing the full repo map using a graph ranking algorithm, computed on a graph where each source file is a node and edges connect files which have dependencies."* So aider pays a **graph index** to be able to send a *fixed 1k-token* map every turn. That is the correct shape for R5.3's cost argument — a bounded, always-cheap prompt-side artifact — but **no measurement of tokens saved exists** (see *Not found*).
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:99:**Reading (mine):** across four tools and five sources, the only ordering variable that predicts a code-structure tool's pay-off is *question type* (multi-file refactor, hidden dependency, transitive hierarchy), never file count. The record's smallest demonstration of a pay-off is 43 files (`N9`); its largest is 11k files at the weakest tool-call ratio (`G-F7`). Repo size is not a trigger for R5.3, and `G-F10` (no measurement on a markdown decisions corpus) is still the load-bearing gap.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/opencode.md:135:MinishLab/model2vec `results/README.md` (opened today), inference-speed table, samples/second: **`model2vec full finetune` 81,018 / `setfit` 1,010 / `minilm full finetune` 274**. Verbatim from the project README: *"makes models up to 500 times faster, with a small drop in performance"*; *"Our best model … is just ~30 MB on disk, and our smallest model just ~8 MB"*; and from the card for the model this repo's bench used, *"allowing text embeddings to be computed **orders of magnitude faster** on both GPU and CPU."* Limits: hardware unspecified; the samples are short single sentences from the classification benchmark, not document chunks; `potion-base-8M` scores 51.32 MTEB-average vs `all-MiniLM-L6-v2` 55.80 (*"91.96% of the performance"*) — a static model is a cheaper rebuild *and* a weaker index, which is the trade-off R5.2 has to record, not hide.

runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/web.md:107:## 3. When retrieval beats loading everything
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/web.md:110:- **W-18 LongMemEval** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813 (abstract) and https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813. Submitted 2024-10 (ICLR 2025). Quote: "commercial chat assistants and long-context LLMs showing a 30% accuracy drop". LongMemEval_S is "approximately 115k tokens per problem, with histories of about 50 sessions"; LongMemEval_M is 500 sessions, "around 1.5 million tokens." Long-context reading, oracle (only the right sessions) against S (everything): GPT-4o 0.870 vs 0.606 (30.3% drop); Llama 3.1 70B 0.744 vs 0.334 (55.1%); Llama 3.1 8B 0.710 vs 0.454 (36.1%); Phi-3 128k 14B 0.702 vs 0.380 (45.9%). So reading about 115K tokens of history, which fits in every window above, cost 30% to 55% of accuracy for those 2024 models.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/web.md:120:  Quote: "even LLMs with long context windows perform substantially worse as conversation length increases." Read: retrieval already matched or beat loading at 100K in 3 of 4 models and beat it in all 4 from 500K (my reading of the table). Caveats from the paper: vanilla at 10M uses "the largest recent segment that fits" each window; the Qwen vanilla baseline used 128K while RAG used 32K. Models are 2025-era. The unit is conversation tokens, not entries.
runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/web.md:121:- **W-20 Mem0, vendor** — REPORTED (vendor's own page). https://mem0.ai/blog/state-of-ai-agent-memory-2026 — page shows "Published Oct 12, 2026; first posted Apr 1, 2026". Mem0 retrieves "approximately 6,900 tokens per query" on LoCoMo (92.5), LongMemEval (94.4), BEAM 1M (64.1) and BEAM 10M (48.6); the 2025 paper's full-context baseline was "~26,000" tokens per conversation. The page says the figures are not independently reproduced and one is flagged "confirm sourcing before publishing".

runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/codex.md:565:- Proof that memory retrieval was never slow, missed, or expensive.
   175	*proposed* marks wording EJ has not decided. Each has a check, **Fails if**, that some case breaks. There are no fixed
   176	numbers: where a limit applies, it is a comparison that measurement decides.
   177	
   178	### Write — what goes in, and how
   179	
   180	**W1 — The shape of an entry** (d: What will be put in)
   181	- **W1.1** Each entry is one file: `project-memory/entries/<prefix>-<id>-<slug>.md`. The prefixes are `D-` decision,
   182	  `F-` fact, `L-` lesson and `R-` runbook, plus `O-` observation and `H-` hypothesis (*proposed*; the design leaves
   183	  these two open).
   184	- **W1.2** The header holds `id`, `kind`, `status`, `author`, the date recorded, `covers` (the code paths it is
   185	  about), its source or evidence, `depends_on`, `supersedes`, `superseded_by`, and `changed:`. A decision to build
   186	  something, once its work is complete, also holds `done:` (W5.3).
   187	- **W1.3** The body is the claim in one or two sentences, then **Why:**, then **How to apply:**. There is one claim per
   188	  entry.
   189	- **Fails if** a lint over `entries/` finds a missing field or section, or a reviewer finds two claims in one entry.
   190	
   191	**W2 — What each kind means** (v; d: What it is for)
   192	- **W2.1** A **decision** is a choice the code must follow, with the reason. A **fact** is a truth or outside
   193	  constraint the code must respect, not something discovered afterwards. Both are requirements: they are written
   194	  before the code.
   195	- **W2.2** A **lesson** comes from a failure or a human correction. An **observation** records what was seen. A
   196	  **hypothesis** is a guess, labelled as one. A **runbook** says how to run or fix something and points at a script
   197	  where one exists.
   198	- **Fails if** `kind` holds any other value, or a guess is recorded under any kind but hypothesis.
   199	
   200	**W3 — What never goes in** (v; d: What will not be put in)
   201	- **W3.1** Nothing that can be read directly from the code, the tests or the commit log: what a function does, which
   202	  test covers it, what a commit changed. The memory lives in git; the rule is about what an entry says.
   203	- **W3.2** No credentials or sensitive data.
   204	- **W3.3** No raw transcripts, and nothing from another agent's private state.
   205	- **W3.4** No work described as "verified" when it is only implemented.
   206	- **W3.5** No user memory (one person, across projects) and no agent memory (one agent name). Both live in the home
   207	  folder.
   208	- **W3.6** No copy of the project's existing documents (requirements, architecture in `docs/`): an entry links to them.
   209	- **Fails if** a secret scanner finds a credential (W3.2). For W3.1 and W3.3–W3.6, a reviewer finds such content.
   210	  Those are judged checks.
   211	
   212	**W4 — Who writes, and the change log** (v: no owners, every change logged)
   213	- **W4.1** Anyone, agent or person, may create or change any entry, and `AGENTS.md` too. There is no owner and no
   214	  approval: `status` is only `active`, `superseded` or `retired`. The one exception is Rd5.3: a reader who finds a
   215	  decision or fact out of step with the code reports it and does not settle it (EJ, 2026-10-12).
   216	- **W4.2** Every change to an entry adds one line to its `changed:` log: the time, and who made it (the model ID for an
   217	  agent, the name for a person). Earlier lines are never edited.
   218	- **W4.3** Files that are not entries (`state.md`, `journal/`, `AGENTS.md`) rely on git history.
   219	- **Fails if** git shows a change to an entry with no matching `changed:` line, or a `status` outside the three.
   220	
   221	**W5 — When to write** (d: When to write)
   222	- **W5.1** Written immediately: a decision or fact, before the code it governs; a human correction; and the state of
   223	  unfinished work, before a handoff, an interruption or a context reduction.
   224	- **W5.2** Written at the end of a task: other lessons, observations, hypotheses, and runbooks.
   225	- **W5.3** A decision to build something is started when it is written. When the work is complete, whoever finishes it
   226	  adds `done:` to the decision: the time, the result in one sentence, and the test results, or "no tests" said plainly.
   227	  `done:` points to the evidence (`runs/<run-id>/` or the commit) rather than copying test output (W3.1). A standing
   228	  rule, such as "all timestamps are UTC", is never complete and never gets `done:`. If the result differs from the
   229	  decision, that is a mismatch for Rd5.3: it is reported, not settled in `done:`. (EJ, 2026-10-12; refined after four
   230	  readers.)
   231	- **Fails if** a commit changes code under a new decision with no entry in that commit or an earlier one. For a human
   232	  correction, it fails if the session ends without one (judged). For W5.3, it fails if a reviewer of a finished task
   233	  finds that its decision has no `done:`, or a `done:` without a time, a result and test results or "no tests"
   234	  (judged; EJ, 2026-10-12).
   235	
   236	**W6 — Writers do not collide** (d: Changing entries)
   237	- **W6.1** Each new entry gets its own file. Agents never append to one shared file; each journal, the task's or the
   238	  project's, has one file per agent session.
   239	- **W6.2** Each agent works on its own branch or worktree, so two edits to the same lines meet as a merge conflict, not
   240	  a silent overwrite.
   241	- **Open:** who resolves a merge conflict.
   242	- **Fails if** an edit to an entry is lost without a conflict.
   243	
   244	### Keep — memory stays true, and nothing is lost
   245	
   246	**K1 — The decision or fact first, then the code** (v; d: What it is for)
   247	- **K1.1** To change behaviour that a decision or fact governs, change the entry first, then the code. Work that has to
   248	  go the other way is a matter for the project's workflow, not for memory.
   249	- **Fails if** git shows a commit changing the behaviour a decision or fact governs, with no change to that entry in the
   250	  same commit or an earlier one (judged).
   251	
   252	*K2 (the drift-check script) and K3 (the background subagent) were removed by EJ on 2026-10-12. Their numbers are not
   253	reused, because other text cites K4–K6. A mismatch between a decision and the code is now caught when the decision is
   254	read (Rd5).*
   255	
   256	**K4 — Never deleted, never expired by age** (v: nothing lost; d: What will not be put in)
   257	- **K4.1** An entry is never deleted. It is marked `superseded` or `retired`, links to what replaced it
   258	  (`superseded_by`), and keeps its old body.
   259	- **K4.2** Age alone never expires an entry. An entry is found out of date only when someone reads it and checks it
   260	  against the code (Rd5).
   261	- **K4.3** *Proposed:* a decision refined without changing its meaning is edited in place, with a `changed:` line; a
   262	  decision replaced by a different one gets a new entry that supersedes it. The design leaves this open.
   263	- **Fails if** git shows an entry file deleted, or a `superseded` or `retired` entry has no link.
   264	
   265	**K5 — The journal becomes entries** (v: nothing lost between sessions)
   266	- **K5.1** Task and project memory each keep a journal: a task's in `work/<task-id>/journal/`, the project's in
   267	  `journal/` for work not tied to a task. Both hold raw notes, one file per agent session, appended to and never
   268	  edited. Agent and user memory keep no journal under this design (EJ, 2026-10-12).
   269	- **K5.2** At the end of a task, and before a handoff or session end, an agent reads its task's journal (or the project
   270	  journal) written since the last read, writes what is worth keeping as entries, and logs that read in the task's `state.md`. A session cut off
   271	  before it can read loses nothing: the next read covers it (EJ, 2026-10-12: no separate milestones, the start and the
   272	  end already cover them).
   273	- **Fails if** a task's `state.md` shows it complete or handed off with no journal-read log line.
   274	
   275	**K6 — `INDEX.md` stays in step** (d: Where it is put; When to write)
   276	- **K6.1** `INDEX.md` is generated from `entries/`: one line per active entry, showing its id, kind and one-line
   277	  claim. It is never edited by hand.
   278	- **K6.2** It is regenerated right after any decision or fact is written, so the next session sees it.
   279	- **Open:** which tool generates it and what runs it (a build detail).
   280	- **Fails if** regenerating `INDEX.md` changes it, meaning it was out of step, or it lists a superseded entry as
   281	  active.
   282	
   283	### Read — the right memory reaches the agent at the right time
   284	
   285	**Rd1 — Where it lives, and readable from a clone alone** (v: plain files; never a database or service)
   286	- **Rd1.1** `AGENTS.md` sits at the repo root (`CLAUDE.md` only imports it). `project-memory/` holds `INDEX.md`,
   287	  `entries/`, `work/<task-id>/` (with `state.md`, `evidence/` and `journal/`), `journal/`, and `.cache/`, which is
   288	  optional and gitignored.
   289	- **Rd1.2** With no network, no cache and no private agent store, a fresh clone can read `INDEX.md` and every entry.
   290	- **Fails if** an entry exists only in a database, a cache or one agent's private memory.
   291	
   292	**Rd2 — Session start** (v: a fresh session carries on; d: When it will be used)
   293	- **Rd2.1** At session start, an agent reads `AGENTS.md`, `INDEX.md`, the `work/` listing and its task's `state.md`.
   294	  Agents skip optional reads, so these are loaded for the agent wherever its tool allows: an `@` import in
   295	  `CLAUDE.md`, the tool's own loading of `AGENTS.md`, or a start hook for the task's `state.md`. Only where none of
   296	  these exists does the first instruction in `AGENTS.md` ask the agent to read them (EJ, 2026-10-12: *"we should just
   297	  make sure they read all the required"*).
   298	- **Rd2.2** Not at session start, but read later at its set time:
   299	  - the task and project journals (K5.2);
   300	  - `.cache/`, only through search (Rd4.2);
   301	  - entry bodies, `evidence/` and other tasks' `state.md`, on demand (Rd4).
   302	- **Rd2.3** Never loaded, at session start or at any later point:
   303	  - superseded or retired entries, except by following a link (Rd4.4);
   304	  - the whole `project-memory/` tree at once;
   305	  - whole graph or code-map dumps;
   306	  - bulk issue or PR text;
   307	  - another agent's transcripts;
   308	  - credentials.
   309	- **Fails if** a start hook or instruction loads any memory file outside Rd2.1, or a session starts work on a task
   310	  before the four in Rd2.1 have reached it (judged, from the start of the session). It also fails if a session loads
   311	  anything in Rd2.3 at any point (judged, from the session).
   312	
   313	**Rd3 — `INDEX.md` fits every agent that reads it** (d: the size check; CACHES.md § Example numbers by case)
   314	- **Rd3.1** `INDEX.md` fits the strictest start-file limit among the agents that work on the project, and the build
   315	  checks it. *Example*, from `CACHES.md`: about 24 KB and 200 lines when Claude Code, Qwen and agy share a project.
   316	- **Fails if** `INDEX.md` exceeds that strictest limit.
   317	
   318	**Rd4 — On demand** (d: When it will be used)
   319	- **Rd4.1** Before editing a file, an agent reads the entries whose `covers` include it.
   320	- **Rd4.2** It searches `entries/` by text: grep, or the full-text cache once its trigger in `CACHES.md` fires.
   321	- **Rd4.3** For "why" questions it reads the decision entry; for why a line is the way it is, `git blame` and
   322	  `git log -S`.
   323	- **Rd4.4** It reads a superseded or retired entry only by following a link.
   324	- **Rd4.5** An agent may read another agent's memory. It treats what it reads as that agent's memory, not project
   325	  memory, and says whose it is: the agent name and the tool. For example: Claude Code agent `reviewer`,
   326	  `~/.claude/agent-memory/reviewer/` (EJ, 2026-10-12).
   327	- **Fails if** an agent edits a covered file without having read its covering decisions, or uses another agent's memory
   328	  without naming the agent and the tool (judged, from the session).
   329	
   330	**Rd5 — Check before acting** (d: When it will be used; When it will not be used)
   331	- **Rd5.1** Before acting on something read from memory, the agent checks it against the code, test or commit it
   332	  points to.
   333	- **Rd5.2** Text read from memory never overrides standing instructions.
   334	- **Rd5.3** When a decision or fact no longer matches the code, the reader does not change the entry. It writes a report
   335	  in its task's `state.md` and tells EJ: which entry, what the entry says, and what the code does instead. EJ decides
   336	  whether the entry is updated or the code is fixed, and may do either at once. Until then, the reader does not act on
   337	  the contradicted part and carries on with the rest of its work. (EJ, 2026-10-12: the report goes in `state.md`.)
   338	- **Rd5.4** Two cases are not mismatches under Rd5.3:
   339	  - a decision to build something that has no `done:` yet, whose code is still being built (W5.3);
   340	  - the reader whose own task is to make that change, which under K1.1 changes the entry first and then the code.

 The caches are two optional indexes over the project's markdown memory — full-text search and vector search. Both
 live in `project-memory/.cache/`, both are gitignored, and both are rebuilt from the files. Neither is needed today.
 
 **Who it is for.** An agent retrieving an entry on demand. Not the human reviewer, who reads the entries themselves,
-and not EJ, who sets the triggers.
+and not EJ, who decides whether a cache is built once its comparison passes (B1.0).
 
-**The problem it solves.** The memory is markdown in git, and today `INDEX.md` plus grep finds what an agent needs —
-at 10,000 entries a grep takes 0.038–0.06 s (`S-F13`). As the memory grows, that stops being enough in two ways:
-- **Too many entries — full-text search.** `INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry under its size limit,
-  so the one-line scan of everything is gone; and grep returns unranked matches, where every hit an agent opens
-  costs context (`S-F1`).
+**The problem it solves.** The memory is markdown in git, and today `INDEX.md` plus grep finds what an agent needs — in
+the researcher's own test, a one-word grep over 10,000 synthetic entries took 0.038 s (`S-F13`). As the memory grows,
+that stops being enough in two ways:
+- **Too many entries — full-text search.** Reaching the right entry through `INDEX.md` and grep means reading more
+  and more: grep returns unranked matches, and every hit an agent opens costs context (`S-F1`).
 - **Different words — vector search.** The agent's words differ from the entry's, so `INDEX.md`, grep and
   full-text search all miss an entry that exists (`V-F13`).

132:## Where the engines disagree
136:- **D1 — What answers a free-text memory question?** opencode: **FTS5 first**, when `INDEX.md` is over capacity and

141:- **D2 — Is the FTS5/vector benefit measured?** opencode presents FTS5 against grep as a material token saving
142:  (~5.8K vs ~226K per question; `S-F4`, `S-F13`). codex calls the vector benefit *"guidance, not a measured advantage
146:- **D3 — Token cost of grep + opening hits.** opencode: **~226K per question** at 10,000 entries (`S-F4`, `S-F13`).
149:- **D4 — Cost of `INDEX.md` at query time.** claude: **no extra read**, it is loaded at session start (`M17`).

107:## 3. When retrieval beats loading everything
