Quote: "commercial chat assistants and long-context LLMs showing a 30% accuracy drop". LongMemEval_S is "approximately 115k tokens per problem, with histories of about 50 sessions"; LongMemEval_M is 500 sessions, "around 1.5 million tokens." Long-context reading, oracle (only the right sessions) against S (everything): GPT-4o 0.870 vs 0.606 (30.3% drop); Llama 3.1 70B 0.744 vs 0.334 (55.1%); Llama 3.1 8B 0.710 vs 0.454 (36.1%); Phi-3 128k 14B 0.702 vs 0.380 (45.9%). So reading about 115K tokens of history, which fits in every window above, cost 30% to 55% of accuracy for those 2024 models.
111:- **W-19 BEAM** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/html/2510.27246v1 (Tavakoli et al., ICLR 2026; v1 2025-10). 100 conversations up to 10M tokens, 2,000 questions. Table 1 average score, "Vanilla" (long context) / RAG:
113:| Model | 100K | 500K | 1M | 10M |
120:  Quote: "even LLMs with long context windows perform substantially worse as conversation length increases." Read: retrieval already matched or beat loading at 100K in 3 of 4 models and beat it in all 4 from 500K (my reading of the table). Caveats from the paper: vanilla at 10M uses "the largest recent segment that fits" each window; the Qwen vanilla baseline used 128K while RAG used 32K. Models are 2025-era. The unit is conversation tokens, not entries.
123:- **W-21 Anthropic, context engineering** — CONFIRMED. https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents, 2025-09-29. Quote: "CLAUDE.md files are naively dropped into context up front" while "primitives like glob and grep allow it to navigate its environment." Subagents return summaries of "1,000-2,000 tokens" after exploring with "tens of thousands of tokens or more." No threshold.
128:## 4. When search beats grep for an agent, by corpus size
130:- **W-22 BM25 Wins at Scale** — CONFIRMED (upgrades S-F4, which had only the abstract and the smallest tier). https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26497v3 (v3 2026-07-31). Combined score, by tier (docs, corpus tokens: BM25 / File-System Agent with grep, up to 30 matching paths / DenseRAG):
142:  Tokens per query: BM25 5.8K (nearly constant), dense 4.9K, grep agent 226K at the smallest tier and 343K at 21,614 docs. Quote (from S-F4): "Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it". Reading from the table: the grep agent leads by 2.7 and 4.0 points at 1,144 and 2,254 docs, BM25 leads by 0.2 at 6,980 docs. Dense was the lowest at every size. General RAG corpus, not code or notes; one reader model; preprint. Entry equivalents are the repo's arithmetic (S-F5): 8.5M tokens is about 30,000 entries at 0.27M tokens per 1,000.
147:  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14885 (DR-DCI, 2026): raw DCI "becomes unstable" as the corpus grows from 100K to 10M documents.
148:  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20317 (2026-08-20): moving BrowseComp-Plus (about 100K documents) into a 553M-document corpus, the strongest agent "loses five points of answer accuracy" but evidence recall falls "from 84.3% to 21.4%", with 63% more search calls.
149:  - Reading: grep-agent trouble shows at 100K to 1M documents (web-size documents), well above any memory store.
150:- **V-F6 (cited):** Cursor, 2025-11-06: 12.5% average accuracy gain from semantic search; the effect on code retention grows to 2.6% "on large codebases with 1,000 files or more". Cursor's own model and benchmark. Best result is grep plus semantic.
151:- **V-F4 (cited):** EnterpriseRAG-Bench, 500K documents: BM25 68.8 / vector 51.4 / bash agent 60.6 correctness.
158:## 5. When a code graph pays off
174:- **W-26 Aider repo map** — CONFIRMED. https://aider.chat/docs/repomap.html (undated). Quote: `--map-tokens` "defaults to 1k tokens"; it "does expand the repo map significantly at times, especially when no files have been added to the chat." Ranking: "a graph ranking algorithm, computed on a graph where each source file is a node." A real default for "how much structure map to show an agent": 1,000 tokens.
179:## 6. Rebuild and index times, and sizes
182:- **W-25 CodeGraph build times** — CONFIRMED (vendor). Swift compiler repo (about 27k files) "fresh-indexes in about 100 seconds"; Linux kernel (about 70k files, about 2M symbols) indexes "in under 12 minutes" on a 2-core, 6 GB VPS; a one-file edit re-syncs in "~4" seconds. My arithmetic: about 3.7 seconds per 1,000 files (Swift) and at most about 10 seconds per 1,000 files (Linux, upper bound). Compare with G-F12 (cited): the in-repo graph, 0.94 s on this repo.
191:## 7. Recall or precision as a memory grows
193:- **W-19 BEAM (above)**: all methods fall as the dialogue grows from 1M to 10M tokens. RAG: Llama-4 0.307 to 0.249; GPT-4.1-nano 0.302 to 0.218; Gemini-2.0-flash 0.271 to 0.216; Qwen2.5-32B 0.285 to 0.211. The paper's own method LIGHT also falls: Gemini 0.284 to 0.192.

223:| Load everything versus retrieve: 100K to 1M tokens of dialogue | Llama-4-Maverick, GPT-4.1-nano, Gemini-2.0-flash, Qwen2.5-32B | RAG ahead of long-context reading for 3 of 4 models at 100K and for all 4 from 500K; e.g. Llama-4 at 100K 0.323 vs 0.240 | R5.2 | W-19 |

     1	# Node 1b — Web: published numbers per agent type and condition
     2	
     3	Run: `20261012-caches-numbers`. Written 2026-10-12. Engine: Claude subagent (web). Read-only except this file.
     4	
     5	## How to read this
     6	
     7	- **CONFIRMED** = I opened the primary source (the paper's arXiv page or HTML, the vendor's own doc or blog, the vendor's repo README). **REPORTED** = a secondary source or a search snippet only. **GAP** = not found.
     8	- **Quote reliability.** Pages were read through the WebFetch tool, which returns a model-made extract. Quotes marked (raw) were copied from the saved raw page text and are exact (W-1 only). All other quotes are as the extract returned them inside quotation marks; the numbers were cross-checked against the search snippets where both existed, but treat the wording as "as extracted", not as a byte-exact copy.
     9	- Dates are the source's own date where the page showed one. "Undated" means the page showed none; I give the fetch date, 2026-10-12.
    10	- "My arithmetic" marks a number I computed from a sourced number. It is not a finding of the source.
    11	- Earlier research ids (S-F*, V-F*, G-F*) are cited, not redone. Where this node upgrades one, it says so.

    14	
    15	## 1. Size limits each tool puts on its memory and instruction files
    16	
    17	### W-1 Claude Code — CONFIRMED
    18	- URL: https://code.claude.com/docs/en/memory — undated (fetched 2026-10-12).
    19	- Quote (raw): "The first 200 lines of `MEMORY.md`, or the first 25KB, whichever comes first, are loaded at the start of every conversation. Content beyond that threshold is not loaded at session start."
    20	- Quote (raw): "Files over 200 lines consume more context and may reduce adherence. Claude Code skips a file over 4 MiB."
    21	- Quote (raw), about CLAUDE.md: "target under 200 lines per CLAUDE.md file."
    22	- Quote (raw): "If the file is over a limit, the write still succeeds, but Claude Code returns an error telling Claude to rewrite the index" (the page continues: everything past the limit is dropped on load).
    23	- Re-verified: the repo's "200 lines or 25KB" is correct. It is a two-part limit; the first one reached applies.
    24	- My arithmetic: 25 KB / 200 lines = 125 bytes per line. Lines longer than that make the byte limit bind first. Measured here: `wc -lc` on this project's `MEMORY.md` = 19 lines, 3,182 bytes; median line 159 bytes, max 218. At about 167 bytes per line (3,182 / 19), 25 KB is reached at about 150 lines, before the 200-line limit.
    25	
    26	### W-2 Codex CLI — CONFIRMED
    27	- URL: https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/agent-configuration/agents-md (redirect target of https://developers.openai.com/codex/guides/agents-md) — undated.
    28	- Quote: "Codex skips empty files and stops adding files once the combined size reaches the limit defined by `project_doc_max_bytes` (32 KiB by default)."
    29	- The extract also gave: raise the limit or split instructions across nested directories "when you hit the cap"; example config sets 65536. The cap applies to the combined guidance, not per file. Codex concatenates files from the root down; at the global level it uses only the first non-empty file of `AGENTS.override.md` / `AGENTS.md`.
    30	- Config key default 32768 bytes: REPORTED by a search snippet of OpenAI's config sample (https://developers.openai.com/zh-Hans/docs/config-file/config-sample, "Default: 32768"); I did not open it.
    31	- Not established: whether truncation is silent. A third-party post says so; the official page does not say (UNPROVEN).
    32	
    33	### W-3 Cursor — CONFIRMED
    34	- URL: https://cursor.com/docs/context/rules — undated.
    35	- Quote: "Keep rules under 500 lines." Also "Split large rules into multiple, composable rules."
    36	- Always-apply rules: "Always included. Globs and description are ignored." No token or character limit is stated.
    37	- This is a recommendation, not an enforced limit.
    38	
    39	### W-4 Google Antigravity — CONFIRMED, with a conflict
    40	- URL: https://www.antigravity.google/docs/rules?tab=ide — undated.
    41	- Quote: "Antigravity truncates any single rule file that exceeds 24,000 bytes" (after `@[label](path)` includes are expanded). Limit is in bytes, so multi-byte text costs more.
    42	- Conflict (REPORTED): a search snippet says the same docs' Rules page and a third-party guide give "12,000 characters" per Rules file, and a Flutter docs table lists "12,000 characters" as a hard limit (https://flutter.googlesource.com/mirrors/flutter.git/+/HEAD/docs/rules/, not opened). The page I opened says 24,000 bytes. Likely a doc revision, but unproven. A design guide should say "between 12,000 characters and 24,000 bytes depending on version" or re-check at use time.
    43	- Whether the same limit applies to a root `AGENTS.md` / `GEMINI.md`: GAP.
    44	
    45	### W-5 Gemini CLI — GAP for a limit
    46	- URL: https://geminicli.com/docs/cli/gemini-md/ — "last updated Jun 18, 2026" (per the extract). CONFIRMED that no limit is stated.
    47	- Quote: the CLI "concatenates the contents of all found files, and sends them to the model with every prompt."
    48	- So the cost of `GEMINI.md` is paid on every prompt and no cap protects the user. `Auto Memory` appears only as a nav link marked experimental; no details.
    49	
    50	### W-6 opencode — GAP for a limit
    51	- URL: https://opencode.ai/docs/rules/ — "Last updated: Oct 12, 2026". CONFIRMED that no size limit is stated; the only number is "Remote instructions are fetched with a 5 second timeout." Files from `opencode.json` instructions are "combined with your `AGENTS.md` files."
    52	
    53	### W-7 Letta / MemGPT core memory blocks — partial
    54	- URL: https://docs.letta.com/guides/core-concepts/memory/memory-blocks — undated. CONFIRMED: "A `limit`, which is the size limit (in characters) of the block." Examples set 5000, 4000 and 6000 characters. Default value and over-limit behaviour: GAP (the docs page does not state them; the source file `letta/constants.py` returned 404 on both the raw URL and `gh api`, so I could not check it).
    55	- A different package (pi-memory-blocks) shows a 2000-character default in a search snippet; that is not Letta (do not use).
    56	- MemGPT paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08560 (2023). CONFIRMED the idea only: it "manages different memory tiers in order to effectively provide extended context within the LLM's limited context window." No block size there.
    57	
    58	### W-17 Do instruction files help at all? — CONFIRMED (abstract)
    59	- URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11988 — submitted 2026-02-12 (v3 revised 2026-09-29). ETH Zurich + LogicStar.
    60	- Quote: "providing context files does not generally improve task success rates"; "increasing inference cost by over 20% on average." Setup (from a search snippet, REPORTED): SWE-bench tasks with LLM-generated files plus AgentBench, 138 tasks from repositories with developer-written context files; conclusion that files should describe "only minimal requirements."
    61	- Supports: an index read at session start must earn its tokens; it is not free.
    62	
    63	### W-16 How many instructions can be followed — CONFIRMED (abstract)
    64	- URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11538 — submitted 2025-07-15. 20 models, instruction count 10 to 500 (keyword-inclusion, report writing).
    65	- Quote: "even the best frontier models only achieve 68% accuracy at the max density of 500 instructions." Three decay shapes (threshold, linear, exponential) by model; bias toward earlier instructions (decay shapes: REPORTED from a search snippet).
    66	- Caveat: synthetic keyword task, 2025-era models. It bears on a start-of-session index only as "rules in a list are not all obeyed as the list grows".
    67	
    68	---
    69	
    70	## 2. Context window of each current model, and effective context
    71	
    72	### W-8 Claude — CONFIRMED
    73	- URLs: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/models/overview and https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/context-windows — undated.
    74	- Quote: Fable 5.1, Opus 5.5, Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 5.5 all show "1M tokens" context window, 128K max output. The context-windows page lists Opus 4.6 through 5.5, Sonnet 4.6/5/5.5 and others as 1M; "Other Claude models, including Claude Sonnet 4.5 (deprecated), have a 200k-token context window."
    75	- Quote: "1M tokens is roughly 555k words or 2.5M Unicode characters on the current tokenizer."
    76	- Quote: "more context isn't automatically better. As token count grows, accuracy and recall degrade, a phenomenon known as *context rot*."
    77	- Note: Anthropic's own doc states context rot as a fact but gives no number on that page.
    78	
    79	### W-9 Other vendors' windows
    80	| Model | Window | Status | Source (date) |
    81	|---|---|---|---|
    82	| GPT-6 Astra, GPT-6.1 Sol, GPT-6 Luna (OpenAI API) | 1.05M | CONFIRMED | https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models (undated) |
    83	| Codex CLI models | not stated on the Codex models page | GAP | https://learn.chatgpt.com/docs/models (recommends "GPT-6.1 Sol"; no window) |
    84	| Gemini 3.1 Pro (Preview) | input 1,048,576; output 65,536 | CONFIRMED | https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/models/gemini-3.1-pro-preview (undated) |
    85	| DeepSeek V4 (Pro, Flash) | 1M; max output 384K | CONFIRMED | https://api-docs.deepseek.com/quick_start/pricing (footer 2026; model "DeepSeek-V4-Pro-0813") |
    86	| MiniMax-M3 / M3.1-Flash-Preview | 1,000,000 | CONFIRMED | https://platform.minimax.io/docs/guides/text-generation (undated) |
    87	| MiniMax-M2.7, M2.5, M2.1, M2 | 204,800 | CONFIRMED | same page |
    88	| Qwen3.5-397B-A17B | 262,144 natively, extensible to 1,010,000 | CONFIRMED | https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-397B-A17B (undated). Quote: "Context Length: 262,144 natively and extensible up to 1,010,000 tokens." Hosted Qwen3.5-Plus: "1M context length by default". YaRN extension is static and "potentially impacting performance on shorter texts." |
    89	
    90	Cursor and opencode have no window of their own; they use whichever model is selected. Antigravity/Gemini CLI: the model's window above.
    91	
    92	### Effective context: the research
    93	
    94	- **W-10 RULER** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.06654, 2024-04-09 (v3 2024-08-06). Quote: "While these models all claim context sizes of 32K tokens or greater"; "only half of them can maintain satisfactory performance at the length of 32K." 17 models. Old models.
    95	- **W-11 NoLiMa** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05167, 2025-02-07 (v3 2025-07-09). Quote: "At 32K, for instance, 11 models drop below 50% of their strong short-length baselines." GPT-4o "from an almost-perfect baseline of 99.3% to 69.7%." Needles with little word overlap with the question (the case for memory entries phrased differently from the question).
    96	- **W-12 Lost in the middle** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.03172, 2023-07-06 (v3 2023-11-20, TACL). Quote: performance "significantly degrades when models must access relevant information in the middle of long contexts." Supports putting the key lines of an index first and last, not in the middle.
    97	- **W-13 Chroma context rot** — CONFIRMED. https://www.trychroma.com/research/context-rot, 2025-07-14. 18 models (Claude Opus 4/Sonnet 4/3.7/3.5/Haiku 3.5, o3, GPT-4.1 family, GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5/2.0, Qwen3 235B/32B/8B). Quote: "model performance consistently degrades with increasing input length." Quote: "performance degrades more quickly in input length with lower similarity needle-question pairs." LongMemEval subset (306 prompts): focused prompts about 300 tokens against full prompts about 113k tokens, "Performance was consistently higher on focused prompts"; exact accuracy only in charts (GAP for the numbers).
    98	- **W-14 Anthropic, Opus 4.6** — CONFIRMED. https://www.anthropic.com/news/claude-opus-4-6, 2026-02-05. Quote: Opus 4.6 "scores 76%, whereas Sonnet 4.5 scores just 18.5%" on MRCR v2 8-needle at 1M. Calls it "a qualitative shift in how much context a model can actually use." This is the best vendor number for "window is not effective context" for a Claude model, but Opus 4.6 is not the current generation (Opus 5.5 is).
    99	- **W-15 MRCR v2 across vendors** — REPORTED. https://yage.ai/share/long-context-benchmark-en-20260315.html, 2026-03-15, compiling vendor system cards and model cards. Its table: Opus 4.6 93.0% at 256K and 76.0% at 1M; Sonnet 4.6 90.3% / 65.8%; GPT-5.4 79.3% (128K–256K bin) and 36.6% (512K–1M bin); Gemini 3.1 Pro 84.9% at 128K and 26.3% at 1M; Gemini 3 Pro 45.4% at 256K and 24.5% at 1M. The post itself warns scores from different sources "cannot be directly compared." I did not open the vendor reports behind the GPT and Gemini cells.
   100	- **GAP:** no published long-context score found for the current generation (Claude Opus 5.5 / Sonnet 5.5 / Fable 5.1, GPT-6.x, Gemini 3.6/3.8, DeepSeek V4, MiniMax M3). All the effective-context numbers above are for older models. An example value for a 1M-window model today would have to say "Opus 4.6-era: 76% at 1M, 93% at 256K".
   101	- **W-31 Gemini** — CONFIRMED. https://ai.google.dev/gemini-api/docs/long-context (undated). Quote: "You can get ~99% on a single query, but you have to pay the input token cost every time you send that query." And with multiple needles, "the model does not perform with the same accuracy." No number given for multi-needle.
   102	
   103	What this gives the guide for "how big an index read at session start": the cited limits (W-1, W-2, W-3, W-4) are all 25 to 32 KB or 500 lines; the research says accuracy loss shows up from tens of thousands of tokens on older models and from a few hundred thousand on newer ones (W-10, W-11, W-14), and that a rule list loses adherence as it grows (W-16, W-17). All of that is far above a few KB. So an index of a few KB is not near any effective-context problem; the limits matter for cost and adherence, not for recall.
   104	
   105	---
   106	
   107	## 3. When retrieval beats loading everything
   108	
   109	- **V-F8 (cited, not redone):** Anthropic 2024: under 200,000 tokens, include the whole knowledge base. Still the only vendor threshold found.
   110	- **W-18 LongMemEval** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.10813 (abstract) and https://arxiv.org/html/2410.10813. Submitted 2024-10 (ICLR 2025). Quote: "commercial chat assistants and long-context LLMs showing a 30% accuracy drop". LongMemEval_S is "approximately 115k tokens per problem, with histories of about 50 sessions"; LongMemEval_M is 500 sessions, "around 1.5 million tokens." Long-context reading, oracle (only the right sessions) against S (everything): GPT-4o 0.870 vs 0.606 (30.3% drop); Llama 3.1 70B 0.744 vs 0.334 (55.1%); Llama 3.1 8B 0.710 vs 0.454 (36.1%); Phi-3 128k 14B 0.702 vs 0.380 (45.9%). So reading about 115K tokens of history, which fits in every window above, cost 30% to 55% of accuracy for those 2024 models.
   111	- **W-19 BEAM** — CONFIRMED. https://arxiv.org/html/2510.27246v1 (Tavakoli et al., ICLR 2026; v1 2025-10). 100 conversations up to 10M tokens, 2,000 questions. Table 1 average score, "Vanilla" (long context) / RAG:
   112	
   113	| Model | 100K | 500K | 1M | 10M |
   114	|---|---|---|---|---|
   115	| Llama-4-Maverick | 0.240 / 0.323 | 0.283 / 0.330 | 0.259 / 0.307 | 0.104 / 0.249 |
   116	| GPT-4.1-nano | 0.239 / 0.309 | 0.194 / 0.314 | 0.191 / 0.302 | 0.109 / 0.218 |
   117	| Gemini-2.0-flash | 0.242 / 0.280 | 0.257 / 0.267 | 0.199 / 0.271 | 0.122 / 0.216 |
   118	| Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ | 0.280 / 0.269 | 0.200 / 0.291 | 0.193 / 0.285 | 0.133 / 0.211 |
   119	
   120	  Quote: "even LLMs with long context windows perform substantially worse as conversation length increases." Read: retrieval already matched or beat loading at 100K in 3 of 4 models and beat it in all 4 from 500K (my reading of the table). Caveats from the paper: vanilla at 10M uses "the largest recent segment that fits" each window; the Qwen vanilla baseline used 128K while RAG used 32K. Models are 2025-era. The unit is conversation tokens, not entries.
   121	- **W-20 Mem0, vendor** — REPORTED (vendor's own page). https://mem0.ai/blog/state-of-ai-agent-memory-2026 — page shows "Published Oct 12, 2026; first posted Apr 1, 2026". Mem0 retrieves "approximately 6,900 tokens per query" on LoCoMo (92.5), LongMemEval (94.4), BEAM 1M (64.1) and BEAM 10M (48.6); the 2025 paper's full-context baseline was "~26,000" tokens per conversation. The page says the figures are not independently reproduced and one is flagged "confirm sourcing before publishing".
   122	- **W-13 (above)**: focused ~300 tokens against full ~113k, same questions: focused better.
   123	- **W-21 Anthropic, context engineering** — CONFIRMED. https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents, 2025-09-29. Quote: "CLAUDE.md files are naively dropped into context up front" while "primitives like glob and grep allow it to navigate its environment." Subagents return summaries of "1,000-2,000 tokens" after exploring with "tens of thousands of tokens or more." No threshold.
   124	- **GAP:** no newer vendor threshold than 200K tokens, and no threshold stated in entries. S-F5 (my arithmetic in the repo: 200K tokens is about 400 to 650 entries at 300 to 500 tokens each, V-F12) stays an estimate.
   125	
   126	---
   127	
   128	## 4. When search beats grep for an agent, by corpus size
   129	
   130	- **W-22 BM25 Wins at Scale** — CONFIRMED (upgrades S-F4, which had only the abstract and the smallest tier). https://arxiv.org/html/2607.26497v3 (v3 2026-07-31). Combined score, by tier (docs, corpus tokens: BM25 / File-System Agent with grep, up to 30 matching paths / DenseRAG):
   131	
   132	| Docs | Corpus tokens | BM25 | Grep agent | Dense |
   133	|---|---|---|---|---|
   134	| 1,144 | 1.7M | 74.7 | 77.4 | 58.1 |
   135	| 2,254 | 3.0M | 71.4 | 75.4 | 55.7 |
   136	| 6,980 | 8.5M | 70.1 | 69.9 | 51.0 |
   137	| 21,614 | 25.6M | 64.9 | 62.6 | 44.2 |
   138	| 66,932 | 78.6M | 59.5 | 56.7 | 38.1 |
   139	| 131,876 | 154.7M | 55.2 | 50.9 | 36.0 |
   140	| 511,959 | 600.8M | 50.5 | 30.7 | 29.9 |
   141	
   142	  Tokens per query: BM25 5.8K (nearly constant), dense 4.9K, grep agent 226K at the smallest tier and 343K at 21,614 docs. Quote (from S-F4): "Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it". Reading from the table: the grep agent leads by 2.7 and 4.0 points at 1,144 and 2,254 docs, BM25 leads by 0.2 at 6,980 docs. Dense was the lowest at every size. General RAG corpus, not code or notes; one reader model; preprint. Entry equivalents are the repo's arithmetic (S-F5): 8.5M tokens is about 30,000 entries at 0.27M tokens per 1,000.
   143	  The accuracy gap is small in the first tiers; the token cost gap is large (226K vs 5.8K per question at 1,144 docs). So at small sizes the case for an index is cost, not accuracy.
   144	- **W-23 Direct corpus interaction (grep agents) at large scale** — CONFIRMED abstracts.
   145	  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05242 (Li et al., 2026-05-03): grep-and-shell agents "without any embedding model, vector index, or retrieval API"; outperform sparse, dense and reranking baselines on several BRIGHT and BEIR sets. No numbers on the abstract page.
   146	  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06880 (Zhuang et al., 2026): "latency degrades sharply as the corpus grows"; at 1M documents RISE-BM25 reaches 81% (gpt-5.4-mini) while pure-shell DCI on gpt-5.4-nano "degrades to 60%, with 33 of 100 queries failing on wall-clock time."
   147	  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14885 (DR-DCI, 2026): raw DCI "becomes unstable" as the corpus grows from 100K to 10M documents.
   148	  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20317 (2026-08-20): moving BrowseComp-Plus (about 100K documents) into a 553M-document corpus, the strongest agent "loses five points of answer accuracy" but evidence recall falls "from 84.3% to 21.4%", with 63% more search calls.
   149	  - Reading: grep-agent trouble shows at 100K to 1M documents (web-size documents), well above any memory store.
   150	- **V-F6 (cited):** Cursor, 2025-11-06: 12.5% average accuracy gain from semantic search; the effect on code retention grows to 2.6% "on large codebases with 1,000 files or more". Cursor's own model and benchmark. Best result is grep plus semantic.
   151	- **V-F4 (cited):** EnterpriseRAG-Bench, 500K documents: BM25 68.8 / vector 51.4 / bash agent 60.6 correctness.
   152	- **S-F6 (cited):** LongMemEval QA, grep beat vector in a harness study (Claude Opus 4.6 in Claude Code: inline grep 76.7, inline vector 75.0).
   153	- **W-28 ripgrep, the floor for grep** — CONFIRMED but old. https://burntsushi.net/ripgrep/ (2016; hardware: EC2 c3.2xlarge, Ubuntu 16.04). Linux kernel source, pattern `PM_RESUME`: "rg 0.349 +/- 0.104", "git grep 0.342 +/- 0.005". One 1 GB file: "rg 0.268", "grep 0.516". So a text search over a source tree the size of the Linux kernel took about a third of a second on 2016 hardware. This sets the latency baseline: a memory store of a few thousand short files is far smaller.
   154	- **GAP:** no controlled study of full-text or vector against grep on a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short notes or memory entries (S-F5 gap holds). The smallest controlled tier found is 1,144 documents (W-22).
   155

   357	
   132	## Where the engines disagree
   133	
   134	No average and no silent winner. `unresolved` means the three files cannot settle it.
   135	
   136	- **D1 — What answers a free-text memory question?** opencode: **FTS5 first**, when `INDEX.md` is over capacity and
   137	  ranking matters, with vector only for meaning (`S-F1`, `S-F4`, `S-F3`). codex: **baseline first** — `INDEX.md` and
   138	  grep — then vector "alongside keyword search" when wording mismatch defeats the baseline; codex names **no FTS5**
   139	  anywhere (`V-F11`, `V-F13`, `M4`). claude: **no store at all** for any memory question (`G-F2`, `G-F3`, `G-F10`,
   140	  `M17`, `M19`). **unresolved.**
   141	- **D2 — Is the FTS5/vector benefit measured?** opencode presents FTS5 against grep as a material token saving
   142	  (~5.8K vs ~226K per question; `S-F4`, `S-F13`). codex calls the vector benefit *"guidance, not a measured advantage
   143	  here"* (`V-F11`, `V-F13`, `M4`) and says its synthetic timings *"do not establish vector savings."* claude says
   144	  memory-corpus costs are GAP and there is no head-to-head (`S-F5`, `V-F11`, `G-F10`). **unresolved** — whether
   145	  `S-F4`'s 1,144-document BM25 study transfers to this project's 10,000-entry claim is not settled by the three files.
   146	- **D3 — Token cost of grep + opening hits.** opencode: **~226K per question** at 10,000 entries (`S-F4`, `S-F13`).
   147	  claude: **GAP per question**, "because it depends on hits and grep is unranked". codex: **tokens were not
   148	  measured** (`S-F13`). **unresolved.**
   149	- **D4 — Cost of `INDEX.md` at query time.** claude: **no extra read**, it is loaded at session start (`M17`).
   150	  opencode: **<0.01 s** scan, minimal header-only tokens (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`). codex: **time GAP and tokens GAP**
   151	  (`M17`, `M19`). Two engines read the same `M17` differently. **unresolved.**
   152	- **D5 — Does `INDEX.md` carry `covers:` (and status) per line?** codex asserts the `covers:` match directly
   153	  (`V-F21`). opencode asserts status per line (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH:100`). claude hedges: `RECOMMENDATION.md`'s Layout
   154	  says the lines carry `covers`, `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` says only *"one line per active entry"* — so if `covers` is not
   155	  there, that row falls back to grep over `entries/`. **unresolved** (a documentation conflict; the three files do
   156	  not settle which doc is right).
   157	- **D6 — What counts as a fired trigger?** opencode treats a **size crossover (~37K entries)** as a build condition
   158	  (`S-F4`, `S-F5`, `S-F13`). codex treats only a **recorded miss count** as one and says corpus size alone does not
   159	  justify vectors (`V-F11`, `V-F13`, `M4`). claude requires a **demonstrated baseline failure** (`M21`) plus a fired
   160	  R5.3 condition. **unresolved** — the three name three different currencies.
   161	- **Not disagreements, for the record (one engine only, so the table must not read as consensus):** the code graph's
   162	  lane, its triggers and its drift rule rest on claude alone (codex and opencode did not address code structure); the
   163	  `~37K` grep crossover, the `0.0006 s` FTS5 figure and the `~5.8K`/`~226K` token pair rest on opencode alone; the
   164	  vector timings rest on codex alone.

   433	
   434	**When an index read stops fitting**
   435	
   436	There are three distinct boundaries:
   437	
   438	- **Whole-context capacity:** the formula above. A memory-entry count cannot determine it without the index’s characters per entry.
   439	- **Automatic loading caps:** Claude’s documented `MEMORY.md` cap is **200 lines or 25KB**, whichever comes first. Qwen’s installed source defines `MAX_MANAGED_AUTO_MEMORY_INDEX_LINES = 200` and `MAX_MANAGED_AUTO_MEMORY_INDEX_BYTES = 25e3` at [chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:84471](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:84471). These can bind long before model capacity.
   440	- **A tool’s advisory budget:** Qwen defines `MEMORY_CONTEXT_WARNING_RATIO = 0.15` at [chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:146045](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:146045). Its warning estimates tokens from character count and compares all always-loaded context against that fraction at [line 147724](/home/smoke01/.npm-global/lib/node_modules/@qwen-code/qwen-code/chunks/chunk-GXSNGY5M.js:147724). This is a software warning threshold, **not measured evidence of degradation**.
   441	
   442	Current agy documentation also specifies a “24 KB (`24,000` bytes) per-file limit” and a “20,000-token aggregate rules budget.” Over-budget rules may become pointers rather than inline text. [Antigravity rules](https://www.antigravity.google/docs/rules/), accessed 2026-10-12. **GAP:** verified behavior of those limits on SEZA’s installed CLI.
   443	
   444	Codex’s instruction cap and DSH’s instruction-loader byte budget apply if an index is placed in that instruction chain. An ordinary subsequent file read consumes model context but is not thereby governed by the same loader cap.
   445	
   446	For an illustration tied to SEZA’s actual index, the following arithmetic assumes a future index has the same average line density as Claude’s current index. **This extrapolation is mine, not a finding.** Its hypothetical entry counts are capacity illustrations, not cache-adoption triggers.
    15	### N1 — CONFIRMED (by id). The one size-ordered crossover in the record is 10M tokens, and it is *above* our entry sizes.
    16	`S-F4`, arXiv 2607.26497v3 (v3 2026-07-31), a general RAG corpus, not code and not memory entries:
    17	> "Around 10 million corpus tokens, BM25 overtakes it and leads at every larger shared tier"
    18	
    19	and at the smallest tier (1,144 documents / 1.7M corpus tokens) BM25 used **5.8K tokens per question against 226K** for the grep-and-read agent — but the agent **led at the smallest shared tiers**. So the crossover is a *floor below which grep wins*, not a threshold we are near. `S-F5` already converts this to ≈37,000 entries; I add nothing to that arithmetic.
    20	
    21	### N2 — CONFIRMED, NEW, and the most useful thing I found: the only file-count threshold in the record was withdrawn by its own vendor 15 days later.
    22	- `V-F6` (cited, cursor.com/blog/semsearch, 2025-11-06): semantic search *"significantly improves agent performance, especially over large codebases"*; *"This effect increases to 2.6% on large codebases with 1,000 files or more"*; *"the combination of these two leads to the best outcomes."*
    23	- **NEW**, cursor changelog **2.1, Nov 21 2025** (opened today): *"Instant Grep (Beta) — All grep commands run by the agent are now instant."*
    24	- **NEW**, cursor.com/docs/context/codebase-indexing (undated page, opened today): *"Cursor ships with **Instant Grep**, a custom search engine that outperforms `ripgrep` on large codebases."* and *"Cursor does not upload file paths or code to build a search index, and it **does not store embeddings of your codebase for search**."*
    25	
    26	Read together: the single published corpus-size number favouring semantic search over grep (1,000 files) came from a feature the same vendor has since replaced with an **indexed lexical search**, with no correctness number published for the replacement ("outperforms ripgrep on large codebases" is unquantified). This is an example value for R5.2, and it is a *vendor's reversal*, not a study.
    27	
    28	### N3 — CONFIRMED, NEW. Anthropic's own architecture note says the hybrid is index-up-front plus grep-just-in-time, and names the price.
    29	anthropic.com/engineering/effective-context-engineering-for-ai-agents, published **Sep 29 2025**:
    30	> "Claude Code is an agent that employs this hybrid model: [CLAUDE.md] files are naively dropped into context up front, while primitives like glob and grep allow it to navigate its environment and retrieve files just-in-time, **effectively bypassing the issues of stale indexing and complex syntax trees**."
    31	
    32	> "Of course, there's a trade-off: **runtime exploration is slower than retrieving pre-computed data.**"
    33	
    34	No number. It is a design statement from the vendor of the agent the record's studies measure (Claude Code appears in `V-F3` and `s3-F16`), and it is the closest published statement of the R5.1/R5.2 ordering rule: a small always-loaded index, grep for the rest.
    35	
    36	### N4 — CONFIRMED, NEW. The axis BM-6 actually measures ("characters printed") has a vendor number for what printing everything costs.
    37	anthropic.com/engineering/code-execution-with-mcp, published **Nov 04 2025**:
    38	> "This reduces the token usage from 150,000 tokens to 2,000 tokens—a time and cost saving of 98.7%."
    39	
    40	Context: this is about tool definitions/results, not search. It matters here because it is the largest published effect size for *not* printing a result set into context — the same lever `BM-6` pulls (grep 4,304 mean output characters vs full-text 1,817, `BM-6`). It is evidence for the proxy, not for a crossover.
    41	
    42	### N5 — CONFIRMED, NEW. The only published *size cap on a memory index* anywhere is a line-per-memory file, and over the cap the documented behaviour is read-on-demand with ordinary file tools — not a vector search.
    90	
    91	| When | What |
    92	|---|---|
    93	| Immediately | Decisions and facts, before the code — and `INDEX.md` is regenerated right after, so the next session sees them. A human correction. The state of unfinished work before a handoff, an interruption or a context reduction. |
    94	| When the work a decision to build something is complete | The decision's `done:` line, written by whoever finishes the work: the time, the result, and the test results or "no tests", with a pointer to the evidence. |
    95	| At the end of a task | Other lessons, observations and hypotheses; merging duplicates; runbooks. |
    96	
    97	**Keeping it current.** Entries may go out of date while work goes on; that is accepted. Nothing watches for it: there
    98	is no drift-check script and no background subagent. Whoever reads a decision or fact checks it against the code before
    99	acting on it. When they no longer match, the reader does not change the entry. It writes a report in its task's
   100	`state.md` and tells EJ which entry, what the entry says, and what the code does instead. EJ decides whether the entry
   101	is updated or the code is fixed. Until then, the reader leaves the contradicted part alone and carries on with the
   102	rest. A decision still being built (no `done:` yet), or one the reader's own task is changing, is not a mismatch
   103	(Rd5.4).
   104	
   105	**Still open:** which tool generates `INDEX.md` and what runs it (K6); who resolves a git merge conflict (W6). Whether a
   106	changed decision is edited in place or superseded is proposed in K4.3.
   107	
   108	## What will not be put in
   109	
   110	- Anything that can be read directly from the code, the tests or the commit log (W3.1). The memory itself lives in git.
   111	- Credentials or sensitive data.
   112	- Guesses not labelled as a hypothesis.
   113	- Raw transcripts, or another agent's private state.
   114	- Work described as "verified" when it is only implemented.
   115	
   116	An entry is never deleted: it is marked `superseded` or `retired` and linked to what replaced it. Age alone never
   117	expires an entry. Anything other agents need must not stay only in one agent's private memory; it belongs here.
   118	
   119	## Where it is put
   120	
   121	At the repo root:
   122

The stores picked it up — which is rule 1 working, but it means these numbers are a snapshot of a corpus in motion. Re-run before the tables are used.

   138	## Rules for every cache
   139	
   140	These apply to whichever cache is built. They come from how the researched tools fail.
   141	
   142	- **Full rebuild, never patched.** A live, incrementally updated index drifted silently from a clean rebuild: 4.3% of
   143	  edges wrong, with every integrity check passing (`G-F5 drift`). Its makers decided drift cannot be measured cheaply
   144	  (`G-F5c`). An FTS rebuild of 10,000 synthetic entries takes 0.53 s (`S-F13`).
   145	- **Rebuild before answering when stale.** Before each query, compare the recorded content hash of the
   146	  sources with the index and rebuild if they differ. Build into a temporary file and rename it over the old one
   147	  (`S-F9`, quoted: *"A stale index is worse than no index."*).
   148	- **Fall back when absent.** If the cache file or SQLite's FTS5 is missing, the tool answers with grep and says so in
   149	  every answer (Rd3.2) (`S-F9`); the same for a missing embedding model (my reading, extending `S-F9`). A mistyped
   150	  database path silently creates an empty database, which looks exactly like a stale one (`G-F5 drift`, failure
   151	  mode 5), so the tool opens the cache read-only and treats "no such file" as absent.
   152	- **Index only active entries** — for `project-memory/` — the same set as `INDEX.md`, with `covers` stored
   153	  beside each row (`V-F22`, my reading). Otherwise superseded entries come back looking current.

codex
### Verdict

REPAIR — my reading, codex: the main changes follow EJ’s decisions, but dependent wording, evidence claims and unresolved-status statements still mislead.

### Findings

1. **Location: K2.3, `CACHES.md:402`. Severity: high.**

   **Quoted text:** “The full-text and vector indexes are rebuilt by one command, in one step. If either fails, neither replaces the old ones”.

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** This unconditionally requires both indexes even when only full-text has been approved. It can make a full-text rebuild depend on a vector index or embedding model that must not yet exist.

   **Source line — quoted:** B1.2, `CACHES.md:344`: “it is built only after B1.1 has fired”; B5.1, `CACHES.md:381`: “the full-text path still works.”

   **Fix:** Apply the joint rebuild and joint publication rule when both indexes are built. State that a full-text-only installation rebuilds full-text alone.

2. **Location: Rd4.1, `CACHES.md:454`. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “Every other question goes unrecorded”.

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** B1.1 also uses a journal note about a successful search that required reading many hits. That question is not necessarily a miss, so the blanket prohibition conflicts with the new full-text comparison event.

   **Source line — quoted:** B1.1, `CACHES.md:327`: “when an agent notes in its journal that `INDEX.md` plus grep made it read through many hits to reach an entry”.

   **Fix:** Say ordinary searches need no log, while preserving the specific B1.1 reading-friction notes alongside Rd4.1 miss notes.

3. **Location: `CACHES.md:149`, Rules for every cache. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “the same for a missing embedding model”.

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** The preceding sentence sends the query to grep. The accepted Rd3.3 now sends it to available full-text first.

   **Source line — quoted:** Rd3.3, `CACHES.md:442`: “A missing embedding model falls back to full-text if it is there, and to grep if not; the answer says so.”

   **Fix:** Copy that fallback order into the summary rule, including disclosure of the route used.

4. **Location: K3.1 and Rd4.1, `CACHES.md:411`. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “for the vector index, on the noted misses”.

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** Journal notes persist, but their target entries can subsequently become superseded or retired. The design does not say how those historical targets participate in a comparison against today’s active-only index. A formerly valid target can become deliberately unsearchable, then appear to demonstrate that the cache stopped paying.

   **Source line — quoted:** B4.1, `CACHES.md:371`: “every file in `entries/` whose own header says `status: active`, and nothing else”. Rd4.2, `CACHES.md:457`: questions are written “from the entries that exist then”.

   **Fix:** State how journal misses are reconciled with the comparison’s current active corpus. Preserve the historical notes, but distinguish an ineligible old target from a retrieval failure.

5. **Location: `CACHES.md:549`, Example numbers by case. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “K2 (stale never answers) matters more here than any of the three stores.”

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** This connects stale memory content to cache freshness. K2 detects disagreement between an index and its entry files; it cannot detect an outdated statement faithfully indexed from an unchanged file. The missing projection is instead relevant to Rd3.

   **Source lines — quoted:** K2.1, `CACHES.md:395`: “compares the recorded content hash with the entry files”. CX, `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/codex.md:341`: outdated information remains “unless memory maintenance and source checking addressed it.”

   **Fix:** Explain that stale statements require checking the source content, while an unavailable projection motivates fallback. Keep K2’s claim limited to agreement with the files.

6. **Location: `CACHES.md:518`, Example numbers by case. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “On **tokens read**, full-text wins much earlier: on SEZA-sized memory, a single broad term already floods grep’s output. The B1.1 comparison measures exactly that.”

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** Large grep output establishes a reason to compare routes, not a full-text win. The SEZA measurement did not measure the paired reading path and found full-text misses. B1.1 also requires no more misses.

   **Source lines — quoted:** SZ, `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/seza.md:337`: “n=5, no verdict.” Its `:349` says it recorded “bytes printed and rank, not the full reading path of an agent”.

   **Fix:** Describe broad output as possible reading friction. Leave the winner unmeasured until the paired B1.1 comparison establishes both reading savings and misses.

7. **Location: `CACHES.md:526` and `:535`, Example numbers by case. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “Loading about 115K tokens of history instead of retrieving”; “it is keyword retrieval that wins.”

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** W-18 compares full history with oracle-selected relevant sessions, not with an implemented retrieval route. W-19 reports RAG against vanilla long context; the opened report does not establish that its result is specifically a keyword-retrieval win. The conclusion combines different experiments into a stronger claim.

   **Source lines — quoted:** W-18, `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/web.md:110`: “oracle (only the right sessions) against S (everything)”. W-19, `:111`: “‘Vanilla’ (long context) / RAG”.

   **Fix:** Label W-18 as oracle context selection and W-19 as RAG versus long context. Keep the BM25-versus-dense findings separate.

8. **Location: `CACHES.md:531`, Example numbers by case. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “Code, 1,000 or more files | +12.5% accuracy with vector search”.

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** The file-count condition belongs to Cursor’s code-retention result, not its average question-answering accuracy result.

   **Source line — quoted:** V-F6, `runs/20261012-memory-caches/nodes/vector.claude.md:93`: “agent code retention increases by 0.3% … This effect increases to 2.6% on large codebases with 1,000 files or more”.

   **Fix:** Present the average accuracy gain separately from the retention gain and its file-count condition.

9. **Location: `CACHES.md:212`, Vector index, fifth why. Severity: medium.**

   **Quoted text:** “On memory-style text, the positive results found combine vectors with keyword search (`V-F6`, `V-F7`”.

   **Why it misleads — my reading:** Those findings support hybrid retrieval in other settings. They do not establish the stated result on memory-style text.

   **Source lines — quoted:** V-F6, `vector.claude.md:95`: “code (not decisions/lessons)”. V-F7, `:102`: “a 2024 chunk-RAG test with API embeddings”.

   **Fix:** Name the actual corpora and describe hybrid-only as this design’s proposed policy, supported by evidence from other settings.

10. **Location: `CACHES.md:484` and `:487`, startup examples. Severity: medium.**

    **Quoted text:** “instructions plus index under 32 KiB”; “instructions plus index under 64 KiB”.

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** These examples make instruction-loader budgets appear to govern every subsequent index read. Their applicability depends on whether the index is included in the instruction chain.

    **Source line — quoted:** CX, `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/codex.md:444`: “An ordinary subsequent file read consumes model context but is not thereby governed by the same loader cap.”

    **Fix:** State that the combined caps apply when the index is loaded through those instruction loaders. Distinguish a later file read from instruction discovery.

11. **Location: `CACHES.md:111` and `:117`, When to use which store. Severity: medium.**

    **Quoted text:** “Still unresolved”; “For memory entries all six stay open.”

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** The run record’s historical disagreements are preserved correctly, but this sentence presents every disagreement as a current design question. EJ has now selected observable comparisons, full-text before hybrid under the stated policy, and decision recording. Actual benefits and costs remain unmeasured.

    **Source lines — quoted:** B1.1, `CACHES.md:323`: “The comparison decides; no fixed size is set in advance”. B1.2, `:344`: “it is built only after B1.1 has fired”.

    **Fix:** Separate historical engine disagreements from current open evidence gaps. Mark the trigger-policy disagreement as settled by B1 and identify the remaining measurement questions individually.

12. **Location: `CACHES.md:240`, Measured here. Severity: medium.**

    **Quoted text:** “Rebuilding from scratch gave the same answers on all 180 question-route pairs.”

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** This correctly reports determinism, but the section omits the unresolved corpus-stability warning. Equal rebuild answers do not establish that the benchmark’s routes and gold answers were evaluated against one stable snapshot. The warning appears only much later in the unresolved table.

    **Source lines — quoted:** The builder, `runs/20261012-caches-routing/nodes/build.opencode.stdout.md:68`: “these numbers are a snapshot of a corpus in motion. Re-run before the tables are used.” The run recorder, `state.md:59`, records a later run “on the repaired harness”.

    **Fix:** Carry the unclosed snapshot warning beside the benchmark’s other limitations. Acknowledge the later rerun without claiming it proved snapshot stability.

13. **Location: `CACHES.md:104`, Costs. Severity: medium.**

    **Quoted text:** “an `INDEX.md` scan, under 0.01 s and minimal tokens (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`)”.

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** This sits under a “Measured” introduction, but the cited design supplies no scan timing. The number is an engine’s unsupported figure, and the original run explicitly preserved that disagreement.

    **Source line — quoted:** `runs/20261012-caches-enhance/DECISION-RULES.md:150`: “opencode: **<0.01 s** scan … codex: **time GAP and tokens GAP**”.

    **Fix:** Remove the timing or attribute it as an unmeasured estimate. Do not cite the source-of-truth design as a measurement.

14. **Location: `CACHES.md:152`, Rules for every cache. Severity: low.**

    **Quoted text:** “the same set as `INDEX.md`”.

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** As an implementation rule, this still suggests using the generated index to select cache membership. B4 was changed specifically to make entry headers authoritative. A stale generated index must not determine the cache’s membership.

    **Source line — quoted:** B4.1, `CACHES.md:373`: “The headers are the reference, not `INDEX.md`, which is generated from them”.

    **Fix:** Say membership is selected from active entry headers. Equality with a correctly generated `INDEX.md` is a consequence.

15. **Location: `CACHES.md:145`, Rules for every cache. Severity: low.**

    **Quoted text:** “compare the recorded content hash of the sources with the index … (`S-F9`”.

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** The core requirements now distinguish the hash source from the rebuild-before-answering source, but this summary still cites only S-F9 beside the hash procedure.

    **Source lines — quoted:** S-F9, `sqlite-fts.claude.md:80`: “compares the index build time with the newest source mtime”. S-F11, `:89`: “Change detection: SHA-256 on files and chunks.”

    **Fix:** Cite S-F11 for hashing and S-F9 for rebuilding before answering and temporary-file publication, as K1/K2 already do.

16. **Location: `CACHES.md:45`, `:54` and `:550`. Severity: low.**

    **Quoted text:** “Its conflict with the vision for code and docs is left to EJ”; “None of the three caches”; “any of the three stores”.

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** The scope conflict has been decided, and the live caches design contains two caches. Historical three-store benchmark descriptions remain appropriate; these current summary statements do not.

    **Source lines — quoted:** `CACHES.md:61`: proposals to widen the vision “were not adopted”. The snapshot vision, `caches-vision.md:27`: “two optional indexes”.

    **Fix:** Update the summary counts to two and mark the code-and-docs scope decision settled. Retain three-store wording only when describing historical research or benchmark evidence.

17. **Location: `CACHES.md:561`, Not known. Severity: low.**

    **Quoted text:** “All evidence is on other corpora: source code, chat memory, enterprise documents.”

    **Why it misleads — my reading:** The listed evidence now includes SEZA markdown memory and decision records. The genuine remaining gap is measurement on the active-entry corpus specified by this design.

    **Source line — quoted:** SZ, `runs/20261012-caches-numbers/nodes/seza.md:245`: “five questions taken from SEZA memory entries or docs”.

    **Fix:** Include SEZA memory among the example corpora. State the narrower gap: neither accepted comparison has been established on this design’s active `entries/` corpus.

### Unresolved table

- **BM-9 speed bound and `git ls-files` overhead — settled for the recorded bound:** removing overhead cannot increase the recorded maximum; the comparative timing bias remains a disclosed caveat, not evidence that the 15 ms observation is false.
- **Corpus snapshot stability — still open:** a repaired-harness rerun is recorded, but the opened evidence does not close the builder’s stable-snapshot warning.
- **Audit’s lower-severity findings — still open in part:** the repair was not re-audited; some items affect character output or presentation, while the “No problem found” item is already settled and must not be counted as a defect.
- **Discovery versus exhaustive memory routes — still open:** the completeness and absence rows correctly require exhaustive routes, but the structured-header and “why” rows still say “none” without distinguishing finding a candidate from reading or exhaustively enumerating authoritative entries.

### What holds

- **Vision:** two optional, derived memory indexes; EJ decides whether to build after a comparison passes.
- **Changes since review:** replacing the full question log with miss notes and comparison-time question sets is sound in principle; the dependent wording exceptions are identified above.
- **The answer first:** memory-only scope and absence of a demonstrated present need hold; the code-and-docs proposals were not adopted.
- **When to use which store, Part 1:** the B1.1 comparison replaces the size trigger; literal lookup, completeness and proof of absence retain appropriate file routes.
- **Part 2 and code-graph removal:** the operational sections moved out; historical graph evidence need not be removed merely because its subject is outside the caches scope.
- **Rules for every cache:** full rebuild, read-only opening, source freshness and active-only membership hold, subject to the summary corrections above.
- **SQLite full-text index:** the revised trigger, EJ’s build decision, one row per entry, default tokenizer and removal of stored `status` are consistent.
- **Vector index:** meaning-miss comparison, full-text non-regression, model identity and the explicitly proposed hybrid policy hold.
- **Measured here:** BM-1–BM-6 agree with the supplied results; BM-7 agrees with the runtime-recall artifact; proxy limitations and lack of an end-to-end agent trial are disclosed.
- **Core requirements:** B4 consistently removes stored `status` from both schemas while retaining header filtering and `covers`; decision records and trial-index placement hold.
- **K3:** dropping rebuild cost is consistently reflected in its operative clauses and removal of its example table; benchmark rebuild timings can remain as historical measurements.
- **Example numbers:** the scaling table, synthetic timings and SEZA’s “no verdict” preserve useful evidence when kept separate from adoption conclusions.
- **What this changes elsewhere:** retaining the existing optional `.cache/` placement is consistent; no change to `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` is proposed.
- **Not known:** quote-verification limits, missing measurements on the designed corpus and the void planted-tool check remain appropriate caveats.
- **Unresolved after review:** keeping attributed historical positions is sound, provided their current dispositions are made clear.
